The Carpe DiemCourse notes · seizes the semester
These are proper study notes written from the course slides — explanations first, then the key points, then a worked example where it helps. Each section tells you exactly which slide it came from. Open the matching question bank at the bottom of each course to test yourself.

Pharm D

?# First-Year Pharm D — Maximum-Density Study Notes from the KNUST Slides

These notes expand the first-year lecture slides using the v1.19 Way of Life standard. Every major concept is examined through six layers — Mechanistic (structural/chemical/physical why), Historical/Research (who found it, what it replaced, what is unresolved), MLS/Diagnostic (specimen, method, reference range, error, QC), Clinical/Medical (disease, failure physiology, bedside use), Pharmaceutical (named drugs at the exact point of mechanism), and Research/Frontier (what a researcher still needs to know). All six are checked for every concept; where a layer is genuinely unsupported by the slides, that is stated in one short clause rather than silently skipped. Each section opens with biological geography and physics-first framing, closes with an end-of-section synthesis, and contains at least one fully worked, arithmetic-verified example.

Accuracy labels used throughout. [slide fact] = stated in the named file and slide. [model knowledge] = established pharmacology/chemistry added by the author, not in the slides. [inference] = reasoned connection, not stated. No measurement, page number, or source is invented. The slides themselves are sometimes compressed; where they are imprecise, that is said plainly.

What Pharmacy Is and Where It Came From

Biological geography (opening). The slides for this topic are historical, so the biological zoom is thin here — that is stated rather than invented. The profession's object is the patient, and the chain a pharmacist works along is: organ system → organ → tissue → cell → protein target → binding site. [model knowledge] Take digoxin, one of the active principles isolated in the 19th century [slide fact — SLIDE 11]: made in foxglove (Digitalis) leaves → extracted as a defined molecule → compressed into a tablet → absorbed across intestinal epithelium → distributed in plasma → binds the Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase on the outside of a cardiomyocyte → raises intracellular Na⁺, which slows the Na⁺/Ca²⁺ exchanger and leaves more Ca²⁺ available to the contractile machinery → metabolised mainly in the liver and eliminated by the kidney [model knowledge]. It should not be allowed to accumulate when renal function falls — a dose-adjustment point the slides do not make [inference].

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem the profession solves is control of exposure: deliver a defined number of molecules of a defined chemical identity to a defined site at a defined rate, with bounded loss and bounded variation. A crude plant extract fails this problem because its composition varies between harvests; isolation and assay convert a distribution of outcomes into a specification. [inference from SLIDE 11]

Concept 1 — Pharmacy as a science and a practice.

  • Mechanistic. Pharmacy is defined in the slides as the science and practice concerned with discovery, development, manufacture, distribution and rational use of medicines [slide fact — SLIDE 18]. The name comes from Greek pharmakon, medicine or drug [slide fact — SLIDE 18]. The mechanism that makes this a single profession is the feedback loop between chemical identity and biological effect: change the structure and you change potency, toxicity and duration [model knowledge].
  • Historical/Research. The slides trace pharmacy from empirical natural substances to a regulated science-based profession [slide fact — SLIDE 3]. What it replaced is unsystematic herbalism; what remains unresolved is how to integrate traditional medicine into evidence-based therapeutics — the slides list "integration of traditional medicine research" as a post-independence goal, not an achieved fact [slide fact — SLIDE 16]. [model knowledge] The 20th-century shift from compounding to industrial manufacture and then to clinical pharmacy is the same story of control moving from the workshop to the standard and then to the patient.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No specimen, method, reference range or QC procedure appears in this lecture — the layer is genuinely thin and is not padded. [slide fact — no MLS content in SLIDE 3–24] [model knowledge] The diagnostic contact point is therapeutic drug monitoring: plasma digoxin or gentamicin concentrations are measured to keep exposure in range, but neither assay nor range is given here.
  • Clinical/Medical. The slides name patient-centred pharmaceutical care, medication therapy management, ward rounds and pharmacovigilance as hospital roles [slide fact — SLIDE 19]. The physiology of failure is that an unmonitored drug can cross from therapeutic to toxic without a visible change in the tablet; this is why the pharmacist checks dose before dispensing in the posology lecture [inference linked to PHARM 161 posology 2026.pptx, SLIDE 17].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named drugs at the point of isolation: morphine from opium, quinine from cinchona and digitalis from foxglove [slide fact — SLIDE 11]; penicillin as the 20th-century antibiotic breakthrough [slide fact — SLIDE 12]. [model knowledge] Morphine is an agonist at the μ-opioid receptor; quinine blocks haemozoin formation in Plasmodium; digoxin inhibits Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase — three different molecular targets reached by the same professional workflow.
  • Research/Frontier. The slides name pharmacogenomics, precision medicine, biotechnology and biologics as future trends [slide fact — SLIDE 12, 16, 23]. [inference] The open research question is how to predict, before dispensing, which patient will fall outside the population dose–response curve; the slides announce the question but provide no method.

Concept 2 — The historical chain and what each step changed.

  • Mechanistic. Mesopotamian clay tablets (c. 2600 BC) combined plant, animal and mineral substances and already separated physicians from drug preparers [slide fact — SLIDE 5]. The Ebers Papyrus (c. 1500 BC) records about 700 formulations and early dosage forms — ointments, suppositories and decoctions [slide fact — SLIDE 5]. Each new dosage form is a physical solution to a delivery problem: an ointment holds a drug on skin, a suppository avoids the stomach, a decoction extracts plant material into water [inference].
  • Historical/Research. Hippocrates (460–370 BC) separated medicine from superstition and left the Hippocratic Oath; Theophrastus systematised medicinal plants; Galen (AD 129–200) standardised complex preparations, still called galenicals [slide fact — SLIDE 7]. The Islamic Golden Age created the first pharmacies (apothecaries) in Baghdad in AD 754, with Avicenna's Canon of Medicine and Al-Razi's work in pharmaceutical chemistry [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. [model knowledge] What is unresolved historically is the degree to which Indian Ayurveda and Chinese Shen Nong Ben Cao Jing classification influenced later Islamic and European materia medica — the slides list them side by side without a transmission claim [slide fact — SLIDE 6].
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No laboratory method is given. [slide fact — SLIDE 5–12] [model knowledge] The closest modern analogue is identity testing of botanicals (macroscopic and microscopic examination), which belongs to the Cognosy slides rather than this one.
  • Clinical/Medical. The separation of physician from drug preparer is the origin of the pharmacist's independent checking role [inference from SLIDE 5]. [model knowledge] In modern terms this is the difference between a prescriber's diagnosis and a pharmacist's verification of dose, interaction and formulation.
  • Pharmaceutical. Galen's preparations are the historical ancestor of today's compounded creams and mixtures [slide fact — SLIDE 7]; distilled syrups and elixirs from the Islamic period are the ancestors of modern oral liquids [slide fact — SLIDE 10].
  • Research/Frontier. The slides give no primary literature; the frontier claim that "pharmaceutical care" and "pharmacogenomics" are 21st-century directions is asserted, not evidenced [slide fact — SLIDE 12, 23]. [inference] A researcher would need outcome trials, not a timeline, to test that claim.

Concept 3 — Ghanaian and professional orientation.

  • Mechanistic. KNUST's Faculty of Pharmacy and Pharmaceutical Sciences was established in 1961 as Ghana's first pharmacy school, moving from BPharm to PharmD in 2012 [slide fact — SLIDE 16]. Later schools followed at University of Ghana, Central University, UHAS, UCC and UDS [slide fact — SLIDE 16]. The regulatory structure — Pharmacy Council of Ghana (1994), Food and Drugs Authority (1992–1996) — is the institutional equivalent of a quality-control barrier [inference].
  • Historical/Research. Pre-colonial Ghana relied on indigenous herbal medicine and oral transmission; the colonial period brought Western practice and dispensers trained by government [slide fact — SLIDE 15]. [inference] What the slides leave unresolved is how oral transmission can be preserved while being subjected to modern identity and potency testing.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No assay or range appears in this lecture. [slide fact — SLIDE 15–16] [model knowledge] In practice the FDA and Pharmacy Council set compendial identity and dissolution tests for medicines, but those tests are not described here.
  • Clinical/Medical. Contemporary Ghanaian practice listed in the slides includes hospital pharmacy, community growth, local manufacture, herbal research and public-health pharmacy [slide fact — SLIDE 17]. [model knowledge] The bedside consequence of a weak regulatory barrier is substandard or falsified medicine, which produces treatment failure and toxicity — the slides do not discuss this directly.
  • Pharmaceutical. Professional bodies named are the Pharmaceutical Society of Ghana (1957) and the Ghana College of Pharmacists (2011) [slide fact — SLIDE 16]; core roles are drug expert, patient-care provider, researcher, educator, regulator and entrepreneur [slide fact — SLIDE 18].
  • Research/Frontier. Local pharmaceutical manufacturing and herbal medicine research are named as growth areas [slide fact — SLIDE 17]. [inference] The research question is stability and standardisation of locally produced herbal batches — a question the Pharm 172 metabolite slides later sharpen but do not answer.

Worked example — why isolation changes dose control (arithmetic verified). [model knowledge; illustrative numbers, not in slides] Suppose two harvests of the same medicinal plant contain 1.2% w/w and 0.6% w/w of a active principle respectively. Batch A: 100 g × 0.012 = 1.20 g of active principle. Batch B: 100 g × 0.006 = 0.60 g. Combined: 1.80 g in 200 g of powder → 1.80/200 = 0.009 = 0.9% w/w. Giving 10 g of the mixed powder delivers 0.09 g = 90 mg, whereas 10 g of batch A alone delivers 120 mg and batch B alone 60 mg — a twofold spread from the same "dose" of plant material. This is the physical reason the 19th-century isolation of morphine, quinine and digitalis mattered [inference from SLIDE 11].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Pharmacy evolved from empirical substance use to a regulated science whose job is to make exposure predictable.
  • Mechanistic Story. Identity and purity control shrink the variance between one dose and the next.
  • Clinical Story. The checking role exists because therapeutic and toxic doses can look identical in the container.
  • MLS Story. Thin in this lecture; diagnostic drug monitoring belongs elsewhere in the course.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Morphine, quinine, digitalis and penicillin mark the shift from botanicals to defined molecules.
  • Biotech Story. Biologics and biotechnology are named as 21st-century trends [slide fact — SLIDE 12]; recombinant insulin would be the canonical example [model knowledge].
  • Manufacturing Story. GMP and regulatory frameworks (FDA, EMA) are named [slide fact — SLIDE 12]; the slides do not describe a production line.
  • Computational Story. Pharmacogenomics and bioinformatics are listed [slide fact — SLIDE 12, 16]; no dataset is given.
  • Evolutionary Story. Plants did not evolve alkaloids for human therapy; pharmacy co-opted existing defence chemistry [inference, developed in the metabolites section].
  • Innovation Story. Pharmaceutical care and expanded clinical roles are presented as service innovations [slide fact — SLIDE 23].

Source: Introduction to Pharmacy.pptx — SLIDE 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 23

Pharmaceutical Chemistry — The Science Behind Medicines

Biological geography (opening). Chemistry is the study of matter — composition, structure, properties and the changes it undergoes in reactions [slide fact — Lecture 1, SLIDE 4]. The biological zoom for a drug molecule is: administered at a mucosal or skin surface → crosses intestinal epithelium (or avoids it by injection) → enters portal blood → passes the liver → reaches systemic circulation → partitions into tissue → binds a receptor or enzyme active site → is chemically altered by metabolic enzymes → is excreted. [model knowledge] A molecule such as paracetamol should appear in urine mainly as glucuronide and sulfate conjugates, not as unchanged parent drug; finding large amounts of parent drug would imply impaired metabolism or overdose [model knowledge, not in these slides].

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem is solubility versus permeability: a drug must dissolve in aqueous gut fluid yet cross a lipid membrane, and these requirements pull in opposite directions. [model knowledge] Pharmaceutical chemistry is the discipline that resolves this by changing ionisation, salt form, particle size or formulation [inference from SLIDE 5–9].

Concept 1 — Definition, scope and the "bridge" claim.

  • Mechanistic. Pharmaceutical chemistry applies chemical principles to the discovery, development and analysis of drugs, creating biologically active molecules that treat, prevent or diagnose disease [slide fact — SLIDE 5, 6]. The mechanism of the bridge claim is structure–activity: the same atoms arranged differently produce different receptor affinity and different metabolism [model knowledge].
  • Historical/Research. The slides present chemistry as the "defining science of pharmacy" and pharmaceutical chemistry as its specialised application [slide fact — SLIDE 5]. [model knowledge] Historically this specialisation grew out of natural-product isolation and synthetic dye chemistry; what is unresolved is how far computational prediction can replace laboratory synthesis — the slides list computational chemistry as a key area but give no success rate [slide fact — SLIDE 11].
  • MLS/Diagnostic. The slides connect the field to quality control and impurity identification [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. [model knowledge] Specimen = finished dosage form or dissolution sample; method = HPLC or UV spectroscopy; reference range = pharmacopoeial assay limits. None of these methods or limits is stated here, so they are added as model knowledge, not attributed to the slide.
  • Clinical/Medical. The slides claim the field supports patient-specific decisions, mechanism-of-action studies, dose control and patient safety through quality control, stability and compatibility [slide fact — SLIDE 13]. The physiology of failure is that an impurity or a degraded molecule has a different receptor profile, so a "same-looking" tablet can behave differently [inference].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: aspirin (acetylsalicylic acid) irreversibly acetylates cyclooxygenase; digoxin inhibits Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase; omeprazole is a proton-pump inhibitor whose activation depends on gastric acidity [model knowledge — used only to illustrate the structure–property link the slides describe at SLIDE 9].
  • Research/Frontier. Natural product chemistry is defined as extracting bioactive compounds from plants or animals to serve as drug leads [slide fact — SLIDE 11]. [inference] The frontier question is how to keep a natural-product lead soluble and stable once it leaves the plant; the slides do not address developability.

Concept 2 — What must be known about a medicine.

  • Mechanistic. The slides list synthesis, purity determination, formulation, dosage, absorption and distribution, drug–receptor interaction, metabolism and elimination [slide fact — SLIDE 8, 9]. These are not a checklist but a chain: a synthetic impurity changes purity; purity changes dose; formulation changes absorption; absorption changes receptor exposure; metabolism changes elimination [inference].
  • Historical/Research. Structure–Activity Relationship (SAR) is defined as understanding how chemical structure influences properties and behaviour in the body [slide fact — SLIDE 9]. [model knowledge] SAR grew from the observation that congeneric series show graded potency; what remains unresolved is the contribution of protein flexibility to binding, which static structures under-represent.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No assay is described. [slide fact — SLIDE 8, 9] [model knowledge] The nearest laboratory analogue is content uniformity testing, where several tablets are assayed individually to confirm each lies within limits; this is not stated in the lecture.
  • Clinical/Medical. Dose control through pKa, molarity and ionisation is named as a practice-relevant use [slide fact — SLIDE 13]. [model knowledge] A weak acid with low pKa is largely ionised at blood pH, which keeps it in plasma and limits central nervous system entry — the reason Morphine's glucuronide metabolites are less brain-penetrant than the parent [model knowledge; not in slides].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of mechanism: lidocaine is a weak base used as a local anaesthetic; its unionised fraction crosses the nerve membrane and the ionised fraction blocks voltage-gated Na⁺ channels from inside [model knowledge] — an example of the pKa/ionisation principle the slides name but do not work through [slide fact — SLIDE 13].
  • Research/Frontier. ADME is defined as absorption, distribution, metabolism and excretion [slide fact — SLIDE 11]. [inference] A researcher still needs transporter-level data (uptake and efflux), which the four-letter acronym hides.

Concept 3 — The five key areas and their boundaries.

  • Mechanistic. Medicinal chemistry designs and optimises lead compounds; analytical chemistry handles quality control and impurities; computational chemistry models molecules before lab testing; pharmacokinetics and metabolism study ADME; natural product chemistry extracts leads from plants and animals [slide fact — SLIDE 10, 11]. Each area attacks a different failure mode: wrong target, wrong purity, wrong prediction, wrong exposure, wrong source [inference].
  • Historical/Research. The slides do not name founders. [slide fact — SLIDE 10, 11] [model knowledge] Computational chemistry historically grew from quantum chemistry and molecular graphics; the unresolved issue is scoring-function accuracy for flexible proteins.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Analytical pharmaceutical chemistry is the only area with a direct diagnostic analogue in the slides — identifying impurities [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. [model knowledge] Specimen = drug substance; method = chromatography with a detector; sources of error = co-elution and column degradation; QC relevance = a failed impurity test blocks release.
  • Clinical/Medical. Compatibility checking when drugs are mixed is listed under patient safety [slide fact — SLIDE 13]; the detailed chemistry of that is the Incompatibilities section.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: parabens are antimicrobial preservatives whose activity depends on the unionised fraction, so formulation pH changes their effectiveness [model knowledge; link to Emulsions section].
  • Research/Frontier. The slides assert the interdisciplinary nature of the field but give no example of a successful prediction replacing an experiment [slide fact — SLIDE 7, 11]. [inference] That evidence is what a researcher would need next.

Worked example — ionisation ratio (arithmetic verified). [model knowledge formula; illustrative values, not in slides] For a weak acid, Henderson–Hasselbalch gives A⁻/HA = 10^(pH − pKa). Take an acid with pKa 4.4 in plasma at pH 7.4: exponent = 7.4 − 4.4 = 3.0, so A⁻/HA = 10³ = 1000. Fraction ionised = 1000/(1000 + 1) = 0.999, about 99.9%. In gastric lumen at pH 1.4: exponent = 1.4 − 4.4 = −3, A⁻/HA = 0.001, fraction ionised ≈ 0.1%. [inference] This 1000-fold shift is the physical basis for the slides' statement that ionisation influences absorption and dose [slide fact — SLIDE 13]; the numbers are illustrative, not slide-derived.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Chemistry is not background science here; it is the mechanism by which dose, purity and effect are made predictable.
  • Mechanistic Story. Structure → ionisation → solubility → absorption → receptor binding → metabolism → excretion.
  • Clinical Story. A change in chemical form can change safety without changing appearance.
  • MLS Story. Impurity identification is the diagnostic face of the field, but the lecture names no method or limit.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Lidocaine, aspirin, digoxin and omeprazole each link a chemical property to a clinical use.
  • Biotech Story. Biologics enlarge the definition of "molecule" beyond small synthetic compounds [slide fact — Introduction to Pharmacy.pptx, SLIDE 12].
  • Manufacturing Story. Purity and stability determine whether a batch can be released [slide fact — SLIDE 10, 13].
  • Computational Story. Molecular modelling is explicitly listed [slide fact — SLIDE 11].
  • Evolutionary Story. Natural products are evolved molecules co-opted as leads [inference; see metabolites section].
  • Innovation Story. Little here; the field is presented as infrastructure rather than a product [inference].

Source: Lecture 1- Introduction to Pharmaceutical Chemistry.pdf — SLIDE 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 13

Atomic Structure and Chemical Bonding

Biological geography (opening). The lecture begins with orbitals and ends with molecular shape [slide fact — SLIDE 2–78]. The biological zoom: an ion or drug reaches a protein binding pocket → the pocket presents charged and polar groups → the ligand is held by ionic, hydrogen-bonding and dispersion forces → the protein changes shape → a physiological response follows. [model knowledge] Sodium sits mainly in extracellular fluid and potassium inside cells; the Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase maintains that separation using ATP [model knowledge, not in this lecture]. A potassium ion should not be high in plasma — hyperkalaemia is a medical emergency because it depolarises myocardium [model knowledge].

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem an ionic solid solves is how to hold oppositely charged ions together despite the energy cost of making them. The lecture's own numbers show the problem: removing an electron from sodium costs 495 kJ/mol, and giving it to chlorine returns only 349 kJ/mol, leaving +290 kJ unfavourable before the lattice forms [slide fact — SLIDE 29, 30]. [inference] The lattice energy is then the compensating term.

Concept 1 — Orbital filling rules.

  • Mechanistic. The Aufbau principle puts each electron into the lowest-energy available orbital; orbitals of equal energy, such as the three 2p orbitals, are degenerate [slide fact — SLIDE 4]. Pauli exclusion allows at most two electrons per orbital and only with opposite spins, "spin up" and "spin down" [slide fact — SLIDE 5]. Hund's rule requires singly occupied degenerate orbitals to share the same spin before pairing occurs; nitrogen is 1s² 2s² 2p³ [slide fact — SLIDE 6]. These are not conventions: they follow from the quantum-mechanical description of electrons as Fermions and from Coulomb repulsion [model knowledge].
  • Historical/Research. The slides attribute the periodic table to Mendeleev, who arranged elements by atomic mass and predicted unknown elements from their neighbours, and to Moseley, who showed periodicity follows atomic number [slide fact — SLIDE 10, 11]. [model knowledge] Aufbau, Pauli and Hund came later from quantum theory; what is unresolved in introductory teaching is that the Aufbau order is a rule of thumb which fails for several heavier elements.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No specimen or reference range appears in this lecture — the layer is thin. [slide fact — SLIDE 2–8] [model knowledge] Electron-paramagnetic resonance could in principle observe unpaired electrons, but this is not part of the slides or of routine pharmacy practice.
  • Clinical/Medical. The clinical connection is indirect: unpaired electrons and transition-metal d-orbital occupancy explain why some ions are paramagnetic and biologically active [inference, expanded in the bio-inorganic section]. The slides themselves make no clinical claim here.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: gadolinium-based MRI contrast agents exploit unpaired electrons to alter relaxation of nearby water protons [model knowledge] — an application of the unpaired-electron concept, not stated in the slides.
  • Research/Frontier. The lecture gives no open question. [slide fact] [inference] A researcher would ask how to predict orbital energies in a protein environment, where the vacuum ordering used here no longer holds.

Concept 2 — Electronic configuration and the d⁴/d⁹ exception.

  • Mechanistic. In arsenic's 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 4s² 3d¹⁰ 4p³, the leading integer is the principal quantum number n, the letter identifies the sub-orbital and the superscript counts its electrons [slide fact — SLIDE 7]. A configuration ending in d⁴ or d⁹ is relatively unstable; chromium moves one 4s electron into 3d, becoming 4s¹ 3d⁵ [slide fact — SLIDE 8]. [model knowledge] Half-filled and fully filled d subshells gain exchange stabilisation, which outweighs the cost of promotion.
  • Historical/Research. No discoverer is named for this exception in the slides [slide fact — SLIDE 8]. [model knowledge] It is a standard correction to the naive Aufbau order; what remains disputed is how well simple exchange arguments predict the configurations of the heaviest transition metals.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Not supported by the slides. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Oxidation-state interpretation of metal-containing drugs is a laboratory concern, not orbital bookkeeping itself.
  • Clinical/Medical. The exception matters biologically because chromium and copper oxidation chemistry depends on d-electron occupancy [inference, developed in section 4]. The slides do not make this link.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: copper(I) versus copper(II) compounds differ in reactivity because of d⁹ versus d¹⁰ configuration [model knowledge] — the slides name both oxidation states for copper in Lecture 2.
  • Research/Frontier. The slides provide no frontier statement. [slide fact] [inference] The open question is the exact energetic balance in solution, where ligand binding changes the ordering.

Concept 3 — Ionic versus covalent bonding and electronegativity.

  • Mechanistic. An ionic bond is the electrostatic attraction between ions formed by electron transfer between atoms of very different electronegativity; examples given are NaCl, CaCl₂ and K₂O, with high melting points and conductivity [slide fact — SLIDE 23, 25]. A covalent bond shares electron pairs between nonmetals of similar electronegativity; examples are O₂, CO₂, C₂H₆, H₂O and SiC, which do not conduct [slide fact — SLIDE 35, 36]. Electronegativity is defined as the ability of an atom in a molecule to attract electrons to itself [slide fact — SLIDE 42]. The greater the difference, the more polar the bond [slide fact — SLIDE 45], and a bond dipole has moment μ = Qr measured in debyes [slide fact — SLIDE 44].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no discoverer for electronegativity. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The concept is usually attributed to Pauling; what is unresolved is that several competing scales rank elements differently, so "polar" is not a single number without a chosen scale.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Conductivity is the laboratory test that distinguishes an ionic from a covalent dispersed system, and the emulsions lecture later uses exactly that test [slide fact — PHARM 162 PHARMACEUTICAL EMULSIONS (4).pptx, SLIDE 18]. Source of error: a w/o emulsion with dissolved salts can conduct misleadingly [model knowledge].
  • Clinical/Medical. Water's polarity is why it is a good solvent for salts and why intravenous fluids must be isotonic [inference from SLIDE 43]. [model knowledge] Haemolysis occurs when a hypotonic solution creates an osmotic gradient across the erythrocyte membrane.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: sodium chloride 0.9% infusion supplies Na⁺ and Cl⁻ as hydrated ions; diazepam is poorly soluble in water and is therefore formulated as an emulsion [model knowledge; emulsion example is in the emulsions slide set].
  • Research/Frontier. The slides assert that lattice energy increases with charge and decreases with ion size [slide fact — SLIDE 34]. [inference] A researcher would need to account for polarisation and partial covalency, which the simple Coulomb model ignores.

Concept 4 — Hybridisation and bond energetics.

  • Mechanistic. Carbon's 2s and 2p orbitals combine into four equal sp³ orbitals; because electrons repel, these point to the corners of a tetrahedron at 109.5° [slide fact — SLIDE 65, 66]. In alkenes, one s and two p orbitals form three sp² orbitals at 120°, leaving an unhybridised p orbital to overlap sideways and form a π bond [slide fact — SLIDE 70, 71, 73]. A σ bond comes from end-on overlap and allows free rotation; a π bond comes from sideways overlap and does not [slide fact — SLIDE 68].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no attribution for hybridisation theory. [slide fact] [model knowledge] It was introduced to reconcile carbon's observed tetravalence with its ground-state electron configuration; what remains interpretive is whether hybrid orbitals are physical objects or a convenient basis transformation.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Bond length can be inferred from diffraction methods [model knowledge]; the slides give lengths but not a method [slide fact — SLIDE 74]. This is a stated limitation, not a hidden one.
  • Clinical/Medical. The restricted rotation of a π bond underlies cis/trans isomerism, which changes receptor fit for many drugs [model knowledge] — an inference from the slides' geometry, not stated there.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: cisplatin's square-planar geometry is required for its crosslinking of DNA; the trans isomer is much less active [model knowledge].
  • Research/Frontier. The slides do not discuss limitations. [slide fact] [inference] A researcher modelling drug–receptor binding still needs accurate π-system electrostatics, which a simple hybridisation picture does not supply.

Worked example — the ionic bond energy account (arithmetic verified). [slide fact — SLIDE 29, 30, 31, 32] For 2 Na(s) + Cl₂(g) → 2 NaCl(s): ionisation costs 495 kJ/mol × 2 = 990 kJ. Electron addition to chlorine returns 349 kJ/mol × 2 = 698 kJ (the slide rounds this to 700 kJ). Net before lattice formation = 990 − 698 = +292 kJ (the slide reports +290 kJ using its rounding). [inference] For the crystal to be stable, the lattice energy released when gaseous Na⁺ and Cl⁻ form the solid must exceed 290 kJ for this two-mole reaction. [model knowledge] The experimental lattice enthalpy of NaCl is about 787 kJ/mol, i.e., about 1574 kJ for two moles, which comfortably outweighs the cost. The slide's +290 kJ is therefore the deficit, not the final energy of the reaction — a point worth stating explicitly because the slide stops before the conclusion.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Electron arrangement determines charge, shape and interaction strength; those three determine pharmacology.
  • Mechanistic Story. Fill rules → configuration → orbital hybridisation → σ/π framework → molecular shape → binding.
  • Clinical Story. Ion gradients and molecular geometry are the physical substrate of excitability and drug action.
  • MLS Story. Conductivity distinguishes ionic from covalent dispersed systems; bond lengths are given without a measurement method.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Contrast agents, local anaesthetics and platinum drugs are named anchors.
  • Biotech Story. Protein engineering depends on reproducing these interactions in a designed binding site [model knowledge].
  • Manufacturing Story. Crystal form and salt selection are manufacturing decisions built on lattice energy [model knowledge].
  • Computational Story. Molecular modelling is the computational extension of hybridisation and electrostatics [model knowledge].
  • Evolutionary Story. biology exploits existing ion gradients rather than inventing new energy sources [inference].
  • Innovation Story. Little explicit content; the innovation lies in applying orbital concepts to dosage-form design [inference].

Source: ATOMIC STRUCTURE_curr.pptx — SLIDE 2, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 11, 23, 25, 29, 30, 31, 32, 34, 35, 36, 42, 43, 44, 45, 65, 66, 68, 70, 71, 73, 74

Metals in the Body and Inorganic Medicines

Biological geography (opening). Bio-inorganic chemistry is the study of the roles of inorganic elements in biological processes [slide fact — Lecture 2, SLIDE 5]. Zoom: body fluid compartment → organ → tissue → cell → organelle → metalloprotein → metal active site. [model knowledge] Iron sits in the porphyrin ring of haemoglobin in erythrocytes; zinc sits in carbonic anhydrase in renal tubular cells; calcium is stored in bone hydroxyapatite and signals in cytosol; sodium is the dominant extracellular cation. [model knowledge, not in slides] A transition metal should not be freely available in plasma — it is carried by transferrin, ceruloplasmin or albumin because free iron catalyses radical formation [model knowledge].

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem transition metals solve is redox versatility: a metal that can switch between two oxidation states can hand an electron to one substrate and take one from another without being consumed. [model knowledge] The slides state that copper has +1 and +2 and that this makes d-block metals good catalysts [slide fact — SLIDE 12]. The second physical problem is charge balance: replacing a divalent cation with a monovalent one changes osmotic and electrical consequences, which is why electrolyte compositions are specified in milliequivalents per litre [slide fact — SLIDE 24].

Concept 1 — Biologically important elements and transition metals.

  • Mechanistic. Bulk biological elements are H, C, N, O, P, S and Cl, present as phosphate, sulfate and chloride; bulk metal ion nutrients are Na, Mg, K and Ca [slide fact — SLIDE 7]. Essential transition metals listed are Cr, Mn, Fe, Co, Ni, Cu and Zn, with B, F, Se and I as non-metal essentials [slide fact — SLIDE 7]. Transition metals are groups 3–12 in the d-block and have two or more oxidation states [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. Their general properties include high melting points, several oxidation states and coloured compounds; they absorb light, red at low energy and violet at higher energy [slide fact — SLIDE 11]. [model knowledge] Colour arises because d-orbital splitting absorbs photons whose energy matches the gap.
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no discoverer for biological trace elements. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The recognition of trace elements as essential grew from deficiency diseases in animals and humans; what remains unresolved is the exact mechanism of nickel and chromium in human physiology, which is less settled than iron or zinc.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. The slides give no specimen or range for trace metals. [slide fact — SLIDE 7] [model knowledge] In practice serum iron, transferrin saturation and plasma zinc are measured; reference intervals are laboratory-specific and are not supplied here, so none is stated.
  • Clinical/Medical. Imbalance of body electrolytes results in malfunctioning of the body system [slide fact — SLIDE 20]. [model knowledge] Iron deficiency anaemia reduces oxygen carrying capacity; zinc deficiency impairs wound healing; copper deficiency affects connective tissue. None of these diseases is named in the lecture.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: ferrous sulfate is used orally for iron deficiency; zinc sulfate lozenges are used in zinc deficiency; selenium sulfide is used in dandruff shampoo [model knowledge] — the last is actually named in the slides as SeS₂ 2.5% w/v suspension, Selsun shampoo [slide fact — SLIDE 47].
  • Research/Frontier. The slides give no frontier. [slide fact] [inference] A researcher would need speciation data — the biological effect of Cr(III) differs from Cr(VI), and "chromium" alone is insufficient.

Concept 2 — Oxidation state as an electron-accounting tool.

  • Mechanistic. Oxidation state is the number of electrons an atom loses, gains or appears to use when joining another atom, and it determines the ability to oxidise or reduce other species [slide fact — SLIDE 14]. Three rules are given: a free element is 0; for an ion the state equals the charge, so Fe³⁺ is +3; in a neutral compound the states sum to zero, so in FeCl₃ with three Cl at −1, Fe is +3 [slide fact — SLIDE 16]. In MnO₄⁻, four oxygens at −2 contribute −8 and the ion carries −1, so manganese must be +7 [slide fact — SLIDE 17]. [model knowledge] This is bookkeeping, not a measured charge: it assigns electrons to the more electronegative atom.
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no attribution. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The convention grew from early electrochemistry; what is unresolved pedagogically is that oxidation state can be fractional in mixed-valence compounds such as Fe₃O₄, which the slides do not mention.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No assay is given. [slide fact — SLIDE 14–18] [model knowledge] Redox titrations using permanganate are a classical analytical method; the endpoint is self-indicating because MnO₄⁻ is purple and Mn²⁺ is nearly colourless [model knowledge].
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] High oxidation-state manganese species are strong oxidants and therefore toxic; the body handles manganese mainly through binding proteins. The slides make no clinical claim here.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: potassium permanganate is used as a topical oxidising antiseptic [slide fact — SLIDE 44, 45]; hydrogen peroxide is used as a wound cleanser because released oxygen mechanically dislodges bacteria [slide fact — SLIDE 42].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The open question is how to predict redox behaviour inside a protein pocket, where the dielectric environment shifts potentials substantially from aqueous values.

Concept 3 — Electrolytes and replacement fluids.

  • Mechanistic. Electrolytes are minerals with an electrical charge, found in blood, urine and tissues, that help balance water and pH [slide fact — SLIDE 20]. Significant ones named are sodium, potassium, chloride, magnesium, calcium, phosphate and bicarbonate [slide fact — SLIDE 20]. Average adult water turnover is 30–35 mL/kg/day, about 2.4 L/day [slide fact — SLIDE 21]. Replacement fluids listed are glucose, normal saline, Ringer's lactate and oral rehydration salt [slide fact — SLIDE 22]. The slides give a table of ionic contents in mEq/L, including normal saline 154 Na⁺ and 154 Cl⁻, and Ringer's lactate 130 Na⁺, 4 K⁺, 109 Cl⁻, 28 HCO₃⁻ [slide fact — SLIDE 24].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no discoverer of ORS. [slide fact] [model knowledge] ORS was developed from the discovery that glucose-coupled sodium transport in the intestinal brush border drives water absorption even in cholera; what is unresolved is optimal formulation for malnourished patients.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Plasma sodium and potassium are among the most frequently requested urgent tests [model knowledge]; no reference range is given in the slides, so none is invented here. [inference] Sources of error include haemolysis, which falsely raises plasma potassium [model knowledge].
  • Clinical/Medical. Indications for IV therapy given in the slides are to provide water, electrolytes and nutrients for daily requirements; to replace water and correct deficits; and to administer medicines and blood products [slide fact — SLIDE 23]. [model knowledge] The physiology of failure is that loss of isotonic fluid reduces circulating volume without changing osmolality, while loss of hypotonic fluid raises osmolality and triggers thirst and ADH release.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: normal saline is 0.9% w/v sodium chloride [inference from the 154 mEq/L figure, SLIDE 24]; Ringer's lactate contains lactate as a bicarbonate precursor [model knowledge]; WHO oral rehydration salt is a defined powder for reconstitution [slide fact — SLIDE 25].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] A researcher still needs to know the transport kinetics of the sodium–glucose cotransporter in different disease states; the slides state the composition but not the mechanism.

Concept 4 — Inorganic compounds as gastrointestinal, topical and dental agents.

  • Mechanistic. Antacids are alkaline bases that neutralise excess gastric hydrochloric acid, which the slides give as 0.155 M, and can inactivate pepsin [slide fact — SLIDE 27, 28]. Calcium carbonate reacts CaCO₃ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + H₂CO₃, with H₂CO₃ → H₂O + CO₂; magnesium hydroxide gives MgCl₂ + 2H₂O; aluminium hydroxide gives AlCl₃ + 3H₂O; sodium bicarbonate gives NaCl + H₂CO₃ [slide fact — SLIDE 29]. Combination products balance the constipating effect of calcium and aluminium against the laxative effect of magnesium, and simethicone is included as a defoaming agent [slide fact — SLIDE 30]. Laxatives are classified by mechanism into saline, stimulant, lubricant and stool-softener types [slide fact — SLIDE 32, 33]. Adsorbents coat the GI wall and bind toxins for elimination [slide fact — SLIDE 37]. Topical agents are protective or antimicrobial; the antimicrobial group is subdivided into oxidising and protein-precipitant agents [slide fact — SLIDE 40, 41].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no discovery dates. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The move from single-salt antacids to combination products was driven by side-effect management, not by greater acid-neutralising capacity — an inference consistent with the slide's own rationale [slide fact — SLIDE 30].
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No gastric pH measurement is described. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Twenty-four-hour pH monitoring is the clinical test for reflux; the 0.155 M figure on the slide is a textbook concentration, not a measured patient value.
  • Clinical/Medical. Excess acid produces heartburn and possible ulceration of oesophagus, stomach or duodenum [slide fact — SLIDE 28]. [model knowledge] H₂ receptor antagonists and proton-pump inhibitors act upstream by reducing acid secretion, whereas antacids neutralise acid already present — a distinction the slides do not make.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named products and salts: Tums and Rennies (calcium carbonate), Milk of Magnesia (magnesium hydroxide), Maalox and Mylanta (aluminium/magnesium hydroxides), Alka-Seltzer (sodium bicarbonate), Kaolin and activated charcoal as adsorbents, AgNO₃ 0.5–1.0% w/v for burn dressings, sodium thiosulfate for ringworm, and selenium disulfide 2.5% w/v as Selsun shampoo [slide fact — SLIDE 29, 30, 38, 46, 47].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The unresolved question is how to keep an antacid's neutralising capacity high while reducing aluminium and magnesium load in renal impairment; the slides present the combination approach but not this limitation.

Worked example — WHO ORS composition check (arithmetic verified). [slide fact — SLIDE 25] The slide lists sodium chloride 2.6 g/L, glucose anhydrous 13.5 g/L, potassium chloride 1.5 g/L and trisodium citrate dihydrate 2.9 g/L, with a stated total of 20.5 g/L. Sum: 2.6 + 13.5 = 16.1; 16.1 + 1.5 = 17.6; 17.6 + 2.9 = 20.5 g/L — the total is internally consistent. Percentage check: 2.6/20.5 × 100 = 12.683%; 13.5/20.5 × 100 = 65.854%; 1.5/20.5 × 100 = 7.317%; 2.9/20.5 × 100 = 14.146%. Sum = 100.000%. [inference] The glucose fraction is two-thirds of the powder by mass, which is consistent with the glucose-coupled sodium absorption mechanism noted above [model knowledge].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Inorganic elements do structural work, signalling work and catalytic work; pharmacy uses the same chemistry as medicines.
  • Mechanistic Story. Oxidation-state flexibility → catalysis; ionic charge → osmotic balance; acid–base stoichiometry → neutralisation.
  • Clinical Story. Electrolyte imbalance, acidosis and constipation or diarrhoea are the clinical faces of this chemistry.
  • MLS Story. The slide's electrolyte table is a formulation table, not a patient reference range; no range is supplied.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Tums, Milk of Magnesia, Maalox, Kaolin, AgNO₃ and Selsun are named at their points of action.
  • Biotech Story. Metalloenzymes are engineering targets for designer catalysts [model knowledge].
  • Manufacturing Story. ORS is a powder blend whose mass fractions must be accurate; the worked example is a batch-check calculation.
  • Computational Story. Predicting metal-site geometry in proteins remains a modelling challenge [model knowledge].
  • Evolutionary Story. Biology selected abundant, redox-flexible metals — iron and copper — and tightly binds them to avoid uncontrolled oxidation [inference].
  • Innovation Story. Combination antacids are a formulation-level innovation driven by tolerability [slide fact — SLIDE 30].

Source: Lecture 2 Bio-inorganic Chemistry.pdf — SLIDE 5, 7, 10, 11, 12, 14, 16, 17, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24, 25, 27, 28, 29, 30, 32, 33, 37, 38, 40, 41, 42, 44, 45, 46, 47, 48

Posology — Choosing and Calculating Doses

Biological geography (opening). Posology is defined from Greek posos, "how much", and logos, "science", and is the branch of medicine dealing with doses; a dose is the quantity of medicine prescribed to be taken at one time [slide fact — PHARM 161 posology 2026.pptx, SLIDE 4, 5]. Zoom: oral dose → GI lumen → intestinal epithelial cell with its efflux transporter P-glycoprotein and metabolic enzyme CYP3A4 → portal vein → hepatocyte → systemic circulation → target tissue → receptor [model knowledge, not in slides]. An injected dose bypasses the gut and liver first pass [model knowledge]. A drug should not appear in breast milk or cerebrospinal fluid unless that is intended; the slides do not discuss either compartment.

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem is exposure control under uncertainty: the same nominal dose produces different plasma concentrations because absorption, distribution and clearance vary. [model knowledge] Dose rules are control algorithms that reduce variance using a measurable surrogate — age, weight or surface area [inference from SLIDE 18–26].

Concept 1 — What changes a dose.

  • Mechanistic. The slides list genetic makeup, acquired tolerance, idiosyncrasy, route of administration, rate of elimination, drug interactions and formulation [slide fact — SLIDE 6]. Genetic differences can mean an enzyme is absent or overactive; G6PD deficiency and alcohol tolerance are the examples given [slide fact — SLIDE 7]. Tolerance means a person can take, with comparative safety, doses that would harm a normal person — morphine, heroin and cocaine are named [slide fact — SLIDE 8]. Idiosyncrasy is exceptional individual intolerance: aspirin causing asthma, penicillin causing an irritating rash, barbiturates causing a measles-like eruption [slide fact — SLIDE 9].
  • Historical/Research. No discoverer is named. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The concept of idiosyncrasy predates pharmacogenetics; what is unresolved is how much of "idiosyncratic" reaction is now explicable by HLA variants and metabolic phenotypes — a question the slides anticipate but do not answer.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No laboratory test or range is given in this lecture. [slide fact] [model knowledge] G6PD deficiency is screened by an enzyme activity assay; the slides name the deficiency but provide no method or cutoff, so none is stated here.
  • Clinical/Medical. A drug interaction may be synergistic and require dose reduction of one or both drugs, or may be dangerous [slide fact — SLIDE 12]. The physiology of failure is that two drugs sharing a clearance pathway compete, raising exposure [model knowledge].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of mechanism: two blood thinners that dissolve clots taken together can cause fatal bleeding [slide fact — SLIDE 12]; the slides point to the BNF and BPC for interaction lists [slide fact — SLIDE 12]. Warfarin and aspirin would be the modern named pair [model knowledge].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The open question is which genetic tests are worth running before the first dose; the slides announce genetic variability but give no testing strategy.

Concept 2 — Route and elimination.

  • Mechanistic. Absorption is more rapid and complete by the parenteral route than by mouth, so oral doses of most medicines are greater than parenteral doses [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. First-pass metabolism by the liver is named as the reason [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. Rate of elimination affects duration of activity; age or disease can impair liver function and delay breakdown, and reduced kidney activity causes incomplete excretion [slide fact — SLIDE 11].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no history of first-pass metabolism. [slide fact] [model knowledge] It was established through portal-vein versus peripheral-vein dosing studies; what remains unresolved is the contribution of gut-wall metabolism versus hepatic metabolism for individual drugs.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. The lecture does not provide renal or hepatic function tests. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Dose adjustment in practice uses creatinine clearance; the slides say "age and disease" only, so any numeric threshold would be invented and is not given.
  • Clinical/Medical. A prolonged or exaggerated effect may cause toxic reactions [slide fact — SLIDE 11]. [model knowledge] In renal failure, a renally cleared drug such as gentamicin accumulates and produces ototoxicity and nephrotoxicity — named as model knowledge because the slides do not name it.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: morphine has higher oral than parenteral dose because of first-pass metabolism [model knowledge]; the slide's own rule is generic [slide fact — SLIDE 10].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] A researcher needs transporter-expression data along the gut, not just an average bioavailability figure.

Concept 3 — Expression of frequency and prescriber authority.

  • Mechanistic. Dose frequency can be given as an adult range for a stated route — oral, subcutaneous or intravenous — or as an amount suitable over 24 hours, with a statement that it be given in divided doses, as in griseofulvin 1 g daily in divided doses [slide fact — SLIDE 14]. Where no 24-hour amount is stated, the quantity is usually for one occasion, as in sodium bicarbonate 5 g [slide fact — SLIDE 15].
  • Historical/Research. Official doses are not binding on a prescriber; doses above the official maxima are sometimes required and are usually underlined, circled or initialled [slide fact — SLIDE 16]. [inference] This convention is a paper-era control that is now replaced by electronic prescribing alerts [model knowledge].
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No assay is named. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Dosage reconciliation — comparing the medication list with the prescription — is the practical checking method, but the slides only say the pharmacist should check each dose [slide fact — SLIDE 17].
  • Clinical/Medical. It is the pharmacist's duty to ensure that an overdose has not been prescribed [slide fact — SLIDE 16]. [inference] This is a hard control because the same drug may be legitimately dosed differently in different patients.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of checking: sodium bicarbonate 5 g and griseofulvin 1 g are the slide's own examples [slide fact — SLIDE 14, 15].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The unresolved question is how much of dose verification can be automated without alert fatigue; the slides give the human-checking rule only.

Concept 4 — Paediatric scaling.

  • Mechanistic. Children's doses may be proportional to age, body weight or surface area [slide fact — SLIDE 18]. Young's rule uses age/(age + 12); Dilling's rule uses age/20 for ages 4 to 20; Fried's rule uses age in months/150 for infants up to 2 years [slide fact — SLIDE 20, 21, 22]. Surface-area dosing uses child surface area divided by 1.73 m² times the adult dose [slide fact — SLIDE 26]. [model knowledge] Surface area tracks metabolic rate, lean mass and extracellular fluid volume better than age or weight, which is the rationale the slide gives [slide fact — SLIDE 25].
  • Historical/Research. These are empirical rules named after their authors. [slide fact — SLIDE 19–22] [model knowledge] All three are now largely superseded by weight-based mg/kg dosing; the slides do not say so, which is an important limitation to state rather than silently repeat them as current best practice.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No growth chart, body-surface-area nomogram or laboratory range appears. [slide fact — SLIDE 18–26] [model knowledge] A nomogram or the Mosteller formula would supply the surface area; the slide supplies only the 1.73 m² adult reference [slide fact — SLIDE 26].
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] The failure mode of age-based rules is that children of the same age differ greatly in weight, so a thin child may be overdosed and an obese child underdosed; the slides do not discuss this.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of calculation: artesunate 4 mg/kg and amodiaquine 10 mg/kg for uncomplicated malaria, taken twice daily for 3 days [slide fact — SLIDE 24].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The research need is a paediatric formulation that delivers small doses accurately; the slides assume a divisible adult dose.

Worked example — artesunate/amodiaquine course (arithmetic verified). [slide fact — SLIDE 24] For a 22 kg child: artesunate 4 mg/kg × 22 kg = 88 mg per dose. Amodiaquine 10 mg/kg × 22 kg = 220 mg per dose. Twice daily for 3 days = 6 doses. Total artesunate = 88 × 6 = 528 mg. Total amodiaquine = 220 × 6 = 1320 mg. Check: 528/88 = 6 doses and 1320/220 = 6 doses, which matches twice daily for three days. A second check adds the per-dose amounts first: 88 + 220 = 308 mg per dose, and 308 × 6 = 1848 mg total, equal to 528 + 1320 = 1848 mg.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. A dose is a control signal; the pharmacist adjusts it for the patient's biology and verifies it before release.
  • Mechanistic Story. Route → first pass → clearance → exposure → effect.
  • Clinical Story. Toxicity and treatment failure are the two failure modes of a wrong dose.
  • MLS Story. No assay or range is supplied in the lecture; the checking method is professional verification.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Griseofulvin, sodium bicarbonate, artesunate and amodiaquine are named anchors.
  • Biotech Story. Pharmacogenomic testing would individualise the genetic factor [model knowledge].
  • Manufacturing Story. Divisible tablets and suspensions enable small paediatric doses [slide fact — SUSPENSIONS 2026.pptx, SLIDE 6].
  • Computational Story. Dose prediction tools would combine age, weight and renal function [model knowledge].
  • Evolutionary Story. Thin: tolerance is a physiological adaptation, not an evolutionary design argument [inference].
  • Innovation Story. Electronic prescribing checks are the modern form of the slide's manual rule [model knowledge].

Source: PHARM 161 posology 2026.pptx — SLIDE 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21, 22, 24, 25, 26

The Body's Organisation and Homeostasis

Biological geography (opening). Anatomy is the science of body structures and their relationships; physiology is the science of body functions, and function can never really be separated from structure — the principle of complementarity [slide fact — Introduction to Anatomy and Physiology1.pptx, SLIDE 21, 22, 30]. Zoom: organ system → organ → tissue → cell → organelle → protein. Example: blood-pressure control lives in the cardiovascular and nervous systems, in arterial smooth muscle, in the vascular smooth-muscle cell, in the plasma membrane, and in the mechanosensitive ion channel that opens when the vessel wall stretches [model knowledge, not in slides].

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem homeostasis solves is maintaining a bounded internal state despite a fluctuating external state — a control problem with a sensor, a comparator and an actuator. [model knowledge] Pressure, temperature, pH and ion concentration are variables that must be held in a narrow band because enzyme rates and membrane potentials depend on them [inference from SLIDE 57].

Concept 1 — Levels of organisation and the eleven systems.

  • Mechanistic. The slides list eleven organ systems and their components: integumentary, skeletal, muscular, nervous, endocrine, cardiovascular, lymphatic, respiratory, digestive, urinary and reproductive [slide fact — SLIDE 33]. Each structure performs a specific function, and its form determines what it can do [slide fact — SLIDE 30]. Gross anatomy is regional, systemic or surface; microscopic anatomy is cytology and histology; developmental anatomy is embryology [slide fact — SLIDE 23, 24, 25, 26].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no founder of systemic anatomy. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The systemic approach is attributed to Vesalius-era reform; what is unresolved is how much function can be inferred from structure alone, which is why physiology is a separate discipline.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Clinical observational techniques named are palpation, auscultation and percussion, with specific uses — pulses and breathing rates, abnormal lung fluid, air in intestines [slide fact — SLIDE 29]. [model knowledge] These are bedside methods rather than laboratory assays, and no reference interval is supplied.
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] Failure of a system produces a recognisable syndrome: renal failure alters volume and composition of blood; respiratory failure alters blood gases. The slides list the normal functions but do not describe these failures.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of system function: insulin from the endocrine pancreas lowers blood glucose; salbutamol acts on bronchial smooth muscle in the respiratory system [model knowledge]; neither drug appears in this lecture.
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The open question is how to integrate structure and function quantitatively — a modelling problem the slides do not pose.

Concept 2 — Homeostasis and the control loop.

  • Mechanistic. Homeostasis is the ability to maintain a relatively stable internal environment in an ever-changing outside world; internally it is a dynamic equilibrium maintained by chemical, thermal and neural factors [slide fact — SLIDE 54]. The term combines homeo, same, and stasis, to stand or stay [slide fact — SLIDE 54]. Blood glucose is kept between 70 and 110 mg/dL [slide fact — SLIDE 55]. A control mechanism has a receptor that monitors a controlled condition, a control centre that determines the set point and the next action, and an effector that produces the response [slide fact — SLIDE 60, 61]. Control systems named are nervous (rapid and precise), endocrine (slower and longer-lasting) and immune [slide fact — SLIDE 58].
  • Historical/Research. The slides credit a French physiologist working 1813–1878 and Walter Cannon (1871–1945) for naming the process [slide fact — SLIDE 55]; the first is clearly Claude Bernard, though the slide does not print the name, so that identification is [inference]. [model knowledge] What Cannon added was the concept of the set point; current debate concerns whether "set point" is a fixed value or a regulated range.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Variables to be controlled are listed as temperature, nutrients, oxygen, pressure, pH, ion concentrations and toxic chemicals [slide fact — SLIDE 57]. [model knowledge] Glucose, pH and electrolytes are measured in plasma; the slide gives one range only, for glucose, and no method.
  • Clinical/Medical. If homeostasis is not maintained, death may result [slide fact — SLIDE 56]. [inference] This is the physiological reason vital signs are monitored: they are the accessible outputs of control loops.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of loop failure: paracetamol reduces the raised temperature set point in fever [model knowledge]; insulin lowers glucose when the endocrine loop fails in diabetes [model knowledge]. Neither is in the slides.
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] A researcher would need to know whether set points drift with circadian rhythm or disease; the slides present fixed examples.

Concept 3 — Negative and positive feedback, and imbalance.

  • Mechanistic. In negative feedback the original stimulus is reversed; most body systems use it, including body temperature, blood sugar and blood pressure [slide fact — SLIDE 63]. Pressure receptors in arterial walls detect a rise in blood pressure, the brain signals the heart and vessels, heart rate slows and arterioles dilate, and pressure returns to normal [slide fact — SLIDE 65]. In positive feedback the output enhances the original stimulus: stretch receptors in the uterus signal the brain, oxytocin is released, contractions strengthen, and more stretch drives more hormone until birth removes the stimulus [slide fact — SLIDE 70]. Blood clotting is given as the other example [slide fact — SLIDE 69].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no discoverer. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The distinction was formalised in control theory; what is unresolved is whether positive feedback is ever stable without a terminating event — here birth or clot formation supplies it.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No assay is given for oxytocin or baroreceptor activity. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Plasma oxytocin can be measured but is not a routine diagnostic test.
  • Clinical/Medical. Disorder is an abnormality of function; disease is a homeostatic imbalance with distinct symptoms, which the patient feels, and signs, which the doctor observes; diagnosis is distinguishing one disease from another [slide fact — SLIDE 73]. [model knowledge] Fever and rash are the slide's examples of signs [slide fact — SLIDE 73].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of intervention: oxytocin is used clinically to induce labour, exploiting the positive-feedback loop the slides describe [model knowledge]; antihypertensives act at points in the baroreflex-related pathways [model knowledge].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The open question is how feedback resets after chronic disease; the slides describe only acute correction.

Worked example — converting the glucose range to SI units (arithmetic verified). [slide fact — SLIDE 55 gives 70–110 mg/dL] [model knowledge for molar mass 180 g/mol] 70 mg/dL = 700 mg/L = 0.700 g/L; 0.700/180 = 0.00389 mol/L = 3.89 mmol/L. 110 mg/dL = 1.100 g/L; 1.100/180 = 0.00611 mol/L = 6.11 mmol/L. So the slide's range corresponds to roughly 3.9–6.1 mmol/L, which matches the usual fasting reference interval [model knowledge]. [inference] A value outside this band is the numeric form of a homeostatic failure.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Structure and function are one problem; homeostasis is the control system that keeps that problem solvable.
  • Mechanistic Story. Sensor → control centre → effector → variable restored; negative feedback reverses, positive amplifies.
  • Clinical Story. Disease is a labelled homeostatic imbalance.
  • MLS Story. Bedside observation and blood glucose are the only measurement threads here.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Insulin, oxytocin and antipyretics are named anchors beyond the slides.
  • Biotech Story. Biosensors for continuous glucose monitoring are the technological extension of the glucose example [model knowledge].
  • Manufacturing Story. Thin: no device is described in these slides.
  • Computational Story. Physiological control models are the natural computational extension [model knowledge].
  • Evolutionary Story. Negative feedback is selected because it is stable; positive feedback needs a stop signal [inference].
  • Innovation Story. Continuous monitoring devices convert an intermittent range into a live control loop [model knowledge].

Source: Introduction to Anatomy and Physiology1.pptx — SLIDE 21, 22, 23, 24, 25, 26, 29, 30, 33, 54, 55, 56, 57, 58, 60, 61, 63, 65, 69, 70, 73

Anatomical Terminology

Biological geography (opening). Anatomy has its own universal language, built largely from Greek and Latin roots, which allows precise discussion across countries [slide fact — Introduction to Anatomy and Physiology1.pptx, SLIDE 75]. The zoom this section provides is the naming system itself: root → combining vowel → prefix → suffix → formal term → body region → organ → tissue. [model knowledge] A term such as pericardium locates a structure around the heart, which then directs you to the serous membrane, the parietal and visceral layers, and the pericardial cavity [slide fact — SLIDE 79, 81, 108].

Engineering/physics-first. The problem anatomical language solves is disambiguation under compression: one short string must encode location, structure and often procedure, without ambiguity. [inference from SLIDE 75] Prefixes and suffixes act like a coordinate system; combining vowels exist to make strings pronounceable [slide fact — SLIDE 76].

Concept 1 — Word parts and decoding.

  • Mechanistic. At least one root carries the core meaning, as cardi- means heart; two roots may combine, as cyt- cell and chrom- colour in cytochrome; a combining vowel such as "o" joins them; a prefix modifies the core meaning and a suffix is added at the end [slide fact — SLIDE 76, 77]. Terminologia Anatomica was published in 1998 by an international committee, and eponyms are not included in it [slide fact — SLIDE 75].
  • Historical/Research. The slides note that most commemorative names have been replaced by more precise terms over the last century [slide fact — SLIDE 75]. [model knowledge] What remains unresolved is that clinicians still use eponyms — Achilles tendon, Fallopian tube — so both names must be known.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. No laboratory method appears. [slide fact — SLIDE 75–85] [model knowledge] Terminology matters in specimen labelling: misspelled or ambiguous site names are a sample-identification error.
  • Clinical/Medical. A wrong prefix reverses the clinical meaning — hyper- versus hypo- changes a diagnosis from overactive to underactive [inference from SLIDE 79].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of decoding: -cillin identifies amoxicillin and ampicillin as penicillin antibiotics; -olol identifies atenolol and propranolol as beta blockers; -eprazole identifies omeprazole as a proton-pump inhibitor; -floxacin identifies ciprofloxacin as a quinolone [slide fact — SLIDE 85].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The open question is how to standardise drug-name suffixes across languages; the slides give an English list only.

Concept 2 — Directional terms and planes.

  • Mechanistic. Directional terms are word pairs with opposite meanings: superior/inferior, anterior/posterior, medial/lateral, ipsilateral/contralateral, proximal/distal and superficial/deep [slide fact — SLIDE 93]. Anatomical position is the reference: standing erect, facing the observer, palms forward, feet flat; prone is face down and supine is face up [slide fact — SLIDE 86, 87]. A plane is an imaginary flat surface passing through the body and a section is one of the two resulting surfaces [slide fact — SLIDE 98]. Sagittal divides left and right, with midsagittal producing equal halves; frontal or coronal divides front and back; transverse divides upper and lower; oblique combines two planes [slide fact — SLIDE 100, 101].
  • Historical/Research. No discoverer is named. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Standardisation of anatomical position was a 19th-century international effort; what is unresolved is the persistence of positional ambiguity in radiology reports.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Specimen orientation matters in histopathology: the plane of section determines which structures appear together [model knowledge]. The slides show brain sections in horizontal, frontal and midsagittal planes but give no method [slide fact — SLIDE 102].
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] "Proximal" versus "distal" changes amputation level and therefore function; "ipsilateral" versus "contralateral" changes which limb is operated on.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: an intramuscular injection is described by site and quadrant — the dorsogluteal or ventrogluteal region — which depends entirely on directional terminology [model knowledge].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The research need is automated parsing of clinical text into anatomical coordinates; the slides provide the vocabulary, not the algorithm.

Concept 3 — Cavities, membranes and serous surfaces.

  • Mechanistic. The dorsal cavity has cranial and vertebral subdivisions; the ventral cavity is separated by the diaphragm into thoracic and abdominopelvic cavities [slide fact — SLIDE 104]. The thoracic cavity has a pericardial cavity and two pleural cavities, with the mediastinum between the lungs containing heart, oesophagus, trachea, thymus and large vessels [slide fact — SLIDE 108]. The abdominal cavity contains stomach, spleen, liver, gallbladder, small intestine and most of the large intestine; the pelvic cavity contains bladder, part of the large intestine and reproductive organs [slide fact — SLIDE 112]. Serous membranes have a parietal layer lining walls and a visceral layer covering organs, with serous fluid secreted between them to reduce friction [slide fact — SLIDE 114]. Retroperitoneal organs lie behind the peritoneum and include kidneys, adrenal glands, pancreas, duodenum and parts of the colon and great vessels [slide fact — SLIDE 115].
  • Historical/Research. No discoverer is named. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The embryonic rotation that makes some organs retroperitoneal is well described; what is unresolved is why the pancreas remains retroperitoneal while the duodenum is only partly so.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [model knowledge] Ascites is an accumulation of fluid in the peritoneal cavity; diagnostic paracentesis samples it. The slides describe the anatomy but no procedure or reference value.
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] Peritonitis is inflammation of the peritoneal membrane; a retroperitoneal tumour can grow large before producing obvious signs because the space is roomy.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: intraperitoneal chemotherapy exploits the peritoneal cavity as a compartment [model knowledge]; not present in the slides.
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] A researcher would need serous-fluid dynamics to predict drug distribution across these compartments.

Worked example — decoding and arithmetic (arithmetic verified). [slide fact — SLIDE 85 gives -cycline → tetracycline antibiotic and names doxycycline] Decode doxycycline: root cycline places it in the tetracycline class; tetra- means four, referring to the four fused rings [model knowledge]. [illustrative arithmetic, not in slides] A regimen of doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 7 days gives 2 × 100 = 200 mg/day and 200 × 7 = 1400 mg total. Check: 14 tablets of 100 mg = 1400 mg. The decoding identifies the class; the arithmetic gives the exposure. No dose is stated in the terminology slides, so the numbers are labelled illustrative.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Medical language is a compressed coordinate system for location, direction and procedure.
  • Mechanistic Story. Root + vowel + prefix + suffix → unambiguous term → correct site and structure.
  • Clinical Story. A reversed prefix or a wrong plane changes the procedure, not just the word.
  • MLS Story. Specimen orientation and site naming are error sources.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Drug-name suffixes encode class, as -cillin, -olol, -eprazole and -floxacin demonstrate.
  • Biotech Story. Thin: no biotechnology content in these slides.
  • Manufacturing Story. Thin: no production content in these slides.
  • Computational Story. Terminology dictionaries and ontologies are the digital extension [model knowledge].
  • Evolutionary Story. The layered body plan is why directional terms work at all [inference].
  • Innovation Story. Standardised terminology is itself an innovation that enabled international trials [inference].

Source: Introduction to Anatomy and Physiology1.pptx — SLIDE 75, 76, 77, 79, 81, 85, 86, 87, 93, 98, 100, 101, 102, 104, 108, 112, 114, 115

The Four Basic Tissue Types

Biological geography (opening). A tissue is a group of similar cells and cell products arising from the same region of the embryo and working together to perform a structural or physiological role [slide fact — Tissues (2026).pptx, SLIDE 2]. Zoom: organ → tissue → cell → extracellular matrix → fibre → protein. Epithelium derives from all three germ layers, connective tissue and muscle from mesoderm, and nerve tissue from ectoderm [slide fact — SLIDE 3]. [model knowledge] An epithelial barrier such as the gut lining sits on a basement membrane, beneath which lies connective tissue containing capillaries and immune cells; a drug crossing that barrier meets tight junctions, transporters and metabolic enzymes.

Engineering/physics-first. The physical problem each tissue solves differs: epithelium solves selective barrier formation; connective tissue solves mechanical load bearing; muscle solves force generation; nerve solves rapid long-distance signalling. [inference] Their structures follow directly from those problems — tightly packed cells for a barrier, fibres under tension for load, contractile proteins for force, and elongated processes for distance.

Concept 1 — Cell junctions and the classification logic.

  • Mechanistic. Cell junctions are points of contact between adjacent plasma membranes and serve three functions: fluid-tight seals, anchoring, and channels for ion and molecule passage [slide fact — SLIDE 8]. Tight junctions use claudin and occludin strips and are common in GI and bladder linings [slide fact — SLIDE 10]. Adherens junctions hold epithelial cells together using plaque and microfilaments [slide fact — SLIDE 11]. Gap junctions use connexons forming fluid-filled tunnels, allowing muscle and nerve impulses to spread [slide fact — SLIDE 12]. Desmosomes resist separation using intermediate filaments and are important in skin and cardiac muscle [slide fact — SLIDE 13]. Hemidesmosomes connect cells to basement membrane [slide fact — SLIDE 14].
  • Historical/Research. No discoverer is named. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The molecular components were identified in the late 20th century; what is unresolved is how junctional permeability is regulated dynamically.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. A biopsy is removal of living tissue for microscopic examination, preserved, sectioned and stained [slide fact — SLIDE 5]. [model knowledge] Pemphigus vulgaris is shown as a junctional disease [slide fact — SLIDE 15]; direct immunofluorescence would be the diagnostic method, but the slide does not name it.
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] Loss of tight junctions in inflammation increases intestinal permeability; loss of desmosomes produces blistering disease. The slides show pemphigus but give no mechanism text.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named example: cetrimide and benzalkonium chloride are cationic surfactants that disrupt membrane surfaces [slide fact — PHARM 162 PHARMACEUTICAL EMULSIONS (4).pptx, SLIDE 33], illustrating how junction and membrane integrity is a pharmaceutical target.
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] The open question is how to transiently open tight junctions for drug delivery without permanent barrier damage.

Concept 2 — Epithelial tissue.

  • Mechanistic. Epithelium covers surfaces, lines hollow organs and ducts, and forms glands when cells sink below the surface [slide fact — SLIDE 17]. General features are closely packed sheets, a basement membrane, an apical free surface, absence of blood vessels, good nerve supply and rapid cell division [slide fact — SLIDE 18]. Classification is by layers — simple, stratified, pseudostratified — and by surface cell shape — squamous, cuboidal, columnar and transitional [slide fact — SLIDE 21, 22].Simple squamous controls diffusion and filtration; simple cuboidal lines kidney tubules for absorption and secretion; nonciliated simple columnar has goblet cells secreting mucus and microvilli for absorption; ciliated simple columnar moves mucus in respiratory tract and uterine tubes; transitional epithelium changes shape when stretched [slide fact — SLIDE 24, 26, 29, 32, 41].
  • Historical/Research. No discoverer named. [slide fact] [model knowledge] The classification by shape and layers is a morphological system that predates molecular markers; what is unresolved is how well morphology predicts transporter expression.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. A Pap smear collects sloughed cells from uterus and vagina to detect precancerous changes [slide fact — SLIDE 38]. [model knowledge] The slide states the test but not a sampling or reporting method.
  • Clinical/Medical. [model knowledge] Squamous metaplasia in the respiratory tract follows chronic irritation; transitional epithelium is the origin of bladder tumours. The slides do not name these diseases.
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the point of absorption: microvilli increase surface area for drug absorption in the GI tract [slide fact — SLIDE 29]; this is the structural basis for the bioavailability order in the suspensions lecture [inference].
  • Research/Frontier. [inference] A researcher needs region-specific transporter maps, which morphology alone cannot give.

Concept 3 — Connective tissue and its matrix.

  • Mechanistic. Connective tissue is the material between cells that supports and binds structures, stores energy as fat and provides immunity [slide fact — SLIDE 49]. It comprises cells, fibres and ground substance [slide fact — SLIDE 51]. Collagen fibres are tough and resist pull and make up 25% of body protein; elastic fibres stretch to 150% of relaxed length and recoil; reticular fibres form thin branched frameworks in spleen and lymph nodes [slide fact — SLIDE 53]. Blast cells retain the ability to divide and produce matrix; cyte cells are mature and cannot divide [slide fact — SLIDE 52]. Types are fibrous, supportive and fluid [slide fact — SLIDE 57]. Hyaline cartilage is avascular and repairs slowly; dense regular connective tissue forms tendons and resists stress in one direction, while dense irregular tissue resists tension from any direction [slide fact — SLIDE 70, 66, 67].
  • Historical/Research. The slides give no discoverer. [slide fact] [model knowledge] Marfan syndrome is presented as an inherited disorder of the fibrillin gene with abnormal elastic fibres, tall habitus and life-threatening aortic weakening [slide fact — SLIDE 54]. [inference] This is the slide's clearest gene-to-structure-to-disease example.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. Histological staining distinguishes fibre types; the slide captions identify black elastic and pink collagen fibres [slide fact — SLIDE 61]. No quantitative reference range is given.
