  • Research / Frontier. Super-resolution and expansion microscopy are the live frontier [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. No atoms are rearranged in a microscope. The conserved quantity is optical information carried by photons: a photon from the lamp is either transmitted, diffracted by specimen detail, absorbed or scattered. Energy accounting: a photon of wavelength λ carries E = hc/λ [G]; shorter λ therefore means higher photon energy, which is why UV can damage DNA [S BIOL 157_Lecture 6 SLIDE 10]. In an electron microscope an electron accelerated through potential V gains kinetic energy eV from the electric field; that is a transfer from the power supply, not production of energy [S SLIDE 141–142; G for the eV relation].

Worked example — resolution, arithmetic verified. With λ = 560 nm and NA = 1.4 [S SLIDE 93]: r = 0.61×560/1.4 = 341.6/1.4 = 244 nm. Independent check: 0.61/1.4 = 0.436 < 0.5, so the result must beat the slide's λ/2 ≈ 280 nm bound — 244 < 280 ✓. Second check with a 40× plan achromat at NA = 0.65 [S SLIDE 89]: r = 341.6/0.65 ≈ 526 nm, over twice as large — hence high NA is essential.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Resolution, not magnification, is the real limit; NA and wavelength set it.
  • Mechanistic Story. Diffraction at fine detail → wider collection cone → higher NA → smaller r.
  • Clinical Story. Reports must stay within the physical resolution of the instrument.
  • MLS Story. Oil refractive index, parfocality and working distance are QC variables.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Thin; no drug at this mechanism point.
  • Research Story. Super-resolution is beyond these slides.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered fluorescent tags extend what light microscopy can report [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Objective lenses are manufactured to declared NA and correction class (plan achromat, fluorite, apochromat) with tabulated NA values [S SLIDE 88–89].
  • Computational Story. Deconvolution algorithms estimate the original object from the diffraction-blurred image [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Not applicable to an instrument; the biological analogue is the eye's sensitivity peak at 560 nm [S SLIDE 93].
  • Innovation Story. Low-cost high-NA optics for field diagnostics remain an engineering opportunity [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "More magnification always shows more" is false — empty magnification [S SLIDE 66]. "Oil is for lubrication" is false — its job is refractive-index matching [S SLIDE 78].
  • Memory Hook. NA = n sin U; r = 0.61λ/NA; bigger NA, smaller r, shorter working distance.
  • Connections. This physics is reused in fluorescence, UV and electron microscopy next.

Source: 2025 BIOL 151 CELL STRUCTURE [Autosaved].pdf — SLIDE 22–24, SLIDE 62–72, SLIDE 81–94.

Light microscopy methods compared

Location (Biological Geography). Instrument → lamp → condenser → specimen → objective → phase plate or dichroic mirror → detector/eye; specimen zoom: tissue → cell → organelle → protein → fluorochrome → excited electron. Photon lifecycle: emitted by lamp, shaped by condenser, diffracted by specimen, separated by mirror or plate, absorbed by retina or camera [S SLIDE 97, 103, 116, 132]. Excitation photons should never reach the eyepiece in fluorescence [S SLIDE 130, 132].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem is contrast: living cells are colourless and nearly transparent, so bright-field gives almost no intensity difference [S SLIDE 99, 110]. Each method manufactures contrast from a different variable: dark-field removes the background beam [S SLIDE 103]; phase contrast converts a delay into brightness at a phase plate [S SLIDE 114–117]; fluorescence shifts absorbed light to a longer emitted wavelength [S SLIDE 136–137]; UV shortens λ and improves r [S SLIDE 125].

Just-in-time foundations. Contrast = intensity difference [S SLIDE 98]; diffraction/scattering = bending by fine structure [S SLIDE 113]; phase = position in a wave cycle; amplitude = brightness [S SLIDE 112]; fluorochrome = dye emitting visible light after UV absorption [S SLIDE 134]; epifluorescence = both light paths through the objective [S SLIDE 130]; dichroic mirror = separates the two paths [S SLIDE 132].

Concept 1 — Bright-field microscopy.

  • Mechanistic. The condenser focuses lamp light into a cone at the specimen; images arise because the specimen changes light speed and path by refractive index and opacity [S SLIDE 97]. Rays must interfere for contrast, so a refractive-index match is invisible [S SLIDE 98]; transparent living material needs staining, which kills it [S SLIDE 99].
  • Historical / Research. The bright-field design is the baseline against which the other methods are compared [S SLIDE 105, 123]; no inventor is named.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: stained tissue section; method: H & E on glass slides [S SLIDE 180–183]. Reference range: none. Error source: unstained transparent specimen appears blank [S SLIDE 99]; low-pH stomach inactivates salivary amylase in digestibility tests [S BIOL 157_Lecture 5 SLIDE 36]. QC: correct stain charge — cationic haematoxylin for nucleic acids, anionic eosin for cytoplasmic basic groups [S SLIDE 180, 183].
  • Clinical / Medical. Routine histopathology is bright-field H & E [S SLIDE 183]; a missed stain means a missed diagnosis [I].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — no therapeutic drug; the relevant reagents are dyes.
  • Research / Frontier. Quantitative phase imaging is an outside-slide alternative [G].

Concept 2 — Dark-field microscopy.

  • Mechanistic. An opaque disc in the darkfield condenser blocks direct light; only specimen-reflected light enters the objective, giving a bright specimen on dark background [S SLIDE 103]. It suits live unstained samples such as tissue-culture smears and water-borne organisms [S SLIDE 101], reveals 40 nm gold [S SLIDE 108], but is poor for large objects [S SLIDE 108].
  • Historical / Research. The slides present it as an inexpensive modification of a bright-field microscope [S SLIDE 100]; no named pioneer.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: wet mount of yeast cells in liquid [S SLIDE 104]; method: darkfield condenser with opaque disc [S SLIDE 103]. Reference range: none. Error source: direct background light reaching the objective destroys the effect [S SLIDE 103]. QC: align the condenser so the direct beam is fully blocked [I from SLIDE 103].
  • Clinical / Medical. Useful for organisms invisible or distorted by standard staining [S SLIDE 102] — clinically this applies to spirochaete detection [G, outside slides].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — none at this mechanism point.
  • Research / Frontier. Nanoparticle tracking uses the same dark-field physics [G].

Concept 3 — Phase contrast.

  • Mechanistic. Specimen light is diffracted (darker image) and retarded by an amount depending on thickness [S SLIDE 113–114]; the eye sees only a weak interference pattern. A phase plate further retards diffracted rays, amplifying brightness differences [S SLIDE 115–117] via an annulus below the stage [S SLIDE 119]; direct rays pass a thinner plate region and stay bright [S SLIDE 116, 120].
  • Historical / Research. Frits Zernike's phase-contrast invention is outside the slides [G]; the deck gives only the mechanism [S SLIDE 110–120].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: living cells in culture; method: compare bright-field and phase-contrast images of the same field [S SLIDE 123]. Reference range: none. Error source: thick specimens over-retard light and create halos [G — halo artefact not in slides]. QC: correct annulus/phase-plate pairing [S SLIDE 119].
  • Clinical / Medical. Watching living cell movement and cytoplasmic flow has research and diagnostic value [S SLIDE 118]; time-lapse photography of living cells is specifically cited [S SLIDE 118].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — none.
  • Research / Frontier. Live-cell imaging of drug response is a direct extension [G].

Concept 4 — UV and fluorescence microscopy.

  • Mechanistic. UV roughly doubles resolution [S SLIDE 125]; specimens absorbing UV and re-emitting longer wavelengths, or stained to do so, become visible [S SLIDE 125]. UV will not pass glass, so quartz lenses are needed [S SLIDE 126], and it damages the eye, requiring a camera or screen [S SLIDE 127]. In fluorescence an electron rises to a higher orbital then returns, emitting light and heat [S SLIDE 136–137]; excitation and emission share the objective and are split by a dichroic mirror [S SLIDE 130, 132].
  • Historical / Research. The slides describe immunofluorescence with fluorescein-labelled antibodies [S SLIDE 134–135] but name no inventor; the Coons antibody-labelling method is added [G].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: cells stained with fluorochrome-labelled antibody [S SLIDE 134]; method: excite at absorption wavelength, view only emitted wavelength through a filter [S SLIDE 137]. Reference range: none. Error source: excitation leak gives a bright background [S SLIDE 131]; quartz is required for UV [S SLIDE 126]. QC: efficient separation of excitation and emission [S SLIDE 131].
  • Clinical / Medical. Immunofluorescence detects specific cellular proteins [S SLIDE 135]; clinically this underlies autoimmune serology and pathogen antigen detection [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Fluorescein is named in the slides as the label conjugated to antibody [S SLIDE 134]; as a therapeutic it is also used as a diagnostic dye [G]. No other drug named.
  • Research / Frontier. Multiplexed fluorophores and super-resolution are open frontiers [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. One absorbed higher-energy photon yields one lower-energy emitted photon plus heat; the difference is dissipated, so energy is conserved, not created [S SLIDE 136–137; G for wording]. Shorter-wavelength UV photons carry more energy, which is why they induce mutations [S BIOL 157_Lecture 6 SLIDE 10]. No atoms change; dark-field atoms merely redirect photons.

Worked example — photon energy, arithmetic verified. With E = hc/λ and hc ≈ 1240 eV·nm [G]: 560 nm → 1240/560 ≈ 2.21 eV; 280 nm → 1240/280 ≈ 4.43 eV. Independent check: 560/280 = 2, so halving λ doubles photon energy, matching the slide's two-fold resolution claim [S SLIDE 125]. A 40 nm gold particle is below the ~244 nm light limit, so dark-field detects scattered intensity, not diameter [S SLIDE 108].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Every method here manufactures contrast from a different physical variable.
  • Mechanistic Story. Block the background (dark-field), shift phase (phase contrast), or shift wavelength (fluorescence).
  • Clinical Story. Live-cell and specific-protein visualization change what a laboratory can report.
  • MLS Story. Specimen state (live vs fixed), stain charge and filter choice are the variables.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Fluorescein-labelled antibody is the one named reagent; no therapeutic drug.
  • Research Story. Multiplexing and super-resolution remain open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered fluorescent proteins and antibody conjugates are core tools [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Filters, mirrors and quartz objectives are precision optical products [G].
  • Computational Story. Image segmentation turns fluorescence images into data [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. No evolutionary claim; the viewed biology evolved, the optics were engineered.
  • Innovation Story. Low-cost LED fluorescence adapters for field clinics are a live opportunity [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Fluorescence stains everything" is false — antibody binding is specific [S SLIDE 135]; "dark-field needs a special expensive microscope" is false — it is an inexpensive modification [S SLIDE 100].
  • Memory Hook. Block, delay, or shift: dark-field blocks, phase delays, fluorescence shifts wavelength.
  • Connections. The resolution equation from the previous section explains why UV helps; electron microscopy next removes the wavelength limit entirely.

Source: 2025 BIOL 151 CELL STRUCTURE [Autosaved].pdf — SLIDE 97–104, SLIDE 110–120, SLIDE 125–135.

Electron microscopy and specimen preparation

Location (Biological Geography). Electron gun → accelerating voltage → condenser electromagnetic coil → specimen on copper grid → objective coil → projector coil → fluorescent screen/photographic plate [S SLIDE 143–147]. Biological zoom: tissue → cell → organelle → membrane → lipid/protein → the heavy-metal stain atom that supplies contrast [S SLIDE 185–186]. Stain lifecycle: uranyl acetate binds nucleic acids and proteins, lead binds lipids and membranes, osmium binds membranes, lipids and nucleic acids [S SLIDE 186]; staining is applied during preparation, imaged once, and the specimen is not reused [I]. A heavy-metal stain should never appear in a living cell; its presence means a prepared specimen [I].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: visible light cannot resolve below roughly λ/2 ≈ 300 nm [S SLIDE 94, 158], while viruses and membranes need sub-nanometre detail. Solution: use electrons, whose wave-like wavelength at 60 kV is about 0.005 nm [S SLIDE 141–142]. But electrons penetrate poorly, so the column is under high vacuum and all water must be removed [S SLIDE 165], and the specimen must be ultrathin or all electrons scatter and contrast is lost [S SLIDE 167]. Electromagnetic coils replace glass lenses [S SLIDE 158]; resolution is limited in practice by lens imperfection and preparation quality, not by wavelength [S SLIDE 142, 145].

Just-in-time foundations. TEM = transmission electron microscope, beam passes through the specimen; SEM = scanning electron microscope, beam scans the surface [S SLIDE 143, 151]; ultrathin section = 50–100 nm slice [S SLIDE 168, 171]; fixation = rapid killing and preservation [S SLIDE 159]; embedding = infiltration with a hard support medium [S SLIDE 166, 168]; microtome = instrument cutting thin sections [S SLIDE 171]; positive staining = specimen made electron-dense against a light background [S SLIDE 187]; negative staining = dense background with a bright specimen [S SLIDE 188].

Concept 1 — Electron wavelength and resolution.

  • Mechanistic. All matter has dual nature; electron wavelength is λ = √(1.5/V) nm, so at 60 kV λ ≈ 0.005 nm [S SLIDE 141–142]. Modern instruments reach 0.2–0.3 nm resolution and about 300,000× useful magnification [S SLIDE 145]; the theoretical gain is not realised because specimen contrast and imperfect electromagnetic lenses limit it [S SLIDE 142].
  • Historical / Research. The wave nature of matter is de Broglie's postulate [G]; slides give the formula without attribution [S SLIDE 141]. Unresolved in slides: none.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: ultrathin section on a copper grid [S SLIDE 172, 176]; method: TEM/SEM; reference point: 0.2–0.3 nm resolution [S SLIDE 145]; error source: specimen too thick → poor contrast [S SLIDE 167]; QC: vacuum integrity and correct embedding medium [S SLIDE 165–166].
  • Clinical / Medical. Virus and basement-membrane detail is only accessible this way [I from SLIDE 155, 187]; renal biopsy interpretation depends on it [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — no drug acts at electron optics.
  • Research / Frontier. Cryo-EM avoids chemical fixation entirely [G].

Concept 2 — TEM versus SEM.

  • Mechanistic. TEM: finely focused beam from an electron gun passes through an ultrathin section; acceleration 50–150 kV; deflection depends on electron density of the object, greater atomic mass giving greater deflection [S SLIDE 143–146]; biological material is low contrast because it is mostly C, H, N, O [S SLIDE 147]. SEM: uses electrons rather than light, large depth of field, magnification up to about 100,000× versus 2,000× for light microscopy, surface of a solid specimen, resolution often 10 nm at 20,000×, giving 3D-looking images [S SLIDE 151–155].
  • Historical / Research. No names given; slides contrast the two designs directly [S SLIDE 143, 151].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: ultrathin section (TEM) versus solid surface (SEM) [S SLIDE 155]; reference: light microscope 240 nm, electron 0.2 nm; magnification 1000–1500× versus 300–500,000× [S SLIDE 158]; error source: wrong medium for the objective creates artefacts [S 2025 BIOL 151 … SLIDE 86].
  • Clinical / Medical. SEM reveals surface pathology; TEM reveals interior ultrastructure [I].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin.
  • Research / Frontier. Correlative light and electron microscopy is an active method frontier [G].

Concept 3 — Preparation chain: fixation, embedding, sectioning, staining.

  • Mechanistic. Fixation prevents autolysis and microbial attack; physical fixation uses liquid nitrogen at −196 °C so ice crystals do not form [S SLIDE 160–162]. Chemical fixatives: formaldehyde and glutaraldehyde fix proteins, osmium tetroxide and potassium permanganate fix lipids [S SLIDE 164]. Dehydration uses increasing ethanol to 100%, xylene clears, paraffin or epoxy resin infiltrates [S SLIDE 165–166]. Sections 50–100 nm for EM cut by microtome with glass or diamond knife [S SLIDE 168, 171–172]. Haematoxylin (cationic) binds negatively charged nucleic acids; eosin (anionic) binds basic cytoplasmic groups and stains red [S SLIDE 180, 183]. Uranium binds nucleic acids and proteins, lead binds lipids and membranes [S SLIDE 186].
  • Historical / Research. H & E is described as routine for animal tissues but no inventor is named [S SLIDE 183]; the uranium/lead double stain is likened to H & E for EM [S SLIDE 187].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: any tissue; method sequence: fix → dehydrate → clear → embed → section → stain [S SLIDE 159–187]; error sources: delayed fixation → autolysis [S SLIDE 160], wrong embedding hardness → poor sections [I from SLIDE 169], wrong stain charge → wrong tissue component coloured [S SLIDE 180]. QC: fixation called the basis of all histological work [S SLIDE 159].
  • Clinical / Medical. A poorly fixed biopsy can be diagnostically useless; post-mortem lysosomal change confuses interpretation [S SLIDE 214].
  • Pharmaceutical. Glutaraldehyde and osmium tetroxide are named reagents, not therapies [S SLIDE 164]; no therapeutic drug is discussed.
  • Research / Frontier. Cryo-fixation avoids dehydration artefacts [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. In TEM the electron loses kinetic energy to the specimen by scattering; the image is formed by the electrons that remain [I from SLIDE 146, 184]. The potential difference V does work eV on each electron — energy transferred from the supply to the beam, not created [G]. In staining, heavy atoms (Pb, U, atomic number high) scatter electrons strongly, converting a chemical difference into an intensity difference [S SLIDE 185]; no atoms of the specimen are created or destroyed. Water removal is atom accounting too: specimen H₂O is replaced stepwise by ethanol, xylene, then wax or resin [S SLIDE 165–166].

Worked example — electron wavelength, arithmetic verified. Slide formula: λ = √(1.5/V) nm with V = 60,000 V [S SLIDE 142]. Compute 1.5/60,000 = 0.000025; √0.000025 = 0.005 nm. Independent verification with the standard de Broglie relation λ = 1.226/√V nm [G]: √60,000 ≈ 244.95, so 1.226/244.95 ≈ 0.005005 nm. The two agree to three significant figures because √1.5 = 1.2247 ≈ 1.226. Check the implication: a 0.005 nm wavelength is about 100,000 times shorter than 560 nm green light, consistent with the slide's claim of a huge theoretical resolution gain [S SLIDE 142].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Shorter wavelength buys resolution; vacuum, thin sections and heavy metals buy usable contrast.
  • Mechanistic Story. Electron beam → electromagnetic lenses → differential scattering → image.
  • Clinical Story. Ultrastructure (viruses, membranes) becomes visible for diagnosis.
  • MLS Story. Fixation, dehydration, embedding, sectioning and staining each have failure modes.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Thin; reagents only, no therapeutic drug.
  • Research Story. Cryo-EM and correlative microscopy are open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered labels and cryo methods extend structural biology [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. EM grids, diamond knives and uranyl/lead reagents are specialised products with strict QA [G].
  • Computational Story. Tomographic reconstruction turns projections into 3D volumes [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Not applicable; this is an instrument solution to a physics limit.
  • Innovation Story. Affordable benchtop SEM is a continuing engineering target [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "EM resolution is unlimited" is false — lenses and preparation constrain it [S SLIDE 142, 145]; "SEM looks inside cells" is false — it images surfaces [S SLIDE 155].
  • Memory Hook. Fix, dehydrate, embed, cut, stain; TEM inside, SEM outside; Pb and U give contrast.
  • Connections. The 240 nm versus 0.2 nm comparison [S SLIDE 158] closes the resolution arc opened two sections earlier.

Source: 2025 BIOL 151 CELL STRUCTURE [Autosaved].pdf — SLIDE 141–158, SLIDE 159–187.

Atomic structure and electronic configuration

Location (Biological Geography). Atom → nucleus (protons, neutrons) → electron shells K, L, M → subshells s, p → orbitals → the electron itself → the bond it forms → the functional group of a biomolecule → active site [S SLIDE 16–20]. For a biological electron lifecycle: an electron sits in a low-energy orbital, may be excited to a higher one by a photon (fluorescence, earlier section), returns and releases energy [S BIOL 157_Lecture 6 SLIDE 136]; in metabolism an electron can leave one molecule and be accepted by NAD⁺ [S BIOL 157_Lecture 8 SLIDE 19]. It should never appear free in large amounts in a cell; electrons are always held in bonds or carriers [I].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: electrons around a nucleus cannot be described as classical planets, because an accelerating charge would radiate and spiral in [S SLIDE 9] and because position and momentum cannot both be known [S SLIDE 14]. Quantum mechanics solves this by quantising energy: only certain orbitals are allowed, and an orbital is a 95% probability volume [S SLIDE 15]. Chemical behaviour then follows from which orbitals are filled — this is why sodium loses one electron and why carbon forms four bonds.

Just-in-time foundations. Atom = basic unit of an element [S SLIDE 3]; isotope = same element, different mass (protium, deuterium, tritium) [S SLIDE 5]; quantum number = a label describing an electron's energy, shape, orientation or spin [S SLIDE 16]; shell = orbitals sharing the same principal quantum number [S SLIDE 17]; orbital = 95% probability volume [S SLIDE 15]; ground state = lowest energy arrangement [G].

Concept 1 — The model sequence.

  • Mechanistic. Dalton: indivisible hard ball; Thomson: plum pudding from cathode-ray work; Rutherford: nuclear atom from gold-foil scattering; Bohr: planetary orbits with quantised energies; Schrödinger: wave-mechanical model [S SLIDE 3–8, 13–15].
  • Historical / Research. Democritus proposed particulate matter about 500 BC; Aristotle proposed continuous hyle; Dalton was first with a scientific atomic theory based on definite proportions and conservation of mass [S SLIDE 3]; Dalton's claims were later overturned by subatomic particles, radioactivity/fusion and isotopes [S SLIDE 5]. Unresolved: teaching of wave functions remains mathematically demanding [I from SLIDE 14].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. No specimen or reference range; the "reference" is the periodic table [I]. Error source: using Bohr's model for atoms larger than hydrogen fails [S SLIDE 13].
  • Clinical / Medical. Isotopes matter clinically: tritium is named [S SLIDE 5]; radioactive iodine is used in thyroid disease [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin at atomic-model level; at the isotope level, levothyroxine is a named drug whose iodine atoms are therapeutically relevant [G].
  • Research / Frontier. Multi-electron systems remain analytically hard [S SLIDE 14].

Concept 2 — Quantum numbers and shell capacity.

  • Mechanistic. Principal n sets energy level and closeness to nucleus, shells named K, L, M; maximum electrons per shell = 2n² [S SLIDE 17]. Azimuthal l sets orbital shape, from 0 to n−1; l = 0 gives a spherical s orbital holding two electrons [S SLIDE 18]. Magnetic mₗ sets orientation, −l to +l [S SLIDE 19]. Spin mₛ is ±½ [S SLIDE 20].
  • Historical / Research. The four-number scheme supersedes Bohr's single quantum number [S SLIDE 15–16]; Schrödinger and Heisenberg provide the framework [S SLIDE 14].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference values: 2n² gives K=2, L=8, M=18 [I from SLIDE 17]; no clinical specimen applies.
  • Clinical / Medical. Electron configuration predicts valence and hence drug-relevant bonding [I].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin here; the atom-level drug link is indirect.
  • Research / Frontier. Relativistic effects in heavy elements are outside slides [G].

Concept 3 — Filling rules.

  • Mechanistic. Aufbau: lower-energy orbitals fill first, order 1s, 2s, 2p, 3s, 3p, 4s, 3d, 4p, 5s, 4d, 5p, 6s, 4f [S SLIDE 23]. Pauli: maximum two electrons per orbital with opposite spins [S SLIDE 24]. Hund: degenerate orbitals fill singly with parallel spins before pairing [S SLIDE 25].
  • Historical / Research. Named rules come from Aufbau, Pauli and Hund [S SLIDE 22–25]; no further history in slides.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference example: sodium 2,8,1 and 1s²2s²2p⁶3s¹ [S SLIDE 23]; error source: writing 3d before 4s, contrary to Aufbau [I from SLIDE 23].
  • Clinical / Medical. Hemoglobin's iron and oxygen binding are consequences of electronic structure [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin at this exact step; the slide gives no drug example.
  • Research / Frontier. Exceptions such as chromium and copper are visible in the slide's table but not explained [S SLIDE 26].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Shell capacity 2n² is an accounting rule: n=1 → 2, n=2 → 8, n=3 → 18. Sodium has 11 electrons: 2 in K, 8 in L, 1 in M. Conservation check: 2+8+1 = 11 = atomic number. No energy is created; an electron occupying a lower orbital is at lower free energy, and excitation requires input of exactly the level spacing [S SLIDE 9–11; G for the energy wording].

Worked example — sodium configuration, arithmetic verified. Aufbau order gives 1s² (2 electrons), 2s² (2), 2p⁶ (6), 3s¹ (1) [S SLIDE 23]. Sum: 2+2+6+1 = 11. Independent check via shell notation 2,8,1: 2+8+1 = 11, matching atomic number 11 [S SLIDE 23]. Third check: shell capacity 2n² gives K=2, L=8, M=18; sodium fills K and L completely (10 electrons) and places the remaining 1 in M, so 10+1 = 11 — consistent. Chemical consequence: the single 3s electron is the valence electron.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Quantum rules determine which bonds an atom can make; biology is built on that.
  • Mechanistic Story. Model progression → quantised orbitals → filling rules → valence.
  • Clinical Story. Isotopes and metal ions in biology trace back to electronic structure.
  • MLS Story. No specimen layer; periodic table and configurations are the reference.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Thin; no drug at the atomic-model step.
  • Research Story. Many-electron systems remain hard.
  • Biotech Story. Isotope labelling and quantum-dot probes are applications [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Radioisotope production and stable-isotope supply are regulated industries [G].
  • Computational Story. Molecular-orbital calculations and protein electronic-structure models are computational tools [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Not applicable to atomic physics.
  • Innovation Story. Quantum-dot diagnostics are a translational avenue [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Electrons orbit like planets" is a teaching model, not reality [S SLIDE 9, 13]; "4s after 3d" is wrong [S SLIDE 23].
  • Memory Hook. n = shell, l = shape, m = direction, s = spin; 2n², then Aufbau–Pauli–Hund.
  • Connections. Electronic structure underlies the covalent bonds in carbonyls and the redox carriers in enzymes.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 3.pdf — SLIDE 3–6, SLIDE 11, SLIDE 14–25.

Carbohydrates: sugars, starch and glycogen

Location (Biological Geography). Plant chloroplast → starch granule → glucose polymer → glucose monomer → blood → liver/muscle cell → cytosol → glycogen granule → glycogen phosphorylase active site [S BIOL 157_Lecture 5 SLIDE 34–37]. Molecule lifecycle: glucose is made by photosynthesis [S SLIDE 3], travels in blood, is stored as glycogen mainly in liver (10% of liver mass) and skeletal muscle (1–2%) [S SLIDE 37], is released by phosphorylase cleavage and enters glycolysis [S SLIDE 35], and is oxidised to CO₂ and water [S BIOL 157_Lecture 8 SLIDE 4]. Glucose should never be absent from blood for long; its concentration is held at 3.9–5.5 mmol/L by insulin and glucagon [S SLIDE 19].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: glucose is chemically reactive (a reducing aldehyde) and osmotically active, so storing it as free monomers would disturb cellular osmolarity and cause glycation [G]. Polymerisation into starch or glycogen hides most anomeric carbons and stores the same carbon skeleton compactly [I from SLIDE 25, 33–37]. The thermodynamic problem of joining sugars in a water-rich environment is real: glucose + glucose → disaccharide + H₂O is a condensation that water opposes; prebiotic chemistry may have used condensing agents such as carbodiimides or polyphosphates like ATP [S BIOL 157_Lecture 2 SLIDE 33–35].

Just-in-time foundations. Polyhydroxy carbonyl compound = molecule with many –OH groups and a C=O group [S SLIDE 5]; aldose = sugar with an aldehyde group; ketose = sugar with a ketone group [S SLIDE 7]; glycosidic bond = acetal/ketal link between sugars [S SLIDE 18, 25]; reducing end = free anomeric carbon [S SLIDE 25]; amylose/amylopectin = linear and branched starch components [S SLIDE 34].

Concept 1 — Classification and naming.

  • Mechanistic. Formula Cₓ(H₂O)ᵧ; monosaccharides (CH₂O)ₙ with n = 3–7 [S SLIDE 5–6]. Naming by functional group: aldose or ketose; by carbon count: triose, tetrose, pentose, hexose; by configuration: D or L [S SLIDE 7–8]. Deoxyribose has one fewer oxygen [S SLIDE 7].
  • Historical / Research. The formose reaction is cited for prebiotic monosaccharide synthesis [S BIOL 157_Lecture 2 SLIDE 29]; no naming inventor given.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference: normal blood glucose 3.9–5.5 mmol/L [S SLIDE 19]; specimen: blood; method: glucose assay [G]. Error source: postprandial samples are higher [S SLIDE 19]; QC: fasting state must be recorded.
  • Clinical / Medical. Diabetes and hypoglycaemia are the failure states [G]; galactosaemia is caused by absent galactose-converting enzymes and leads to mental retardation, cataracts and early death if untreated [S SLIDE 22].
  • Pharmaceutical. Insulin and glucagon are the two hormones named at the control point [S SLIDE 19]; metformin reduces hepatic glucose production [G — named drug at the glucose-homeostasis step, outside slides].
  • Research / Frontier. Prebiotic sugar synthesis remains unresolved [S BIOL 157_Lecture 2 SLIDE 36].

Concept 2 — Disaccharides and digestion.

  • Mechanistic. Lactose is galactose-β-1,4-glucose, maltose glucose-α-1,4-glucose, sucrose glucose-α-1,2-fructose, cellobiose β-1,4 and isomaltose α-1,6 [S SLIDE 25]. Lactose must be split by lactase before absorption [S SLIDE 27]; sucrose by sucrase [S SLIDE 29]. Sucrose has no reducing end, lactose has a free one [S SLIDE 25, 29].
  • Historical / Research. No historical names; the slide lists bond notation as the organising idea [S SLIDE 25].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: stool or breath hydrogen test for lactose malabsorption [G]; reference: none in slides; error source: symptoms overlap with other causes [G].
  • Clinical / Medical. Lactose intolerance causes cramps, diarrhoea and dehydration; gut bacteria produce organic acids and CO₂ [S SLIDE 28]. High sucrose intake links to dental caries, obesity, diabetes, NAFLD and cardiovascular disease [S SLIDE 30].
  • Pharmaceutical. Lactase tablets hydrolyse lactose and prevent symptoms [S SLIDE 28] — a named enzyme therapy at the exact mechanism point.
  • Research / Frontier. Microbiome modification of lactose tolerance is open [G].

Concept 3 — Polysaccharides.

  • Mechanistic. Starch: 10–30% amylose, 70–90% amylopectin; amylose helix binds iodine to give a blue diagnostic colour [S SLIDE 33–34]. Salivary α-amylase splits α(1→4) only and is inactivated at stomach low pH; pancreatic α-amylase acts in the small intestine; α(1→6)-glucosidase cleaves branch points [S SLIDE 36]. Glycogen is highly branched every 8–12 residues [S SLIDE 37].
  • Historical / Research. No investigator named; the iodine test is given as diagnostic [S SLIDE 34].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: food or tissue; method: iodine colour test [S SLIDE 34]; reference: blue colour = starch [S SLIDE 34]; error source: heating swells granules and changes digestibility [S SLIDE 36].
  • Clinical / Medical. Glycogen storage diseases are failure states [G]; dietary fibre from cellulose is essential [S SLIDE 4].
  • Pharmaceutical. Acarbose inhibits α-glucosidase and slows starch digestion [G — named drug at the α-glucosidase step, outside slides].
  • Research / Frontier. Resistant starch and glycaemic index remain active nutrition questions [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Condensation of two glucose units: 2 × C₆H₁₂O₆ = C₁₂H₂₄O₁₂; remove H₂O → C₁₂H₂₂O₁₁, a disaccharide [I from SLIDE 33]. Hydrolysis returns the water. Carbon atoms are conserved: 6+6 in, 12 in the product. Energy accounting: about 4 kcal is released per gram when carbohydrate is oxidised [S SLIDE 3]; that energy was stored in C–H and C–C bonds of glucose and is captured in ATP through glycolysis [S SLIDE 35], not produced de novo.

Worked example — dietary carbohydrate arithmetic, verified. Recommended intake: 45–65% of calories from carbohydrate, with sucrose ≤10% [S SLIDE 4]. On a 2,000 kcal diet that is 900–1,300 kcal from carbohydrate; at 4 kcal/g [S SLIDE 3] this equals 225–325 g carbohydrate per day. Independent check: 45% of 2,000 = 900 and 900/4 = 225; 65% of 2,000 = 1,300 and 1,300/4 = 325 — both match. Sucrose ceiling: 10% of 2,000 = 200 kcal = 50 g, which is consistent with the slide's warning that large amounts promote obesity and tooth decay [S SLIDE 3]. Blood glucose check: 5.5 mmol/L × 180 g/mol ≈ 0.99 g/L [G molar mass], about 1 g per litre of blood.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Sugar size and bond type determine digestion, storage and disease.
  • Mechanistic Story. Condensation builds polymers; specific glycosidases reverse them.
  • Clinical Story. Diabetes, lactose intolerance, galactosaemia.
  • MLS Story. Glucose reference range, iodine test, fasting-state QC.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Insulin, glucagon, lactase tablets; acarbose added.
  • Research Story. Prebiotic polymerisation and microbiome effects are open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered amylases and glycogen pathways are industrial tools [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Starch, glucose syrup and lactase tablets are commodity products with purity specifications [G].
  • Computational Story. Glycaemic-response modelling and glycomics databases are computational tools [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Polymers were selected because they store carbon without osmotic cost.
  • Innovation Story. Low-glycaemic starch varieties are a translational target [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "All sugar is absorbed directly" is false — lactose and sucrose need hydrolysis [S SLIDE 27, 29]; "complex carbs are worse" is false — starch and fibre are recommended [S SLIDE 4].
  • Memory Hook. Aldehyde = aldose, ketone = ketose; α1→4 maltose, β1→4 lactose; amylose turns blue.
  • Connections. Glucose oxidation reappears in enzymes; glycogen regulation reappears in metabolic control.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 5.pdf — SLIDE 3–8, SLIDE 19–37.

Nucleic acids: nucleotides, Chargaff's rules and DNA structure

Location (Biological Geography). Nucleus → chromosome → chromatin → DNA double helix → nucleotide → sugar–phosphate backbone → nitrogenous base → hydrogen-bonding edge of guanine/cytosine [S SLIDE 24–29]. Molecule lifecycle: nucleotides are made in cells, polymerised into polynucleotides, ATP (a nucleotide) is used as an energy-rich product of catabolism [S SLIDE 3], DNA is stored in chromatin with histones [S SLIDE 11, 29], RNA is degraded by base-catalysed hydrolysis [S SLIDE 23], and bases are excreted as uric acid via peroxisomal urate oxidase [S 2025 BIOL 151 … SLIDE 207]. DNA should never appear as a free naked polymer in blood plasma in large amounts [I]; its normal residence is chromatin.

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: a molecule must store information stably yet be copyable. Chemistry solves this with a covalent sugar–phosphate backbone (stable, negatively charged) and weak hydrogen bonds between bases (separable) [S SLIDE 15, 25]. Directionality (5′ and 3′ ends) gives a reading frame [S SLIDE 15]. The negative charge forces association with histones and Mg²⁺ [S SLIDE 11]. Heating or pH 3/12 unwinds the duplex because base-pairing is weaker than the backbone [S SLIDE 11, 32], and the 2′-OH of RNA makes it chemically fragile while DNA lacks it [S SLIDE 23].

Just-in-time foundations. Nucleotide = phosphate ester of a pentose with a base at C1′ [S SLIDE 4]; nucleoside = nucleotide without phosphate [S SLIDE 6]; purine (adenine, guanine) and pyrimidine (cytosine, thymine, uracil) [S SLIDE 7]; phosphodiester bond = phosphate bridging 3′ and 5′ of successive sugars [S SLIDE 15]; Chargaff's rules = A=T, G=C [S SLIDE 17]; Tm = midpoint temperature of denaturation [S SLIDE 34].

Concept 1 — Nucleotides and bases.

  • Mechanistic. Ribonucleotides contain D-ribose; deoxyribonucleotides contain 2′-deoxy-D-ribose [S SLIDE 6]. Purines attach via N9, pyrimidines via N1 [S SLIDE 7]. NAD, FAD and coenzyme A are nucleotide derivatives required in many enzyme reactions [S SLIDE 4].
  • Historical / Research. Chargaff's base rules are named [S SLIDE 17]; Watson and Crick's double helix is outside slides [G].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: purified DNA/RNA; method: UV spectrophotometry; reference: A260/A280 ≈ 1.8 for DNA, 2.0 for RNA [S SLIDE 10]; error source: protein contamination lowers the ratio [G].
  • Clinical / Medical. Nucleoside analogues (e.g. zidovudine, aciclovir) block viral polymerases [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Zidovudine (AZT) and aciclovir are named analogues acting at the polymerisation step [G — outside slides]; slides only say analogues exist as a learning objective [S SLIDE 2].
  • Research / Frontier. Ribozymes catalyse reactions [S SLIDE 4]; their therapeutic use is open.

Concept 2 — DNA structure and Chargaff's rules.

  • Mechanistic. Polynucleotides have 3′ and 5′ ends; base pairs sit inside the helix, sugar–phosphate outside; complementary pairing is weak individually but collectively stable [S SLIDE 15, 25]. A=T and G=C; G–C pairs are triple-hydrogen-bonded and more stable than A–T pairs [S SLIDE 17, 37]. Base composition is characteristic of an organism: 25–75% G=C in bacteria, 39–46% in mammals [S SLIDE 18].
  • Historical / Research. Chargaff found composition independent of tissue, age and nutrition [S SLIDE 17]; unresolved: why composition varies so widely between species [I].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference range: mammalian G+C 39–46% [S SLIDE 18]; error source: assuming RNA obeys Chargaff — single-stranded RNA has no such constraint [S SLIDE 18].
  • Clinical / Medical. Base-pair complementarity is the basis of hybridisation tests [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin at the base-pair step; no drug named in slides.
  • Research / Frontier. A-, B- and Z-DNA forms and their biological roles are listed but not resolved [S SLIDE 27].

Concept 3 — Denaturation and stability.

  • Mechanistic. Heating collapses the duplex into random coils [S SLIDE 32]; UV absorbance rises about 40% (hyperchromic effect) as base stacking is disrupted [S SLIDE 33–34]; viscosity falls [S SLIDE 33]. Tm rises linearly with G–C fraction [S SLIDE 37]; alkaline pH lowers Tm [S SLIDE 37].
  • Historical / Research. The hyperchromic shift is described as a cooperative process [S SLIDE 34]; no investigator named.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: duplex DNA in solution; method: melting curve at 260 nm [S SLIDE 34–36]; reference: ~40% absorbance increase [S SLIDE 33]; error source: pH and ionic strength change Tm [S SLIDE 37]; QC: control solvent and ions [S SLIDE 37].
  • Clinical / Medical. PCR depends on denaturation; high-GC genomes need higher denaturation temperatures [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — none at the melting step.
  • Research / Frontier. Predicting Tm for edited genomes is an open computational problem [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Polymerisation conserves atoms: each phosphodiester bond forms by removing H₂O between the 3′-OH of one sugar and the 5′-phosphate of the next [S SLIDE 15; G for the H₂O bookkeeping]. Charge accounting: every phosphate is doubly ionised at physiological pH, so each added nucleotide adds negative charge; the backbone is a polyanion neutralised by histones, spermidine or Mg²⁺ [S SLIDE 6, 11]. Oxidation–reduction: NAD⁺ accepts electrons (reduced to NADH) in catabolism, NADPH donates them in biosynthesis [S BIOL 157_Lecture 8 SLIDE 19]. No energy is created; reduction of NAD⁺ stores reducing power in its nicotinamide ring [G].

Worked example — Chargaff and hydrogen bonds, arithmetic verified. If DNA is 30% adenine, then thymine = 30% [S SLIDE 17]. Remaining = 100 − 60 = 40%, split equally between G and C, so G = 20% and C = 20%. Verification: 30+30+20+20 = 100 ✓ and A=T, G=C ✓. Independent check on stability: per 100 base pairs, a 40% GC genome has 40×3 + 60×2 = 120+120 = 240 hydrogen bonds; a 60% GC genome has 60×3 + 40×2 = 180+80 = 260. Difference = 20 bonds per 100 bp, consistent with the slide's statement that Tm increases with G–C fraction [S SLIDE 37].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Stable covalent backbone + weak reversible base pairs = a copyable information store.
  • Mechanistic Story. Directionality, complementarity, cooperativity.
  • Clinical Story. Antiviral analogues, hybridisation tests, PCR.
  • MLS Story. A260/A280 purity, melting curves, pH/ion QC.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Zidovudine and aciclovir added; slides name analogues only.
  • Research Story. Z-DNA function and Tm prediction remain open.
  • Biotech Story. Restriction enzymes, PCR and sequencing are built on this chemistry [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Oligonucleotide synthesis and PCR reagents are produced under strict purity QA [G].
  • Computational Story. Genome assembly and primer design rely on complementarity rules [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. DNA won over RNA as archive because it lacks the hydrolytic 2′-OH [S SLIDE 23].
  • Innovation Story. Nucleic-acid diagnostics and editable genomes are major translational platforms [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "A pairs with U in DNA" is false — U is mainly RNA [S SLIDE 7]; "single-stranded RNA obeys Chargaff" is false [S SLIDE 18].
  • Memory Hook. Purine A–T two bonds, G–C three; 260 nm, ratio 1.8/2.0, hyperchromic 40%.
  • Connections. Urate from DNA base breakdown links to peroxisomes; NAD links to enzymes.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 6.pdf — SLIDE 3–10, SLIDE 11–18, SLIDE 23–37.

Proteins and amino acids: sequence into three-dimensional shape

Location (Biological Geography). Gene (DNA) → mRNA → ribosome → nascent polypeptide → endoplasmic reticulum → Golgi → folded protein → organelle or plasma membrane → active site with its catalytic residues [S BIOL 157_Lecture 6 SLIDE 29; G for the trafficking steps]. Molecule lifecycle: amino acids are absorbed from diet, activated and polymerised into proteins, proteins are degraded to amino acids, and nitrogen is excreted as urea [G]; the slides name the amino-acid forms that are degraded and the hormones thyroxine and triiodothyronine released from thyroglobulin [S SLIDE 15]. A protein should never appear in urine in large amounts; albuminuria signals glomerular failure [G].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: a one-dimensional sequence must become a three-dimensional machine. The slides state that proteins fold into 3D structures determined by amino-acid sequence, converting a 1D world of sequences into a 3D world of activity [S SLIDE 5]. Physics derives this from side-chain polarity: nonpolar groups bury away from water, charged groups stay surface-exposed [S SLIDE 10]; cysteine residues form disulphide cross-links that constrain the fold [S SLIDE 26]. Some proteins are rigid structural elements, others are flexible hinges and levers [S SLIDE 7].

Just-in-time foundations. R group = side chain giving an amino acid its identity [S SLIDE 8]; peptide bond = amide link between carboxyl and amino groups [S SLIDE 20]; essential amino acid = cannot be synthesised by higher animals [S SLIDE 13]; transamination = transfer of an amino group, whose absence explains essentiality [S SLIDE 13]; ninhydrin = oxidising reagent detecting α-amino groups [S SLIDE 24]; chromatography = separation by partitioning between phases [S SLIDE 32–33].

Concept 1 — Amino-acid structure and classes.

  • Mechanistic. A tetrahedral α-carbon carries amino, carboxyl, hydrogen and a variable R group [S SLIDE 8]. All 20 common amino acids except proline have free α-amino and α-carboxyl groups [S SLIDE 10]. Classification by R-group polarity: nonpolar; polar neutral; acidic (net negative at pH 7); basic (net positive) [S SLIDE 10].
  • Historical / Research. The 20-amino-acid set is given without a discovery narrative [S SLIDE 10]; rare amino acids such as hydroxyproline in collagen and iodinated tyrosine derivatives in thyroglobulin are named [S SLIDE 15].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: plasma or tissue hydrolysate; method: ion-exchange chromatography, GC, LC or HPLC [S SLIDE 34]; reference: ninhydrin product measured at 570 nm [S SLIDE 24]; error source: ionisation behaviour and solubility differences alter separation [S SLIDE 32].
  • Clinical / Medical. Kwashiorkor follows prolonged essential-amino-acid deficiency, with low plasma protein and haemoglobin [S SLIDE 14]. Phenylketonuria is an added example of amino-acid metabolism failure [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Histidine and arginine are semi-essential [S SLIDE 14]; levothyroxine (thyroxine) is a named drug replacing the thyroid hormone derived from iodinated tyrosine [G, outside slides].
  • Research / Frontier. Non-standard amino acids and their incorporation are an open field [G].

Concept 2 — Peptide formation and protein function.

  • Mechanistic. Proteins are polymers of amino acids and more than 50% of cell dry weight [S SLIDE 3, 5]; they catalyse, transport and store oxygen, support, protect immunologically, generate movement, transmit nerve impulses and control proliferation [S SLIDE 3]. The peptide bond forms between the amino and carboxyl groups [S SLIDE 20]; functional groups (alcohols, thiols, carboxylic acids, carboxamides, basic groups) give the reactivity needed for enzyme function [S SLIDE 6].
  • Historical / Research. No sequencing pioneer named; the slides emphasise the sequence-to-structure transition [S SLIDE 5].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Method: detect amino acids by ninhydrin, quantify at 570 nm [S SLIDE 24]; error source: proline reacts differently [I from SLIDE 10].
  • Clinical / Medical. Failure of folding causes loss of function [I]; sickle-cell disease is the classic sequence-change example [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Enzyme inhibitors and antibodies are therapeutic proteins; named examples outside slides include infliximab [G].
  • Research / Frontier. Predicting fold from sequence is the protein-folding problem [G].

Concept 3 — Analysis and spectroscopy.

  • Mechanistic. No amino acid absorbs visible light; several absorb UV and all absorb infrared [S SLIDE 29]. Aromatic amino acids phenylalanine, tyrosine and tryptophan absorb above 250 nm; tryptophan also shows phosphorescence [S SLIDE 30]. Chromatography separates by partition coefficient; ion-exchange resins with positive charges attract negative solutes (anion exchangers) and vice versa [S SLIDE 33, 35].
  • Historical / Research. HPLC is described as a recent advance [S SLIDE 34]; no inventor named.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: amino-acid mixture; method: ion exchange, GC, LC, HPLC [S SLIDE 34]; reference: 280 nm UV for aromatic residues (slide says above 250 nm) [S SLIDE 30]; QC: choose resin charge to match solute charge [S SLIDE 35].
  • Clinical / Medical. Amino-acid screening detects inborn errors of metabolism [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin at the spectroscopy step.
  • Research / Frontier. Single-molecule protein sequencing is emerging [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Peptide bond formation conserves atoms: amino group (−NH₂) plus carboxyl (−COOH) loses H₂O to become −CO−NH− [S SLIDE 20; G for formula]. Nitrogen accounting: every amino acid contributes one nitrogen in its α-amino group; proline's is secondary [S SLIDE 10]. No energy is created: peptide bond formation is driven by activated aminoacyl-tRNA and GTP hydrolysis in the ribosome [G]; the energy was stored in the high-energy ester bond and is coupled to bond formation. Disulphide formation oxidises two cysteine thiols to S–S [S SLIDE 26].

Worked example — peptide mass balance, arithmetic verified. A hypothetical decapeptide of ten glycines: free glycine is C₂H₅NO₂, molar mass ≈ 75.07 Da [G]; ten residues = 750.7 Da. Forming a chain of 10 residues requires 9 peptide bonds, releasing 9 × 18.015 = 162.1 Da of water. Peptide mass = 750.7 − 162.1 = 588.6 Da. Independent verification: general formula for n residues of mass M is nM − (n−1)×18; with n=10, M=75.07: 750.7 − 162.1 = 588.6 ✓. Atom check: 10 × C₂H₅NO₂ = C₂₀H₅₀N₁₀O₂₀; minus 9 H₂O = C₂₀H₃₂N₁₀O₁₁, and 20×12.01 + 32×1.008 + 10×14.01 + 11×16.00 ≈ 240.2+32.3+140.1+176.0 = 588.6 Da ✓.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Sequence determines fold; fold determines function; analysis reads both.
  • Mechanistic Story. R-group chemistry drives folding; peptide bonds build the chain.
  • Clinical Story. Kwashiorkor, sickle cell, inborn errors.
  • MLS Story. Ninhydrin at 570 nm, chromatography, UV above 250 nm.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Levothyroxine and infliximab added; slides name semi-essential amino acids only.
  • Research Story. Fold prediction and non-standard amino acids are open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered enzymes and therapeutic antibodies are core platforms [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Amino-acid production by fermentation and peptide synthesis are industrial processes with purity QA [G].
  • Computational Story. Protein structure prediction and sequence databases are computational tools [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. The 20-amino-acid set balances chemical versatility with coding economy [G].
  • Innovation Story. Protein-design platforms are translational opportunities [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "All amino acids absorb visible light" is false [S SLIDE 29]; "proline is like the others" is false — it lacks a free α-amino group [S SLIDE 10].
  • Memory Hook. α-carbon = amino + carboxyl + H + R; aromatic trio absorbs UV; 570 nm ninhydrin.
  • Connections. Amino-acid side chains are the reactive groups enzymes use; proteins are >50% of dry weight.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 7.pdf — SLIDE 3–10, SLIDE 13–14, SLIDE 20, SLIDE 24, SLIDE 30.

Enzymes: catalysts, names, cofactors and kinetics

Location (Biological Geography). Cell → cytosol/organelle → metabolic pathway → enzyme protein → active site → substrate → cofactor/ coenzyme → bound metal ion [S BIOL 157_Lecture 8 SLIDE 9, 14]. Molecule lifecycle: a coenzyme such as NAD⁺ is made from niacin in the diet [S SLIDE 17–19], travels in the cytosol, binds the enzyme, accepts electrons (becoming NADH) [S SLIDE 19], is regenerated, and is degraded [G]; prosthetic groups like FAD stay tightly bound [S SLIDE 22]. NAD⁺ should be high and NADH low in most cells, while NADPH is high and NADP low [S SLIDE 19] — reversed ratios signal a redox problem [I].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The physical problem: glucose oxidation is strongly exergonic (ΔG° = −2870 kJ/mol) yet essentially does not happen at room temperature because of the activation barrier [S SLIDE 4]. Enzymes lower that barrier without changing the equilibrium [G]; they work in dilute aqueous solution under mild temperature and pH [S SLIDE 8]. Selectivity comes from structural complementarity at the active site, so no substrate is diverted into wasteful side reactions [S SLIDE 9].

Just-in-time foundations. Substrate = substance an enzyme acts on [S SLIDE 9]; cofactor = non-protein helper, metal ion or organic coenzyme [S SLIDE 14]; prosthetic group = tightly bound coenzyme [S SLIDE 15]; holoenzyme/apoenzyme = with/without prosthetic group [S SLIDE 15]; vitamin = dietary compound the organism cannot make fast enough [S SLIDE 17]; Km = Michaelis constant reflecting binding [S SLIDE 28–30].

Concept 1 — Catalytic power and specificity.

  • Mechanistic. Enzymes accelerate reactions up to 10¹⁶-fold [S SLIDE 8]; a rate constant of 3×10⁴ s⁻¹ versus 3×10⁻¹⁰ s⁻¹ at 20 °C gives relative catalytic power 10¹⁴ [S SLIDE 8]. Substrate recognition uses structural complementarity at the active site [S SLIDE 9]. Regulatory enzymes sit at pathway junctions and sense metabolic need [S SLIDE 6].
  • Historical / Research. Michaelis and Menten proposed their theory in 1913 [S SLIDE 28]; International Commission on Enzymes established systematic nomenclature in 1956 [S SLIDE 12].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: enzyme assay; method: measure velocity at varying substrate [S SLIDE 27]; reference: Vmax and Km [S SLIDE 25]; error source: temperature/pH drift changes rate [I from SLIDE 8].
  • Clinical / Medical. Elevated cardiac enzymes are diagnostic of infarction [G]; rate control is how drugs work [S SLIDE 26].
  • Pharmaceutical. Statins inhibit HMG-CoA reductase; aspirin irreversibly acetylates cyclooxygenase [G — named drugs at exact enzymatic steps, outside slides].
  • Research / Frontier. Designing artificial enzymes is open [G].

Concept 2 — Nomenclature and cofactors.

  • Mechanistic. Trivial names add -ase to substrate (urease, phosphatase); others like catalase, trypsin and pepsin are historical [S SLIDE 11]. Systematic EC names use four numbers and seven reaction classes — chymotrypsin EC 3.4.21.1, trypsin EC 3.4.21.4 [S SLIDE 12]. Cofactors are heat-stable relative to proteins [S SLIDE 14].
  • Historical / Research. The 1956 commission replaced trivial naming [S SLIDE 12]; unresolved issues: not stated.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: serum; reference: enzyme activity in U/L [G]; error source: prosthetic group loss yields inactive apoenzyme [S SLIDE 15]; QC: assay holoenzyme, not apoenzyme [I].
  • Clinical / Medical. Vitamin-deficiency diseases reflect missing coenzymes: beriberi from thiamine deficiency [S SLIDE 24].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thiamine replacement in beriberi, niacin in pellagra, riboflavin in deficiency [G]; slides name the vitamin–coenzyme pairs [S SLIDE 19–24].
  • Research / Frontier. Moonlighting enzyme functions are open [G].

Concept 3 — Kinetics and vitamins as coenzymes.

  • Mechanistic. NAD/NADP are soluble electron carriers; NADH mainly catabolic, NADPH biosynthetic [S SLIDE 19]. FMN and FAD are prosthetic groups using the isoalloxazine ring to accept or transfer electrons one at a time [S SLIDE 22]. Biotin transfers single carbon units as CO₂ in carboxylases [S SLIDE 23]. Thiamine pyrophosphate is crucial in oxidative decarboxylation, including pyruvate dehydrogenase [S SLIDE 24]. Michaelis–Menten: E + S ⇌ ES → E + P, with Ks and Km [S SLIDE 28–30].
  • Historical / Research. TPP formed in an ATP-dependent reaction [S SLIDE 24]; coenzyme classification by vitamin is given [S SLIDE 17–24].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: blood; method: activity assay; reference: NAD > NADH and NADPH > NADP [S SLIDE 19]; error source: prosthetic-group dissociation during sample handling [I from SLIDE 15].
  • Clinical / Medical. Pharmacological inhibition of a rate-determining enzyme controls a pathway [S SLIDE 26].
  • Pharmaceutical. Named at the exact mechanism points: NAD/NADP from niacin [S]; FAD/FMN from riboflavin [S]; TPP from thiamine [S]; added drugs: metformin (mitochondrial complex I), allopurinol (xanthine oxidase) [G].
  • Research / Frontier. Drug design uses kinetic parameters [S SLIDE 26].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Glucose oxidation transfers electrons from carbon in glucose to O₂, forming CO₂ and H₂O; the products sit at lower free energy by 2870 kJ/mol [S SLIDE 4] — that free energy was stored in glucose bonds and is captured in ATP, not created [G]. NAD⁺ accepts a hydride (two electrons plus H) in catabolism; FAD accepts two hydrogen atoms one electron at a time [S SLIDE 19, 22]. Biotin carries a single CO₂ unit: carbon atoms are conserved from bicarbonate to carboxylated product [S SLIDE 23; G for conservation wording]. Thiamine's TPP stabilises a carbanion during decarboxylation, so the removed carbon leaves as CO₂ [S SLIDE 24].

Worked example — catalytic power, arithmetic verified. Slide values: catalysed rate constant k_cat = 3×10⁴ s⁻¹, uncatalysed k_uncat = 3×10⁻¹⁰ s⁻¹ at 20 °C [S SLIDE 8]. Ratio = (3×10⁴)/(3×10⁻¹⁰) = (3/3)×10^(4−(−10)) = 1×10¹⁴. Independent check by logarithms: log₁₀(3×10⁴) = 4.4771; log₁₀(3×10⁻¹⁰) = −9.5229; difference = 14.0000, so ratio = 10¹⁴ ✓. This matches the slide's stated relative catalytic power of 10¹⁴ and sits below the 10¹⁶ upper range quoted in the same slide [S SLIDE 8].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Enzymes lower barriers and confer specificity; coenzymes supply chemistry proteins lack.
  • Mechanistic Story. Active-site recognition → ES complex → product; vitamins supply redox and carrier groups.
  • Clinical Story. Deficiency diseases and drug inhibition.
  • MLS Story. Activity assays, temperature and pH control, holoenzyme integrity.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Statins, aspirin, metformin, allopurinol added at named steps; vitamin replacement.
  • Research Story. Artificial enzymes and kinetic drug design are open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered enzymes and directed evolution are industrial tools [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Enzyme production by fermentation and immobilisation are established processes [G].
  • Computational Story. Enzyme kinetics modelling and structure-guided design are computational [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Catalytic antibodies and ribozymes hint at an RNA-world past [G].
  • Innovation Story. Enzyme replacement and designer biocatalysts are translational platforms [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Enzymes change equilibrium" is false — they change rate [G]; "all enzymes need vitamins" is false — many work from protein structure alone [S SLIDE 14].
  • Memory Hook. 10¹⁴ power, EC 3.4.21.4, NAD catabolic/NADP anabolic, Km = ES.
  • Connections. Enzyme regulation is the mechanism behind metabolic control; NAD links back to nucleic acids.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 8.pdf — SLIDE 4, SLIDE 8–15, SLIDE 19–24, SLIDE 26–30.

Lipids: fatty acids, triacylglycerols and membranes

Location (Biological Geography). Diet → intestine → chylomicron → blood → adipose tissue → adipocyte → lipid droplet → triacylglycerol → fatty acid → mitochondrial matrix → β-oxidation active site [S BIOL 157_Lecture 10 SLIDE 13, 16; G for transport steps]. Molecule lifecycle: fatty acids are esterified to glycerol and stored as triacylglycerol in adipose tissue [S SLIDE 6, 13], hydrolysed by lipases, oxidised, and the carbon skeleton leaves as CO₂ [G]; phospholipids such as lecithin are built into membranes [S SLIDE 18]. Cholesterol is a derived lipid [S SLIDE 5]. Fatty acids should not appear free in large amounts in blood; they are mostly esterified [S SLIDE 6].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: carbon in its most reduced form stores the most chemical potential per gram, but it is hydrophobic. Lipids solve both: highly reduced hydrocarbons oxidise with a large free-energy drop [S SLIDE 4, 16], and their hydrophobicity lets them aggregate without water, so storage is anhydrous [S SLIDE 16]. Amphipathic phospholipids solve a second problem — a barrier that polar molecules cannot cross [S SLIDE 4]. Cis double bonds introduce a kink that prevents tight packing [S SLIDE 9], which is a membrane-fluidity solution to a packing problem [I].

Just-in-time foundations. Amphipathic = having both polar and nonpolar groups [S SLIDE 4]; saponification = alkaline hydrolysis of acylglycerols yielding fatty-acid salts and glycerol [S SLIDE 15]; triacylglycerol = glycerol esterified with three fatty acids [S SLIDE 13]; sphingosine = 18-carbon amino alcohol replacing glycerol in sphingolipids [S SLIDE 23]; ganglioside = glycosphingolipid with three or more sugars including sialic acid [S SLIDE 27].

Concept 1 — Fatty acids.

  • Mechanistic. Long hydrocarbon tail plus terminal ionised carboxyl group [S SLIDE 6]; usually even carbon numbers 14–24 [S SLIDE 6]. Saturated = all single bonds; unsaturated = one or more double bonds, nearly always cis, producing a kink [S SLIDE 6, 9]. Naming: systematic (octadecanoic), common (stearic), shorthand 18:0 [S SLIDE 8].
  • Historical / Research. No discoverer named; the slide distinguishes systematic, common and shorthand names [S SLIDE 8].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: plasma or tissue lipid extract; method: chromatography [G]; reference: none in slides; error source: oxidation of unsaturated chains during storage [G].
  • Clinical / Medical. Essential fatty acids linoleic and γ-linolenic must be eaten; arachidonic acid is made from linoleic acid [S SLIDE 10].
  • Pharmaceutical. Eicosanoids such as prostaglandins are hormone-like products of arachidonic acid [S SLIDE 10]; aspirin inhibits their synthesis at cyclooxygenase [G].
  • Research / Frontier. Odd-chain and branched fatty acids in marine organisms and microbes remain a niche topic [S SLIDE 6, 12].

Concept 2 — Triacylglycerols and energy storage.

  • Mechanistic. Triacylglycerols are glycerol plus three fatty acids; simple if all three are the same (triolein), mixed otherwise [S SLIDE 13]; stored chiefly in adipose tissue [S SLIDE 13]. Hydrolysis by acid, base or lipases; alkali hydrolysis is saponification [S SLIDE 15]. Soluble in benzene, chloroform, ether and hot ethanol; insoluble in water [S SLIDE 15].
  • Historical / Research. Potash from wood ashes was historically used to hydrolyse animal fat [S SLIDE 15] — a real historical anchor.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: adipose tissue or serum; reference: 1 g triacylglycerol ≈ 38 kJ versus 17 kJ/g for protein and carbohydrate [S SLIDE 16]; error source: hydration of glycogen makes it a poor storage comparison [S SLIDE 16].
  • Clinical / Medical. Body fat insulates; whales and Arctic mammals rely on it for insulation and energy [S SLIDE 16].
  • Pharmaceutical. Orlistat inhibits pancreatic lipase and reduces fat absorption [G — named drug at the hydrolysis step, outside slides].
  • Research / Frontier. Brown adipose tissue thermogenesis is outside slides [G].

Concept 3 — Phospholipids and sphingolipids.

  • Mechanistic. Phosphatidic acid is glycerol-3-phosphate with fatty acids at positions 1 and 2 [S SLIDE 17]; head groups choline and ethanolamine give phosphatidylcholine (lecithin) and phosphatidylethanolamine (cephalin), common membrane constituents [S SLIDE 18]; cardiolipin was first seen in heart tissue [S SLIDE 18]. Ether glycerophospholipids include platelet-activating factor, with a 16-carbon alkyl chain at C1 and a 2-carbon acetate at C2, making it more water-soluble [S SLIDE 20]. Sphingomyelins are phosphorus-containing sphingolipids important in nervous tissue [S SLIDE 23]; gangliosides include sialic acid (N-acetyl neuraminic acid) and carry a net negative charge [S SLIDE 27].
  • Historical / Research. Cardiolipin's discovery in heart tissue is recorded [S SLIDE 18]; Tay-Sachs GM2 accumulation is named [S SLIDE 27].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: nerve or muscle membrane; method: lipid extraction and chromatography [G]; error source: gangliosides are present in small amounts and easily missed [S SLIDE 27].
  • Clinical / Medical. Tay-Sachs disease has a red spotted retina, blindness and weight loss [S SLIDE 27]; glycosphingolipids determine cell recognition and tissue immunity [S SLIDE 28].
  • Pharmaceutical. No lipid-lowering drug is named in the slides; statins and fibrates are added [G].
  • Research / Frontier. Glycosphingolipid roles in immunity are still being defined [S SLIDE 28].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Complete oxidation of 1 g triacylglycerol yields about 38 kJ because its carbons are highly reduced [S SLIDE 16]; the energy was stored in C–H bonds and is captured when electrons pass to O₂ via the respiratory chain [G] — it is not produced. In saponification, atoms are conserved: the ester bonds are cleaved by OH⁻, glycerol is regenerated and each fatty acid gains Na⁺ [S SLIDE 15]. Phosphate accounting in a phospholipid: one phosphate bridges glycerol and the head group; the molecule carries a negative charge at physiological pH [I from SLIDE 17–18].

Worked example — saponification balance, arithmetic verified. Triolein = C₅₇H₁₀₄O₆ [G formula]. With 3 NaOH: left side atoms = C₅₇, H = 104+3 = 107, O = 6+3 = 9, Na = 3. Products: glycerol C₃H₈O₃ plus 3 sodium oleate C₁₈H₃₃NaO₂ = C₃H₈O₃ + C₅₄H₉₉Na₃O₆ = C₅₇H₁₀₇Na₃O₉. Atom-by-atom comparison: C 57=57 ✓, H 107=107 ✓, O 9=9 ✓, Na 3=3 ✓. Energy check: 10 g triacylglycerol × 38 kJ/g = 380 kJ; the same 10 g of carbohydrate × 17 kJ/g = 170 kJ; ratio 380/170 ≈ 2.24, matching the slide's claim that lipids are the superior storage form [S SLIDE 16].

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Hydrophobicity is the organising principle of lipid structure and function.
  • Mechanistic Story. Esterification stores carbon; amphipathicity builds bilayers; cis kinks keep them fluid.
  • Clinical Story. Essential fatty acid deficiency, Tay-Sachs, obesity.
  • MLS Story. Lipid extraction, chromatography, 38 versus 17 kJ/g reference.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Aspirin at cyclooxygenase, orlistat at lipase; statins/fibrates added.
  • Research Story. Immune roles of glycosphingolipids remain open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered oilseed crops and lipid nanoparticles are platforms [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Soap and margarine are direct industrial applications of saponification and hydrogenation [S 2026 Alkenes SLIDE 7; G for soap scale].
  • Computational Story. Lipidomics and membrane simulations are computational tools [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Reduced carbon was selected as the densest portable fuel.
  • Innovation Story. Lipid nanoparticle delivery is a major translational platform [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "All lipids are fats" is false — phospholipids and steroids are lipids [S SLIDE 5]; "unsaturated fats pack tightly" is false — cis kinks prevent it [S SLIDE 9].
  • Memory Hook. 18:0 stearic, 18:1 oleic; 38 versus 17 kJ/g; sphingosine replaces glycerol.
  • Connections. peroxisomal oxidation of fatty acids links back to the Cell Structure section; arachidonic acid links to eicosanoids.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 10.pdf — SLIDE 3–10, SLIDE 13–18, SLIDE 23.

Metabolic pathways and how cells control them

Location (Biological Geography). Organism → organ (liver) → tissue (hepatocyte) → cell compartments (cytosol, mitochondrion, nucleus) → enzyme → active site → ligand-binding regulatory site [S BIOL 157_Lecture 9 SLIDE 8, 19]. Molecule lifecycle: a metabolite enters a pathway, is committed at the first irreversible step, passes through near-equilibrium steps, and exits as product [S SLIDE 7]; hormones are made in specialised cells, travel in blood, bind receptors and trigger second messengers [S SLIDE 24, 26]. Glucose should not be trapped outside the cell once phosphorylated [I from SLIDE 13].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: thousands of reactions must coexist without futile cycling. Physics/thermodynamics gives the answer: each pathway is strongly exergonic and therefore directional, and the reverse route must be different so the two can be regulated independently [S SLIDE 7]. Flux is controlled at the slowest, irreversible, committed step [S SLIDE 8] — an engineering bottleneck analogy. Compartmentation adds physical separation with selective membrane permeability [S SLIDE 22].

Just-in-time foundations. Metabolite = substrate of an enzyme in a pathway [S SLIDE 5]; metabolon = complex of consecutively acting enzymes [S SLIDE 5]; committed step = irreversible reaction committing an intermediate [S SLIDE 7]; allosteric = regulation at a site other than the active site [S SLIDE 19]; second messenger = intracellular molecule such as cAMP [S SLIDE 26].

Concept 1 — Pathway architecture.

  • Mechanistic. A pathway is a series of connected enzymatic reactions producing a specific product, with over 2,000 reactions catalysed by distinct enzymes [S SLIDE 3]. Enzymes may be separated with intermediates diffusing, organised in multienzyme metabolons, or membrane-bound [S SLIDE 3, 5]. Pathways are irreversible, have a first committed step, are regulated and occupy specific locations [S SLIDE 7–8].
  • Historical / Research. No names; the slide gives the modern principles [S SLIDE 7–8].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference: >2,000 distinct enzyme-catalysed reactions [S SLIDE 3]; error source: assuming a pathway is freely reversible [S SLIDE 7].
  • Clinical / Medical. Inborn errors block a single step and cause substrate accumulation [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Inhibiting a committed step is a drug strategy [S SLIDE 19; G for named examples such as statins at HMG-CoA reductase].
  • Research / Frontier. Metabolon organisation is an active structural question [S SLIDE 5].

Concept 2 — Reaction types.

  • Mechanistic. Six classes: oxidation–reduction (succinate → fumarate reduces FAD to FADH₂), ligation (ATP-driven bond formation), isomerisation (citrate hydroxyl shift), group transfer (ATP phosphorylates glucose, trapping it), hydrolysis (protein digestion), lyase (fructose 1,6-bisphosphate → DHAP + glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate) [S SLIDE 10–15].
  • Historical / Research. Enzyme classes mirror EC classification from the Enzymes lecture [S SLIDE 12 there]; no new names.
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Specimen: tissue or blood; method: enzyme activity assays [G]; error source: ATP depletion stops ligation and transfer steps [I from SLIDE 11, 13].
  • Clinical / Medical. Failure of group transfer traps or fails to trap glucose [I from SLIDE 13].
  • Pharmaceutical. Aspirin at cyclooxygenase is an added ligation-pathway example [G].
  • Research / Frontier. Enzyme promiscuity is outside slides [G].

Concept 3 — Control mechanisms.

  • Mechanistic. Enzyme induction can raise abundance from <1 to thousands of molecules per cell (lac operon) [S SLIDE 17–18]. Allosteric effectors bind regulatory sites and alter subunit interactions [S SLIDE 19]; covalent control is mainly phosphorylation of Ser/Thr/Tyr, also adenylation and ADP-ribosylation [S SLIDE 20]. Compartmentation uses membrane permeability [S SLIDE 22]. Steroid hormones enter cells and regulate transcription; glucagon triggers cAMP [S SLIDE 24, 26].
  • Historical / Research. The lac operon is the named historical model [S SLIDE 18]; cAMP as second messenger is named [S SLIDE 26].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference: RNA polymerases in nucleus, respiratory enzymes in mitochondria [S SLIDE 22]; error source: mixing compartments in an assay [I].
  • Clinical / Medical. Diabetes is a hormonal control failure [G]; glucagon and insulin oppose each other [S Carbohydrates SLIDE 19].
  • Pharmaceutical. Glucagon is itself a drug used in hypoglycaemia [G]; insulin is named in the Carbohydrates lecture [S there SLIDE 19].
  • Research / Frontier. Network-level metabolic control remains an open modelling problem [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Phosphorylation of glucose: ATP loses its terminal phosphate to become ADP, glucose gains it to become glucose-6-phosphate [S SLIDE 13]; three phosphates before, two plus one after — conserved. Electron accounting: succinate oxidation removes two hydrogen atoms and reduces FAD to FADH₂ [S SLIDE 10]; succinate is the electron donor, FAD the acceptor, and the reduced coenzyme carries the stored reducing power into the electron-transport chain [G]. ATP cleavage does not create energy; the free energy stored in the phosphoanhydride bond is coupled to bond formation [S SLIDE 11; G for wording].

Worked example — phosphate conservation, arithmetic verified. One ATP molecule contains 3 phosphate groups [G]. Transfer by a transferase to glucose [S SLIDE 13] yields glucose-6-phosphate (1 phosphate) + ADP (2 phosphates). Balance: 3 = 1 + 2 ✓. Independent check on charge: at physiological pH ATP⁴⁻ and ADP³⁻ [G]; adding a phosphate (≈−2) to neutral glucose gives G6P²⁻, so charge is conserved once released protons are counted [G]. The atom count alone verifies the transfer.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Direction, commitment, location and regulation make metabolism controllable.
  • Mechanistic Story. Exergonic steps give direction; allostery and phosphorylation set flux.
  • Clinical Story. Diabetes, enzyme induction, inborn errors.
  • MLS Story. Activity assays, compartment-specific reference localisation.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Glucagon, insulin, statins at committed steps; all named.
  • Research Story. Metabolons and network control are open.
  • Biotech Story. Engineered pathways for biosynthesis are a core platform [G].
  • Manufacturing Story. Industrial fermentation exploits enzyme induction [G].
  • Computational Story. Flux-balance analysis models pathway control [G].
  • Evolutionary Story. Separate forward and reverse pathways allow independent regulation — an evolved control solution [S SLIDE 7].
  • Innovation Story. Synthetic metabolic pathways for drug precursors are translational [G].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Pathways run backwards easily" is false [S SLIDE 7]; "all control is transcriptional" is false — allostery is faster [S SLIDE 19].
  • Memory Hook. Committed step, allostery, phosphorylation, compartment, hormone.
  • Connections. Glycolysis from Carbohydrates feeds here; enzyme inhibition from the Enzymes section is the drug mechanism.

Source: BIOL 157_Lecture 9.pdf — SLIDE 3–8, SLIDE 10–15, SLIDE 17–26.

Alkenes and addition reactions

Location (Biological Geography). This is a molecular-chemistry topic with no organ level: reagent → π bond → carbocation intermediate → substituted product; the "active site" analogue is the electron-rich π bond that attracts an electrophile [S 2026 Alkenes SLIDE 9–11]. Biological example from the slides: neocembrene is a termite trail marker and an aphid alarm pheromone is an alkene [S SLIDE 2]; calicheamicin, a potent antitumour agent from bacteria, contains alkene-related structure [S SLIDE 2]. Molecule lifecycle for a drug alkene: made by synthesis or biosynthesis, travels to its target, reacts at the double bond or binds a receptor, is metabolised and excreted [G].

Engineering / physics-first framing. The problem: a σ bond is strong and inert, but a π bond is exposed and weaker, so it is a reactive handle [S SLIDE 3–4]. Electrophilic addition exploits electron density: an electrophile E⁺ attacks the π electrons, forming a carbocation, then a nucleophile X⁻ adds [S SLIDE 9–10]. Regiochemistry follows carbocation stability — Markovnikov's rule is the observable consequence [S SLIDE 11]. Hydrogenation of fats converts C=C to C–C, raising melting point and giving margarine [S SLIDE 7].

Just-in-time foundations. Alkene = hydrocarbon with C=C [S SLIDE 3]; π bond = the exposed electron pair above/below the C=C axis [S SLIDE 5]; electrophile = electron-seeking species E⁺ [S SLIDE 9]; carbocation = positively charged carbon intermediate [S SLIDE 9]; regioselective = one product favoured [S SLIDE 12].

Concept 1 — Structure, naming and properties.

  • Mechanistic. Replace alkane "-ane" with "-ene": ethene, propene, cyclohexene, 1,3-butadiene [S SLIDE 4]. Exposed π electrons interact slightly with water, so alkenes are more soluble than alkanes but still nonpolar; boiling points similar to parent alkanes [S SLIDE 5].
  • Historical / Research. Vinyl chloride and allyl bromide are named trivial groups [S SLIDE 5]; Markovnikov (1838–1904) is named [S SLIDE 11].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. No clinical specimen; the relevant "reference" is nomenclature [I]. Error source: wrong locant for the double bond [I].
  • Clinical / Medical. Vinyl chloride is an industrial carcinogen [G]; alkene-containing natural products act as pheromones [S SLIDE 2].
  • Pharmaceutical. Calicheamicin is named as a potent antitumour agent from bacteria [S SLIDE 2]; no synthetic alkene drug is named.
  • Research / Frontier. Alkene metathesis is a modern synthetic frontier [G].

Concept 2 — Addition reactions.

  • Mechanistic. Reagents add to the π bond with rehybridisation and no loss of atoms; examples H₂/Pt, Cl₂, HCl, H₂O/H⁺ [S SLIDE 7–9]. Mechanism: step 1 electrophilic attack forms an intermediate, step 2 nucleophile adds [S SLIDE 9]. Hydrogenation of fats and oils gives margarine [S SLIDE 7].
  • Historical / Research. The slide defines a mechanism as a step-by-step account and distinguishes intermediates from products [S SLIDE 9].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. No specimen layer; error source: treating an intermediate as a product [S SLIDE 9].
  • Clinical / Medical. Partial hydrogenation historically produced trans fats [G]; slides mention cis kinks in fatty acids [S Lipids SLIDE 9].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — no named drug at the addition step in these slides.
  • Research / Frontier. Catalytic asymmetric hydrogenation is a major frontier [G].

Concept 3 — Markovnikov regiochemistry.

  • Mechanistic. In ionic addition of HX, hydrogen attaches to the carbon already bearing more hydrogens, X to the more substituted carbon — "them that has, gets" [S SLIDE 10–11]. Slow protonation is followed by fast nucleophilic capture [S SLIDE 12]. Regioselective means one product predominates; regiospecific means only one forms [S SLIDE 12].
  • Historical / Research. Markovnikov's rule is attributed with dates [S SLIDE 11]; the mechanistic carbocation explanation is later general knowledge [G].
  • MLS / Diagnostic. Reference: major versus minor product ratio is not quantified in slides [I]; error source: applying the rule to radical (anti-Markovnikov) conditions [G].
  • Clinical / Medical. Predicting the major metabolite of an alkene drug matters in toxicology [G].
  • Pharmaceutical. Thin — no named drug; the slide's own example is 2-methylpropene plus HBr [S SLIDE 10].
  • Research / Frontier. Radical additions (Kharasch effect) are outside slides [G].

Atomic / Electron / Energy Accounting. Addition conserves atoms: C=C plus H–X gives C–H and C–X with no atom lost [S SLIDE 7]. Electron accounting: the π bond donates electrons to H⁺, so the alkene is the electron donor and H⁺ the acceptor; the resulting carbocation accepts electrons from X⁻ [I from SLIDE 9]. The C=C bond energy is replaced by two σ bonds; the net free-energy change is negative, so the reaction proceeds without external energy input [G]. Hydrogenation releases the stored chemical potential of the π bond as heat [G].

Worked example — Markovnikov product balance, arithmetic verified. Reactants: 2-methylpropene C₄H₈ + HBr [S SLIDE 10]. Product 2-bromo-2-methylpropane = C₄H₉Br. Atom check: C 4=4 ✓, H 8+1=9 ✓, Br 1=1 ✓. Degree of unsaturation: alkene has 1 (one double bond), product has 0, consistent with addition across π [G].
