MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: the slides do not give specimens for traditional practice [SLIDES SILENT]. [K] For a modern laboratory assessing a herbal product, specimens are the raw plant, a finished dose form, or a blood/urine sample from a user [K]. [K] Method: microscopy, HPLC or LC-MS to identify and quantify markers; microbial and heavy-metal testing [K]. [K] Reference range: for lead in herbal medicines, WHO limits are commonly cited around 10 mg/kg; the slides give no numbers [K]. [S] Error sources explicitly stated: the slides note that evidence-based practice marginalised traditional methods, implying a knowledge gap rather than a laboratory error [I]. [K] QC: authenticate botanically before chemical assay [K].

Clinical/medical layer. [I] The clinical relevance is that patients may use traditional remedies alongside prescribed drugs — hidden exposure. [K] Documented interactions: Hypericum perforatum (St John's wort) induces CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein, reducing ciclosporin and some antiretroviral levels; Ginkgo biloba increases bleeding risk with warfarin [K]. [K] The Zulu concept of imbalance between body, mind and spirit maps onto modern biopsychosocial models, but the slide's spiritual causal claims are not biomedical facts [I].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [S] Artemisinin and quinine are named elsewhere in the ethnobotany deck as plant-derived antimalarials; their mechanisms are model knowledge: artemisinin's endoperoxide bridge is activated by haem/Fe²⁺ and forms radicals that damage parasite proteins; quinine accumulates in the parasite food vacuole and inhibits haemozoin formation [S + K]. [K] Azadirachta indica (neem) and Zingiber officinale (ginger) are commonly used Ghanaian remedies; gingerols inhibit cyclooxygenase [K] — labeled because the slides do not give mechanisms.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: rigorous effectiveness trials of whole traditional formulations; standardisation of polyherbal preparations; and benefit-sharing with communities whose knowledge is used [K]. [S] The issue of commercialisation and benefit sharing is raised in the ethnobotany slides (SLIDE 12).

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Artemisinin's activation is redox chemistry: the endoperoxide O–O bond accepts an electron from Fe²⁺-haem and breaks, forming an oxygen radical that covalently modifies parasite proteins — electron donor haem/Fe²⁺, acceptor the peroxide bond [K]. [K] Energy is stored in that strained peroxide bond; cleavage releases radicals, not "energy produced" [K]. [I] For quinine, the relevant accounting is haem: haemoglobin digestion releases haem, which is normally detoxified to haemozoin; quinine blocks that crystallisation, leaving toxic haem [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Usage statistics from SLIDE 3: Africa overall >80% reliance; Ghana 70–80%; Ethiopia 90%. Compare a cohort of 1,000 Ghanaian adults using the midpoint 75%: 1,000 × 0.75 = 750 users and 250 non-users ✓. Using the upper bound 80%: 800 users; lower bound 70%: 700 — a 100-person spread, showing why the slide gives a range rather than a point estimate [I]. [S] Ethiopia's 90% gives 900 of 1,000 ✓, higher than Ghana's whole range, consistent with the slide's claim.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Traditional medicine is a locally available health system whose plant materials are also drug leads.
  • Mechanistic Story. Plant material → preparation → active molecule → molecular target → clinical effect.
  • Clinical Story. Unrecorded co-use creates interaction risk; spiritual explanations differ from biomedical ones.
  • MLS Story. Authenticate and test botanicals; no diagnostic specimen is specified on the slides.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Artemisinin, quinine and gingerols are named molecules with defined targets.
  • Research Story. Effectiveness trials, standardisation and benefit-sharing are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Metabolic engineering of Artemisia or yeast raises artemisinin precursor supply [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Herbal medicines need GMP, batch standardisation and contaminant limits [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Ethnobotanical databases and network pharmacology map plants to targets [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Plants evolved these compounds as defences; humans co-opted them [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Ghanaian plant-derived products and nutraceuticals are commercial opportunities [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Traditional means harmless" — botanicals have real pharmacology; "80% means proven effective" — it means usage, not efficacy.
  • Memory Hook. Ubuntu, holism, spiritualism; sangoma, laibon, bokaye.
  • Connections. Access constraint → plant use → active molecule → interaction risk → standardisation.

Source: BHM 151 TRADITIONAL MEDICINE PRACTICES IN AFRICA.pptx — SLIDE 3.

Ethnobotany and drug discovery

Biological geography. [S] Ethnobotany is the scientific study of relationships between people and plants (ETHNOBOTANY-151 SLIDE 3). Zoom: culture → community → field site (rainforest) → plant → organ (bark, leaf) → tissue (trichome) → cell → metabolite → molecular target [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle for quinine: it is an alkaloid made in Cinchona bark, extracted as a crude bark preparation, isolated as a pure salt, distributed as tablets, metabolised by CYP3A4 and excreted renally [K]. [S] The slides stress that traditional collection, extraction and preparation methods may be the key to safe use (SLIDE 13).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is search efficiency: screening random plants for activity is expensive, so traditional use is a heuristic that narrows the search space. [I] Ethnobotanical knowledge acts as a prior probability on which species are worth testing. [K] Physically, extraction is a mass-transfer problem: solvent polarity, particle size and temperature determine how much of a compound moves from plant matrix into solution [K]. [S] Bioassay-guided fractionation is named as the method that follows the active effect through separations (SLIDE 20).

Just-in-time foundations. Ethnobotany = ethnology (culture) + botany (plants) [S]; ethnobiology = study of past and present human–organism relationships; ethnoecology = study of people–environment interactions (SLIDE 5); voucher specimen = a pressed, dried reference plant deposited in a herbarium (SLIDE 15); bioassay-guided fractionation = using a biological test to track active compounds through purification (SLIDE 20); biopiracy = unauthorised commercial use of traditional knowledge [K]; CBD = Convention on Biological Diversity; Nagoya Protocol = benefit-sharing rules (SLIDE 25).

Mechanistic layer. [S] The term was coined by William John Harshberger in 1896 (SLIDE 3). [S] Ethnobotany covers food, clothing, currency, ritual, medicine, construction, cosmetics, shelter, hunting and religious ceremonies (SLIDE 4). [S] It is interdisciplinary: anthropology, pharmacology, pharmacognosy, agriculture, botany, chemistry, ecology, forestry, horticulture, linguistics, medicine, religious studies, sociology and systematics (SLIDE 6). [S] Applications: identify promising species, identify targets, guide fractionation, explore chemical diversity (SLIDE 19, 20). [S] Examples: quinine from Cinchona, paclitaxel from Pacific yew, artemisinin from Artemisia annua, salicin/aspirin from willow, digoxin from foxglove (SLIDE 22, 23). [S] Fieldwork: collect, press, dry and deposit vouchers; follow local preparation methods; record knowledge before it is lost (SLIDE 13, 15). [S] Ethical rules: cultural sensitivity, informed consent, benefit sharing, sustainability (SLIDE 24, 25).

Historical/research layer. [S] Plant use is recorded from Babylon around 1770 BC (Code of Hammurabi) and Egypt about 1550 BC (SLIDE 7). [S] Richard Evans Schultes is named as a best-known modern ethnobotanist; he studied the peyote cactus Lophophora williamsii, used on cuts, and found antibiotic activity even against penicillin-resistant Staphylococcus (SLIDE 10). [S] Paul Alan Cox, Michael Balick, Abbiw, Irvine and Oku Ampofo are named (SLIDE 11). [K] Unresolved: how to attribute benefit when knowledge is held collectively and orally [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: a plant collection, pressed and dried as a voucher (SLIDE 15) [S]. [K] Method: botanical identification, extract preparation, bioassay, fractionation, structure elucidation (SLIDE 18, 20, 25 of the role-of-science deck) [S + K]. [K] Reference range: none applicable to an ethnobotanical survey; for a resulting drug, potency specifications apply [I]. [S] Error sources: misidentified plant, loss of traditional knowledge through acculturation and habitat destruction, and failure to follow the original extraction method (SLIDE 13) [S]. [K] QC: deposit a herbarium voucher and authenticate by a taxonomist [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No analytical protocol is given.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] The clinical payoff is a new medicine from a traditional lead. [K] Artemisinin-based combination therapies are first-line for uncomplicated malaria; quinine is reserved for severe cases in some settings [K]. [K] Paclitaxel is used in ovarian, breast and lung cancer; aspirin is used for pain, fever and antiplatelet prophylaxis [K]. [S] The slides present these as successful discovery examples (SLIDE 22, 23).

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Artemisinin: endoperoxide activated by haem iron; dihydroartemisinin forms radicals that alkylate parasite proteins [K]. Quinine: inhibits haemozoin biocrystallisation in the parasite food vacuole [K]. Paclitaxel: binds β-tubulin and stabilises microtubules, arresting mitosis [K]. Aspirin: irreversibly acetylates cyclooxygenase, reducing prostaglandin and thromboxane synthesis [K]. Digoxin: inhibits Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase, increasing intracellular Na⁺ and reducing Ca²⁺ extrusion via NCX, increasing contractility [K]. [S] Only the plant–drug pairs, not mechanisms, are on the slides.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: discovering leads from oral rather than written traditions; ensuring benefit-sharing under Nagoya; and using AI to prioritise plants by chemical space [K]. [S] The slides note knowledge erosion and habitat destruction as threats (SLIDE 13, 26).

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Aspirin synthesis from salicin is a conserved-atom transformation: salicylic acid's phenolic OH is acetylated by acetic anhydride, transferring an acetyl group (CH₃CO) and releasing acetate; no atoms vanish [K]. Paclitaxel's taxane ring is assembled from isoprenoid units derived from acetyl-CoA through the mevalonate pathway; carbon skeletons are conserved [K]. [K] Energy stored in thioester bonds of acetyl-CoA and in the peroxide bond of artemisinin drives these transformations; no energy is created [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] SLIDE 20 describes bioassay-guided fractionation. Suppose 2 kg of bark yields 20 g of crude extract (1% yield), and the active fraction is 0.5% of the crude extract: 20 g × 0.005 = 0.1 g = 100 mg pure compound ✓. If the bioassay uses 10 mg per test, that gives 100/10 = 10 tests from the purified material ✓. [S] Quinine example from SLIDE 22: Cinchona bark traditionally used for fevers → isolated quinine → malaria drug; the reasoning chain is source-grounded, the yields above are illustrative arithmetic labeled [I], not slide data.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Traditional knowledge is a search heuristic that narrows drug discovery.
  • Mechanistic Story. Field observation → extract → bioassay → fraction → pure active compound.
  • Clinical Story. Quinine, artemisinin, taxol and aspirin are real outcomes.
  • MLS Story. Voucher specimens and authenticated material are the quality anchor.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Each named drug has a defined molecular target.
  • Research Story. Oral knowledge, benefit-sharing and AI prioritisation are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Engineered yeast produces artemisinin precursors [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Supply chains for plant-derived drugs need sustainable sourcing [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Molecular docking and cheminformatics prioritise plant metabolites [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Plants make these metabolites as herbivore defences [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Benefit-sharing agreements and community bioprospecting ventures are emerging [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Traditional use proves efficacy" — it generates a hypothesis; "one plant, one drug" — mixtures are common.
  • Memory Hook. Harshberger 1896; quinine, taxol, artemisinin, aspirin.
  • Connections. Culture → plant use → extraction → target → drug → benefit sharing.

Source: ETHNOBOTANY-151.pptx — SLIDE 3.

Plant taxonomy and scientific naming

Biological geography. [S] Taxonomy is the art and science of describing organisms; systematics studies diversity and relationships through time (taxonomy bhm 151 SLIDE 2, 3, 4). Zoom: flora → family → genus → species → plant organ (rhizome, leaf) → tissue → cell → metabolite → target [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle for a wrong identification: a look-alike plant may lack the active metabolite or contain a toxin — the reason a herbarium voucher matters [K]. [S] The slides state that taxonomy provides identification, classification, nomenclature and description (SLIDE 7).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is unambiguous communication across languages and countries. [I] A common name can map to several species or one species to several common names; binomial nomenclature is a addressing system that removes ambiguity. [K] This is an information-theoretic problem: a unique two-word key reduces lookup collisions [K]. [S] The slides give examples: pineapple is neither a pine nor an apple; Kentucky bluegrass is neither blue nor from Kentucky (SLIDE 16).

Just-in-time foundations. Systematics = study of organism diversity and relationships through time [S]; taxonomy = description, identification, naming and classification [S]; classification = grouping by shared characteristics [S]; nomenclature = naming [S]; phylogeny = evolutionary relationships, now via molecular sequencing [S]; taxon = a named group [S]; binomial nomenclature = genus + specific epithet [S]; specific epithet = the second word, often wrongly called "the species" [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Taxonomy sits within systematics (SLIDE 4). [S] Its principles moved from descriptive taxonomy (19th-century gross morphology) to experimental taxonomy (20th-century cytology, genetics, anatomy) to phylogeny as the modern principle (SLIDE 8, 9). [S] Binomial nomenclature was developed by Caspar Bauhin and August Rivinus and adopted by Linnaeus in Species Plantarum (SLIDE 24). [S] The first element is the genus, the second the specific epithet; examples Zingiber officinale, Mangifera indicum (SLIDE 25). [S] Author citations follow: Zea mays L., with "L." for Linnaeus; "Hook. f." means the son (SLIDE 26). [S] Name transfers put the original author in brackets: Orcuttia mucronata Crampton → Tuctoria mucronata (Crampton) Reeder (SLIDE 27). [S] Epithets encode information: officinale medicinal, esculentus edible, somniferum sleep-inducing, canadensis geographic, altisimus tall, serratus saw-toothed (SLIDE 39–44).

Historical/research layer. [S] Sixteenth–18th-century polynomials were long descriptive phrases that grew as new information arrived; the two-word system replaced them (SLIDE 23, 24). [K] Modern taxonomy uses DNA barcoding (rbcL, matK for plants); the slides mention molecular sequencing only for phylogeny [S + K]. [K] Unresolved: delimiting species in plants with hybridisation and polyploidy [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: a plant specimen, ideally flowering or fruiting, pressed and dried (SLIDE 15) [S]. [K] Method: morphological keying, comparison with a herbarium, and DNA barcoding where morphology is insufficient [K]. [K] Reference range: not a clinical range; the accepted name in a authoritative flora is the reference [I]. [S] Error sources: common-name ambiguity, morphological convergence, and name changes over time (SLIDE 14–22) [S]. [K] QC: voucher specimen plus expert determination; record the author citation [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No molecular protocol is given.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Correct identification is a patient-safety issue. [K] Adulteration of herbal products with related but toxic species causes poisoning; the classic problem of Aristolochia in Chinese herbal medicines causing nephropathy and urothelial cancer illustrates the consequence [K]. [K] Correct naming also underpins pharmacovigilance: an adverse event must be tied to a defined species [K].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Species identity determines which named compound is present: Atropa belladonna contains atropine (muscarinic antagonist), Digitalis purpurea digoxin (Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase inhibitor), Papaver somniferum morphine (μ-opioid agonist) [K]. [S] The scientific names of these plants, not the mechanisms, are the slide content; mechanism statements are model knowledge.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: integrating genomic and morphological species delimitation; building searchable digital herbaria for West Africa; and tracking name changes in pharmacopoeial monographs [K]. [S] The slides note names change from time to time (SLIDE 22).

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] No atoms are transformed by naming; the conserved object is the specimen's identity across name changes — the type specimen anchors it [K]. [K] No energy accounting applies; the discipline is information management [I].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] From SLIDE 21: Solanum tuberosum (potato), Solanum melongena (eggplant), Solanum nigrum (black nightshade). All share genus Solanum = 1 shared genus word out of 2 name words ✓. [S] Binomial uniqueness: with 3 example species in one genus, the epithets tuberosum, melongena and nigrum give 3 distinct binomials out of 3 possible ✓. [S] Author-transfer notation: Orcuttia mucronata Crampton → Tuctoria mucronata (Crampton) Reeder — genus word changes, epithet mucronata conserved, original author in brackets ✓ (SLIDE 27). [I] Two-word names reduce collisions relative to a single descriptive phrase, which is the design point.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Taxonomy gives every plant one unambiguous scientific address.
  • Mechanistic Story. Describe → group → name → classify → trace evolutionary relationships.
  • Clinical Story. Misidentified plants cause poisoning; correct names enable surveillance.
  • MLS Story. Voucher specimens and expert determination anchor the lab.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Species identity determines which named drug compound is present.
  • Research Story. Species delimitation and digital herbaria are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] DNA barcoding and metabarcoding authenticate botanical ingredients [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Herbal manufacturers must state the accepted botanical name on labels [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Global biodiversity databases resolve synonyms automatically [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Phylogeny explains why related plants make similar metabolites [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] AI image identification of medicinal plants is an emerging tool [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "The epithet is the species" — the species name is genus + epithet; "common names are enough" — they are ambiguous.
  • Memory Hook. Genus first, epithet second; officinale = medicinal.
  • Connections. Ambiguity → binomial → voucher → authentication → safety.

Source: taxonomy bhm 151.pptx — SLIDE 4.

The role of science in herbalism

Biological geography. [S] Science validates traditional knowledge and turns plant use into reproducible products (BHM 151 Role of Science SLIDE 33). Zoom: culture → plant → extraction vessel → assay plate → protein target → binding pocket [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle in the pipeline: a lead compound is isolated from bark, its structure solved, analogues synthesised, one candidate dosed in animals and humans, then manufactured and monitored after approval [S, SLIDE 19, 25]. [S] The slides trace therapies from prehistoric use to modern drug discovery (SLIDE 4).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is attrition: most candidates fail for lack of efficacy or toxicity, so the pipeline must kill bad candidates early and cheaply. [S] The slides state that development takes 12–15 years and over $1 billion, and drugs fail mainly on efficacy or toxicity (SLIDE 18). [I] Target identification and validation is therefore the highest-leverage step. [K] Physically, screening is a serial dilution and detection problem — signal-to-noise determines the hit threshold [K].

Just-in-time foundations. Target = a molecule whose modulation is expected to treat disease [S]; hit = a compound showing activity in screening [S]; lead = a hit with acceptable potency and properties [S]; lead optimisation = chemical refinement [S]; preclinical = animal testing before humans [S]; Phase 1/2/3 = healthy-volunteer, patient, and large patient trials [S]; ADME = absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion (SLIDE 21); IND/NDA/BLA = regulatory application types [S]; efficacy = ability to work; toxicity = capacity to harm [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Therapies were discovered by trial and error, accident (penicillin, valium, sildenafil, lavender) or belief systems and divination — the latter not accepted by scientists (SLIDE 17). [S] The pipeline: target identification and validation → high-throughput screening → hit generation → lead compounds → lead optimisation → preclinical animal studies → Phase 1 (20–80 healthy individuals) → Phase 2 (100–300 patients) → Phase 3 (1,000–3,000 patients) → FDA review and approval → post-approval (SLIDE 18, 19). [S] Plant selection is random (Taxol) or targeted by phylogeny or cultural/ethnobotanical knowledge (SLIDE 24). [S] Isolation of bioactive constituents, structure elucidation and optimisation follow, then semi-synthetic or synthetic drugs (SLIDE 25). [S] Drugs from plants include morphine, strychnine, quinine, caffeine, nicotine, atropine, cocaine, salicin/aspirin, taxol/paclitaxel, artemisinin, colchicine, digoxin and curcumin (SLIDE 26–28). [S] Science validates knowledge, identifies active compounds, explains mechanisms, ensures safety and standardises products (SLIDE 33).

Historical/research layer. [S] Shen Nung's Pen Tsao listed 365 medicinal plants; the Ebers papyrus (1550 BC) had 876 prescriptions; Ma Huang (Ephedra vulgaris) was used from 2800 BC, and ephedrine was synthesised only in 1904 (SLIDE 7, 8, 10). [S] The Canon of Medicine by Ibn-Sina (980–1037) codified diagnosis and treatments and remained authoritative in Europe until the 15th century (SLIDE 9). [S] Hippocrates used willow bark for pain in the 4th century BC; Dioscorides described over 600 plants (SLIDE 11). [K] Unresolved: better predictive models for clinical success from preclinical data [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: the slides do not name a diagnostic specimen for discovery [SLIDES SILENT]. [K] In development, specimens are screening plates, animal tissues, and patient blood or tumour samples in trials [K]. [K] Method: analytical chemistry for identity and purity, assays for potency, and clinical laboratory safety panels [K]. [K] Reference range: none on slides; trials define endpoints statistically [I]. [S] Error sources: a false-positive screening hit; species misidentification; and failure to reproduce a traditional preparation (SLIDE 13 of ethnobotany deck) [S + I]. [K] QC: validated assays, reference standards and GLP preclinical studies [K].

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Clinical relevance is the translation of a traditional claim into a tested therapy. [K] Artemisinin-based combinations replaced quinine monotherapy for uncomplicated malaria because combination delays resistance [K]. [K] Taxol transformed ovarian cancer treatment; digoxin remains important in heart failure and atrial fibrillation [K]. [K] Adverse events in trials drive dose limits and labelling [K].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Morphine: μ-opioid receptor agonist; atropine: muscarinic antagonist; quinine: inhibits haemozoin formation; salicin/aspirin: cyclooxygenase inhibition after conversion to salicylic acid and acetylation; paclitaxel: β-tubulin stabilisation; artemisinin: haem-activated endoperoxide; colchicine: binds tubulin and blocks polymerisation; metformin (from Galega officinalis, SLIDE 27): reduces hepatic gluconeogenesis, partly via AMPK and mitochondrial complex I [S + K]. [S] The plant–drug pairs are on slides; mechanisms are model knowledge.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: accelerating target validation with human organoids, predicting toxicity with in-silico models, and designing combination therapies from ethnobotanical mixtures [K]. [S] Accelerated approval, as with COVID vaccines, is noted (SLIDE 23).

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Aspirin from salicin: the glycoside is hydrolysed to salicylic acid (the sugar is released and metabolised), then acetylated using acetic anhydride; atoms conserved in each step [K]. [K] Artemisinin's strained endoperoxide holds bond energy; electron transfer from Fe²⁺ haem cleaves it — donor haem, acceptor peroxide [K]. [K] No energy is produced; the stored chemical energy is converted to radical damage [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Trial sizes from SLIDE 19: Phase 1 20–80, Phase 2 100–300, Phase 3 1,000–3,000. A minimal sequential programme enrols 20 + 100 + 1,000 = 1,120 participants ✓; a maximal one 80 + 300 + 3,000 = 3,380 ✓. [S] Development cost >$1 billion over 12–15 years (SLIDE 18): $1,000,000,000 / 15 years ≈ $66.7 million per year on average ✓ — an arithmetic illustration, not slide data.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. A traditional claim becomes a drug only through target validation, screening and trials.
  • Mechanistic Story. Select plant → isolate compound → solve structure → optimise → test → approve.
  • Clinical Story. Efficacy and toxicity decide success; combinations can delay resistance.
  • MLS Story. Analytical assays and trial safety panels support decisions.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Named plant drugs and their targets are the output.
  • Research Story. Predictive toxicology and organoids are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Engineered biosynthesis produces scarce plant drugs [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] GMP scale-up and supply security follow approval [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Virtual screening narrows hit lists before wet-lab work [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Plant defence chemistry is the raw material [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Natural-product startups and licensing deals are common [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Natural means safe" — toxicity testing is still required; "one trial proves a drug" — phases answer different questions.
  • Memory Hook. Target → hit → lead → preclinical → 1/2/3 → approval.
  • Connections. Ethnobotany → target → screening → trials → approved drug.

Source: BHM 151-ROLE OF SCIENCE IN HERBALISM.pptx — SLIDE 18.

Herbal monographs

Biological geography. [S] A monograph is a detailed written study of a specific drug, herb or compound (BHM 151 Introduction to Herbal Monographs SLIDE 2). Zoom: regulatory system → pharmacopoeia → monograph → plant → plant part → tissue → metabolite → assay target [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle of the article itself: drafted by an expert committee, published, revised as evidence changes, and used by manufacturers and regulators to define the product [K]. [S] A herbal monograph defines a botanical drug and gives information allowing proper identification (SLIDE 4).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is product consistency: a batch of dried leaf must match the batch that generated the safety and efficacy data. [I] A monograph is a specification document that converts a biological variable into measurable acceptance criteria. [K] Contaminant limits are set from toxicology; assay ranges are set from observed manufacturing variation [K].

Just-in-time foundations. Monograph = a detailed standard document [S]; pharmacopoeia = an official collection of standards [S]; identification test = a test confirming the right material [S]; purity standard = limits on contaminants [S]; assay = quantification of active compounds [S]; microbial load = number of microbes allowed [S]; heavy metals = toxic elemental contaminants [S]; proprietary = company-owned product information [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Types: pharmacopoeial, herbal, scientific and manufacturer monographs (SLIDE 3). [S] A herbal monograph lists chemical constituents, actions, clinical uses and recommended dosage (SLIDE 4). [S] Its sections: title and definition, description, identification tests (microscopy, chromatography, spectroscopy), purity standards, assay methods, uses and pharmacological actions, dosage and administration, storage conditions (SLIDE 5). [S] Importance: standardisation, regulatory compliance, research and development, education (SLIDE 6). [S] Major pharmacopoeias: USP, BP, Ph. Eur., Chinese Pharmacopoeia and WHO monographs (SLIDE 7). [I] The sequence is logical: identify first, then exclude contaminants, then measure potency.

Historical/research layer. [K] Pharmacopoeias arose from the need to standardise apothecary preparations; the first London Pharmacopoeia appeared in 1617 [K] — the slides list modern examples only. [S] WHO monographs on medicinal plants provide international standards (SLIDE 7). [K] Unresolved: how to standardise a multi-component botanical when no single marker predicts clinical effect [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: the crude drug or finished dosage form (SLIDE 4, 5) [I]. [K] Method: microscopy for identification, chromatography for fingerprinting, spectroscopy for confirmation, assays for potency and contaminant tests (SLIDE 5) [S]. [K] Reference range: the monograph's acceptance criteria are the reference; e.g., a stated assay range for a marker compound [S] — no numerical limits on slides. [S] Error sources: wrong plant part, adulteration, moisture and microbial contamination [S, SLIDE 5]. [K] QC: use certified reference standards and participate in proficiency testing [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No numerical limits are given.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Standardisation protects patients from under- and over-dosing. [K] Variable marker levels in St John's wort products cause unpredictable CYP3A4 induction; monograph limits reduce that variability [K]. [K] Heavy-metal contamination in some traditional products causes poisoning; purity sections exist to prevent this [K]. [K] Adulteration with a related but toxic species is a documented problem that the identification and purity sections are designed to catch [K].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Monographs for Digitalis standardise cardiac glycosides that inhibit Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase; for Atropa belladonna, atropine content is limited because it is a muscarinic antagonist with a narrow therapeutic index; for Cinchona, quinine content is assayed [K]. [S] These are illustrative named substances; the slides give the monograph structure but no specific monograph [SLIDES SILENT].

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: marker-to-effect correlation, harmonisation across pharmacopoeias, and using metabolomics fingerprints instead of single markers [K]. [S] The slides do not discuss research gaps.

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] An assay does not transform analytes; it measures them — a conserved sample is split, extracted and quantified, and mass balance is checked with spikes [K]. [K] No redox claim is needed; spectroscopic and chromatographic signals arise from electron transitions and differential partitioning, not energy production [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Use the SLIDE 5 template. Suppose a monograph states 95–105% of label claim for a marker and a tablet is labelled 10 mg: acceptable range = 10 × 0.95 = 9.5 mg to 10 × 1.05 = 10.5 mg ✓. A batch assay of 9.4 mg fails by 0.1 mg; 10.2 mg passes ✓. [I] These numbers are illustrative arithmetic, not slide values; the slide supplies only the structure of the monograph.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. A monograph turns a plant into a measurable, reproducible product.
  • Mechanistic Story. Identify → test purity → assay potency → set dose and storage.
  • Clinical Story. Consistency prevents toxicity and treatment failure.
  • MLS Story. Microscopy, chromatography, spectroscopy, microbial and metal testing.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Named monographs control digoxin, atropine and quinine content.
  • Research Story. Marker-to-effect correlation remains open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] DNA barcoding can be included in identification sections [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Monographs are the legal basis for batch release [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Chromatographic fingerprints are analysed with chemometrics [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Standardisation is a cultural technology, not an evolved trait [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Digital monograph databases are commercial regulatory tools [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "A monograph lists uses only" — it also sets purity and assay limits; "one marker is enough" — often debated.
  • Memory Hook. Title, description, ID, purity, assay, uses, dose, storage.
  • Connections. Plant → identification → purity → assay → release → safe use.

Source: BHM 151 Introduction to Herbal Monographs.pptx — SLIDE 5.

Sociological perspectives on traditional medicine

Biological geography. [S] Traditional medicine combines plant, animal and mineral medicines with spiritual therapies, manual techniques and exercise, reflecting cultural heritage (BHM 151 Sociological Considerations SLIDE 2). Here the "system" is society, so zoom: society → community → family → healer–patient dyad → individual → body → belief → behaviour [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle is not the right frame for belief; the analogous lifecycle is knowledge: learned through apprenticeship, stored orally, applied in ritual, and lost through acculturation [S, SLIDE 18].

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is social order under healthcare scarcity. [I] Where formal services are absent, a culturally legitimate healer provides continuity and reduces anxiety — a functional solution. [K] Ritual can be understood as a predictable, repeated intervention that lowers uncertainty for patient and community, which itself has measurable physiological effects through stress pathways [K].

Just-in-time foundations. Interactionist perspective = focuses on face-to-face meanings and trust [S]; functionalist perspective = how practices maintain social stability [S]; conflict perspective = power, inequality and competition [S]; social determinants of health = income, education, living conditions [S]; biopiracy = unauthorised appropriation of knowledge [S]; indigenous knowledge systems = locally held, transmitted knowledge [S]; stigma = social devaluation [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Three sociological theories are applied: interactionist, functionalist and conflict (SLIDE 4). [S] Interactionism emphasises shared meaning and trust between healer and patient (SLIDE 5). [S] Functionalism stresses social stability, cultural continuity and healthcare access in underserved areas (SLIDE 6, 7). [S] Conflict theory examines marginalisation by modern medicine, unequal access and tensions over legitimacy (SLIDE 8, 9). [S] Social determinants include income, education, living conditions and rural geography (SLIDE 10). [S] Women are often primary caregivers and healers (SLIDE 11). [S] Healing involves community participation, rituals, sacred objects and prayer (SLIDE 12, 13). [S] Healers are custodians of cultural knowledge with high social status (SLIDE 14). [S] Illness may be perceived as spiritual imbalance, curses or social transgression (SLIDE 15). [S] Knowledge passes through apprenticeship and oral tradition and is threatened by biodiversity loss, biopiracy and globalisation (SLIDE 17, 18).

Historical/research layer. [K] Functionalism is associated with Durkheim, conflict theory with Marx, and symbolic interactionism with Mead and Blumer [K] — the slides name the perspectives but not their founders. [K] These three lenses were developed in the 19th and early 20th centuries to explain how societies hold together and change [K]. [S] Urban stigma and marginalisation are noted (SLIDE 16). [K] Unresolved: how integration of traditional and formal systems affects outcomes and whether it erodes or preserves knowledge [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] No specimen or laboratory method is given — [SLIDES SILENT]. [K] The laboratory relevance is indirect: a patient may present with poisoning, organ failure or interactions after using a traditional remedy, and the lab investigates the biomedical consequences [K]. [K] QC and reference ranges are out of scope for this topic [I].

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Belief shapes help-seeking and adherence. [K] A patient who attributes illness to spiritual imbalance may delay biomedical treatment; conversely, a healer's ritual can reduce anxiety and improve engagement [K]. [K] The clinical task is to ask about traditional medicine use without judgement — a communication skill, not a laboratory test [K].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] No drug is named in this deck — [SLIDES SILENT]. [K] Relevant model knowledge: interactions between traditional remedies and prescribed drugs are the pharmaceutical concern, e.g. St John's wort with warfarin or antiretrovirals, and Ginkgo with antiplatelet agents [K].

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: measuring trust as a therapeutic variable; documenting oral knowledge before it disappears; and designing benefit-sharing that satisfies conflict-theory concerns [K]. [S] The slides call for documentation, research and curriculum integration (SLIDE 18).

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] No atoms or electrons are tracked; the conserved quantity is knowledge content as it is transmitted — and it is easily lost, which is the point [I]. [K] No energy claim is appropriate in a sociological layer [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] The three perspectives are applied to one case — a rural patient using a herbal remedy: interactionism asks about the healer–patient conversation (SLIDE 5); functionalism notes continued community function and access (SLIDE 7); conflict theory notes distance from clinics and marginalisation of healers (SLIDE 9) ✓. [I] Counting: 3 perspectives × 1 case = 3 distinct explanations of the same event, demonstrating why one lens is insufficient ✓. [S] No numerical data appear on these slides; the arithmetic is a counting illustration only.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Traditional medicine is a social institution as well as a set of remedies.
  • Mechanistic Story. Belief → healer interaction → ritual → trust → health behaviour.
  • Clinical Story. Non-judgemental enquiry reveals hidden remedy use.
  • MLS Story. No direct lab method; indirect investigation of toxicity and interactions.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Herb–drug interactions are the drug-related concern.
  • Research Story. Trust measurement and knowledge preservation are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Digital archives preserve oral knowledge ethically [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Community-owned production models address benefit-sharing [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Social-network analysis maps knowledge transmission [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Cultural transmission is a non-genetic inheritance system [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Community-led herbal enterprises are social innovations [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Conflict perspective says traditional medicine is bad" — it analyses power, not quality; "ritual is pointless" — it has social and psychological effects.
  • Memory Hook. Interaction = meaning; functional = stability; conflict = power.
  • Connections. Culture → healer → trust → adherence → clinical outcome.

Source: BHM 151 Sociological Considerations in Traditional Medicine Practice.pptx — SLIDE 4.

Bacterial structure and classification

Biological geography. [S] Bacteriology covers structure, function, culture, growth, reproduction and identification (Bacteriology edited yaw SLIDE 3). Zoom: organism → (no organ/tissue level) → cell → cell wall/chromosome → protein (penicillin-binding protein, porin) → active site [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle: peptidoglycan precursors are made in the cytoplasm, carried by bactoprenol across the membrane, assembled outside, and remodelled by autolysins [K]. [S] The slides give a structure–function table for flagella, pili, capsules, wall, membrane, ribosomes, inclusions, chromosome and plasmid (SLIDE 22).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The physical problem is survival in dilute environments: a bacterium must resist osmotic swelling, attach to surfaces and move toward nutrients. [S] The cell wall gives definite shape and strength to withstand osmotic pressure (SLIDE 23). [K] A cross-linked glycan mesh is a pressure vessel; a flagellum is a rotary motor powered by proton flow [K]. [S] Bacteria are typically 0.5–5 µm (SLIDE 6), a size that keeps diffusion distances short.

Just-in-time foundations. Prokaryote = no nucleus [S]; coccus spherical, bacillus rod, vibrio comma, spirillum rigid spiral, spirochaete flexible spiral, actinomycete filamentous (SLIDE 8); mycoplasma = bacterium lacking a rigid wall (SLIDE 18); peptidoglycan/murein = glycan chains cross-linked by peptides (SLIDE 23); teichoic acid = wall polymer of ribitol/glycerol phosphate [S]; LPS = lipopolysaccharide of the Gram-negative outer membrane [S]; plasmid = extrachromosomal DNA [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Classification uses phenotype (morphology, staining, culture, biochemical reactions, antigenic structure) and genotype (DNA–DNA hybridisation, G+C content) (SLIDE 7). [S] Mycoplasmas are small, lack a rigid wall, are pleomorphic, pass through bacteria-retaining filters and are the smallest autonomously growing organisms (SLIDE 18). [S] Rickettsiae and chlamydiae grow only in living cells (SLIDE 20). [S] Peptidoglycan is built from N-acetyl glucosamine and N-acetyl muramic acid with extensive peptide cross-linking (SLIDE 23). [S] Gram-positive walls are thicker, with teichoic acids; peptidoglycan is 50–90% of the wall, versus 5–10% in Gram-negative bacteria, which also have an outer membrane with LPS (SLIDE 25, 26). [S] Removing the wall leaves a protoplast that lyses in hypotonic or isotonic solutions and survives only in hypertonic ones (SLIDE 25). [S] Functions: flagella swimming, sex pilus DNA transfer, fimbriae attachment, capsule protection, wall prevents osmotic lysis, membrane transports solutes and generates energy, ribosomes translate, chromosome and plasmid carry DNA (SLIDE 22).

Historical/research layer. [S] Microbiology was introduced by Pasteur, who showed fermentation was microbial (1857–60); van Leeuwenhoek saw "animalcules" in 1675; Spallanzani and Pasteur settled spontaneous generation with sterility conditions of 115–120°C; Cohn discovered heat-resistant spores in 1876; Koch developed pure cultures on nutrient agar in 1877 (SLIDE 4, 5). [K] Christian Gram's stain (1884) is the practical classification tool [K]. [K] Unresolved: how wall synthesis is coordinated at the septum and how wall-less forms persist [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: any clinical sample — blood, urine, sputum, wound swab, stool [I]. [S] Method: microscopy, culture on media, Gram staining and biochemical tests (SLIDE 3). [K] Reference range: sterile sites such as blood and CSF should yield no growth [K]. [S] Error sources: contaminated media (the growth-requirements deck insists media be sterile, SLIDE 7); over-decolourisation giving wrong Gram type [K]. [K] QC: control organisms with each stain and media batch [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No numerical QC specifications.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Structure determines virulence and drug susceptibility. [K] LPS (endotoxin) from Gram-negative bacteria triggers TLR4-mediated inflammation and septic shock [K]. [K] Capsules, e.g. Streptococcus pneumoniae, resist phagocytosis [K]. [S] Mycoplasmas lack a wall, so β-lactams cannot work — a direct structure-to-therapy link [I].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Penicillin and ampicillin inhibit transpeptidase cross-linking of peptidoglycan; vancomycin binds D-Ala-D-Ala; cycloserine inhibits alanine racemase; bacitracin blocks bactoprenol dephosphorylation; polymyxins bind LPS and disrupt the Gram-negative outer membrane; rifampicin inhibits bacterial RNA polymerase; tetracyclines bind the 30S ribosomal subunit [K]. [S] Only the structural facts are on slides; drugs are model knowledge.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: new targets in wall assembly for resistant organisms; the role of kinks and heterogeneity in peptidoglycan; and rapid structural diagnostics [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] The deck raises no frontier questions.

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Peptidoglycan polymerisation uses activated nucleotide sugars; hydrolysis of the pyrophosphate linkage on bactoprenol supplies the free energy — stored bond energy coupled to polymer formation [K]. [K] Transpeptidation conserves atoms: the terminal D-alanine is released as the cross-link forms [K]. Flagellar rotation couples the proton-motive force to torque; no energy is created [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Wall composition: Gram-positive peptidoglycan 50–90% of wall, Gram-negative 5–10% (SLIDE 25). For a 100-unit wall, Gram-positive has 50–90 units peptidoglycan, Gram-negative 5–10 — a 5-to-18-fold difference (90/5 = 18; 50/10 = 5) ✓. [S] Size range 0.5–5 µm (SLIDE 6): a 1 µm coccus has surface area 4π(0.5)² ≈ 3.14 µm² and volume (4/3)π(0.5)³ ≈ 0.524 µm³, ratio 6.0 µm⁻¹ ✓; a 5 µm cell ratio 3/2.5 = 1.2 µm⁻¹, five times lower ✓.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Shape and wall architecture define identity, staining and drug susceptibility.
  • Mechanistic Story. Cytoplasmic precursors → membrane carrier → external polymer → cross-linked mesh.
  • Clinical Story. Endotoxin, capsules and wall-less bacteria shape disease and therapy.
  • MLS Story. Microscopy, culture, Gram stain, sterile media and controls.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. β-lactams, vancomycin, polymyxins and others hit named structures.
  • Research Story. Septal synthesis and resistance are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Wall-targeting engineered phages and antimicrobial peptides are in development [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Media and stain reagents are lot-tested [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Genome sequencing predicts cell-envelope targets [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] The wall solved osmosis but created an antibiotic target [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Rapid susceptibility tests are diagnostic products [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "All bacteria have walls" — mycoplasmas do not; "Gram stain reflects colour only" — it reflects wall architecture.
  • Memory Hook. Cocci, bacilli, vibrio, spirilla; Gram+ thick, Gram− thin + LPS.
  • Connections. Wall → Gram stain → drug choice → sepsis pathophysiology.

Source: Bacteriology edited yaw.pptx — SLIDE 22.

Bacterial growth requirements

Biological geography. [S] Bacteria need energy, electrons and carbon for cellular activity, growth and reproduction (Bacteria Growth requirements SLIDE 4). Zoom: environment → culture vessel → medium → cell → membrane → enzyme active site [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle of a nutrient: glucose enters the cell, is phosphorylated by hexokinase, is split by glycolysis, and its carbons end up in pyruvate, CO₂ or biomass; nitrogen from ammonium enters glutamate and then amino acids [K]. [S] The slides list chemical requirements (carbon, nitrogen, phosphorus, sulphur, oxygen, metal ions, vitamins) and physical ones (temperature, pH, gases, osmotic pressure, light) (SLIDE 8, 13).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is to supply a controlled environment that lets one cell become a visible population. [S] Culture media must contain all essential nutrients, proper salt and ion concentrations, correct pH and moisture, and must be sterile (SLIDE 6, 7). [K] Physically, growth is exponential: one cell → 2ⁿ after n generations, so contamination by a single unwanted cell also amplifies exponentially — the reason sterility is non-negotiable [K]. [S] The slides note acidity inhibits growth and is used in pickling (SLIDE 16).

Just-in-time foundations. Chemotroph = uses chemicals for energy; phototroph = uses light [S]; lithotroph = inorganic electron source; organotroph = organic electron source [S]; autotroph = CO₂ as carbon source; heterotroph = organic carbon [S]; growth factor = compound needed for growth that the organism cannot make [S]; essential = growth ceases without it; accessory = enhances growth but is not required [S]; capnophile = requires elevated CO₂ [S]; halophile = salt-tolerant [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Nutritional classification: energy (chemo/photo), electrons (litho/organo), carbon (auto/hetero) (SLIDE 4, 5). [S] Carbon is about 50% of cell dry weight and forms the backbone of organic compounds; nitrogen is 14% and forms amino acids, DNA and RNA; phosphorus forms DNA, RNA, ATP and phospholipids; sulphur forms proteins and vitamins thiamin and biotin (SLIDE 8, 9). [S] Potassium, magnesium and calcium act as enzyme cofactors, with calcium needed for cell-wall synthesis; trace elements include iron, copper, molybdenum and zinc (SLIDE 9). [S] Growth factors are amino acids, vitamins and purines/pyrimidines (SLIDE 10). [S] Temperature classes: psychrophiles below 20°C, mesophiles 20–45°C, thermophiles 45–80°C; pathogens grow best at 37°C (SLIDE 14, 15). [S] pH classes: neutralophiles 7.4–7.6 including E. coli, acidophiles below 5.5 such as Acetobacter aceti, alkaliphiles 8.5–11 such as Bacillus subtilis (SLIDE 16). [S] Oxygen classes: obligate aerobes (Pseudomonas), obligate anaerobes (Clostridium), facultative organisms (most pathogens), aerotolerant (Lactobacillus) and microaerophiles (Campylobacter) (SLIDE 17, 18). [S] Halophiles need 1–6% NaCl (mild, S. aureus), 6–15% (moderate, Vibrio fisheri) or above 15–30% (extreme) (SLIDE 20).

Historical/research layer. [S] The slide deck is a modern teaching summary dated 2/4/2024 (SLIDE 2) [S]. [K] The classification of bacteria by oxygen use dates to early microbiologists; the catalase explanation for oxygen toxicity is classical [K]. [K] Unresolved: how bacteria coordinate nutrient sensing with the stringent response, and how persisters survive without growth [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: clinical material inoculated onto media (SLIDE 6) [I]. [S] Method: culture on media that meet the listed requirements, with isolation and identification (SLIDE 6) [S]. [K] Reference range: sterile sites should show no growth; a pure culture of a recognised pathogen is abnormal [K]. [S] Error sources: non-sterile media, wrong atmosphere (e.g. culturing an obligate anaerobe in air), incorrect temperature (SLIDE 14, 17) [S]. [K] QC: each batch tested with control organisms and known-growth conditions [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No QC numerical criteria are given.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Growth requirements determine where an organism causes disease. [K] Clostridium tetani grows in anaerobic wound tissue; Campylobacter needs low oxygen and causes gut infection; Vibrio species need salt and cause wound infections after seawater exposure [K]. [K] Fever can inhibit some pathogens while the host raises iron sequestration — nutritional immunity [K]. [S] The slides note that pathogenic bacteria grow best at 37°C, body temperature (SLIDE 14).

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] β-lactam antibiotics work only on growing bacteria because they block peptidoglycan cross-linking during wall synthesis — a direct link from growth requirements to pharmacodynamics [K]. Metronidazole is reduced by anaerobic organisms to a radical that damages DNA, so it is active against anaerobes [K]. Ciprofloxacin inhibits DNA gyrase during replication [K]. [S] No drugs are named on these slides.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: culturing the unculturable majority of environmental bacteria; defining the growth state of persisters; and modelling biofilm nutrient gradients [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No frontier content in the deck.

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Carbon accounting: CO₂ fixed by autotrophs enters the Calvin cycle; heterotrophs take reduced carbon from organic molecules; carbon ends in biomass or is released as CO₂ [K]. [K] Electron accounting: lithotrophs donate electrons from inorganic substances (e.g. H₂, Fe²⁺), organotrophs from organic ones; terminal acceptors include O₂ for aerobes and sulfate or nitrate for anaerobes [K]. [K] Energy is conserved in ATP and ion gradients; the oxidation of nutrients releases free energy captured as ATP by substrate-level or oxidative phosphorylation — no energy is created [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Generation arithmetic: one bacterium dividing every 30 minutes gives 2 generations per hour; after 5 hours, 2¹⁰ = 1,024 cells ✓; after 10 hours, 2²⁰ = 1,048,576 cells ✓. [S] Salt classes (SLIDE 20): mild halophiles 1–6% NaCl, moderate 6–15%, extreme above 15% — the categories are contiguous at 6% and 15% ✓. [S] Temperature: mesophiles 20–45°C, pathogens optimum 37°C, which lies inside 20–45 ✓ (SLIDE 14, 15). [I] These calculations use only slide ranges and standard binary arithmetic.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. A medium is an engineered environment matching an organism's chemistry and physics.
  • Mechanistic Story. Nutrient entry → metabolism → biomass → exponential population growth.
  • Clinical Story. Oxygen and temperature preferences shape infection sites.
  • MLS Story. Sterile media, correct atmosphere and control organisms.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. β-lactams and metronidazole depend on growth state and oxygen.
  • Research Story. Unculturable organisms and persisters are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Fermentation Design-of-Experiments optimises media industrially [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Media powders are batch-tested for sterility and growth promotion [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Growth-curve models estimate generation time from OD measurements [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Metabolic versatility evolved to exploit ecological niches [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Rapid microbial identification platforms are commercial diagnostics [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "All bacteria need oxygen" — many are anaerobic; "more nutrient always means more growth" — osmotic and pH limits apply.
  • Memory Hook. Chemo/photo, litho/organo, auto/hetero; 20/45/80°C temperature bands.
  • Connections. Medium → growth → culture → identification → therapy.

Source: Bacteria Growth requirements.pptx — SLIDE 4.

Virology: virus structure and replication

Biological geography. [S] A virus is sub-microscopic, 20–400 nm, an obligate intracellular parasite with DNA or RNA but never both (Virology 2 SLIDE 5). Zoom: host organism → organ (lung, liver) → tissue → cell → organelle (nucleus or cytoplasm) → viral protein (capsid, envelope glycoprotein) → receptor-binding site [S]. [K] Molecule lifecycle: the virion attaches, enters, uncoats, replicates its genome, translates proteins, assembles and exits; enveloped viruses acquire their lipid envelope by budding [S, SLIDE 21]. [S] A complete assembled particle is a virion (SLIDE 7).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The physical problem is delivery: a tiny particle must find a specific cell, cross a membrane, and reuse the host's machinery. [S] Attachment proteins interact with specific receptors, and that specificity determines host range or tropism (SLIDE 21). [K] Binding is governed by affinity and avidity — multiple weak interactions summing to a strong attachment [K]. [S] Viruses are inert outside the cell (SLIDE 7 note).

Just-in-time foundations. Virion = complete infectious particle [S]; capsid = protein coat [S]; envelope = outer lipoprotein membrane [S]; peplomer = glycoprotein spike [S]; icosahedral symmetry = 20-faced capsid arrangement [S]; tropism = which cells a virus infects [S]; cytolytic = kills the cell on release; cytopathic = damages without necessarily killing (SLIDE 21); lysis = rupture; budding = exit wrapped in membrane [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Composition: nucleic acid (DNA or RNA), capsid, optional envelope, non-structural proteins (SLIDE 7). [S] Capsids may show icosahedral symmetry; complex phages have an icosahedral head, collar, helical tail, base plate and fibres (SLIDE 10, 13). [S] Viral proteins are grouped as structural, non-structural, regulatory and accessory; viruses use host machinery because they code for few proteins (SLIDE 14, 15). [S] Structural proteins protect nucleic acid, attach to receptors and penetrate membranes; non-structural proteins replicate nucleic acid and modify the host (SLIDE 16, 17). [S] Comparison with cells: viruses have DNA or RNA (cells have both), few proteins, no ribosomes, no mitochondria, no binary fission (SLIDE 18). [S] Replication cycle: adsorption, penetration, replication, assembly, maturation, release (SLIDE 21). [S] Most DNA viruses assemble in the nucleus; most RNA viruses in the cytoplasm (SLIDE 20 note). [S] Lysis kills the cell (variola/smallpox); budding usually does not and gives the envelope (influenza A) (SLIDE 21).

Historical/research layer. [K] The term virus comes from Latin for poison; Ivanovsky and Beijerinck established filterable agents in the 1890s; the word virology is used formally in the 20th century [K] — the slides give no history. [S] The deck references the ICTV taxonomy website (SLIDE 3). [K] Unresolved: the origin of giant viruses, and how host-range shifts produce pandemics [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: respiratory secretions, blood, stool, CSF depending on the virus [I]. [K] Method: cell culture, antigen tests, PCR and serology (SLIDE 30 names cultivation) [S + K]. [K] Reference range: no virus detectable in sterile-site specimens [K]. [S] Error sources: viruses need living cells, so non-viable transport media or delayed inoculation loses infectivity (SLIDE 30) [I]. [K] QC: positive and negative cell-culture controls; PCR inhibition controls [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No turnaround times or sensitivity figures.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Disease depends on tropism and release mode. [K] Influenza buds from respiratory epithelium and causes seasonal disease; variola causes smallpox by cytolytic release; HIV integrates into host DNA and destroys CD4 T cells [K]. [K] Enveloped viruses are more vulnerable to drying and detergents; this explains why enveloped respiratory viruses spread by droplets rather than fomites as readily as non-enveloped noroviruses [K].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Acyclovir is phosphorylated by HSV thymidine kinase and terminates viral DNA synthesis; oseltamivir inhibits influenza neuraminidase and blocks release; remdesivir terminates RNA synthesis by the viral RNA-dependent RNA polymerase; tenofovir and lamivudine inhibit HIV reverse transcriptase; ritonavir inhibits HIV protease; artesunate? no — not antiviral here [K]. [S] None is named in the slides; mechanisms are model knowledge.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: broadly conserved antiviral targets, predicting zoonotic spillover, and designing broad-spectrum vaccines [K]. [S] The slides mention viruses of importance to Africa as reading (SLIDE 2).

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Genome accounting: a DNA virus has DNA only, an RNA virus RNA only, never both [S]; nucleotide polymerisation conserves phosphodiester bonds and releases pyrophosphate, whose hydrolysis drives the reaction [K]. [K] Budding consumes host membrane and energy for vesicle scission; the envelope's lipids come from the host, not the viral genome [S + K]. No energy is produced — the virus redirects host ATP and nucleotide pools [K].

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Size range 20–400 nm (SLIDE 5): a 100 nm spherical virion has radius 50 nm; surface area 4π(50)² ≈ 31,416 nm², volume (4/3)π(50)³ ≈ 523,599 nm³ ✓ (using nm units). [S] Replication output: one infected cell releasing 1,000 virions produces 10³ particles; after three serial rounds under idealised assumptions, 1,000³ = 10⁹ virions ✓ — illustrative arithmetic, not slide data [I]. [S] Structurally: 20 faces in icosahedral symmetry, so a capsid with 12 vertices and 20 triangular faces satisfies Euler's formula V − E + F = 12 − 30 + 20 = 2 ✓.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. A virus is a minimal delivery system that commandeers a host cell.
  • Mechanistic Story. Attach → enter → uncoat → replicate → assemble → release.
  • Clinical Story. Tropism determines disease; release mode affects spread and virulence.
  • MLS Story. Living-cell culture, PCR, antigen and serology with controls.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Acyclovir, oseltamivir, remdesivir and antiretrovirals hit named steps.
  • Research Story. Giant viruses and spillover are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Viral vectors deliver gene therapies and vaccines [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Vaccine production needs qualified cell banks and inactivation validation [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Genome databases track variants and predict antigenicity [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Minimal genomes trade independence for rapid replication [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] mRNA vaccine platforms are a translational outcome [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Viruses are cells" — no ribosomes or mitochondria; "antibiotics treat viruses" — antibiotics target bacterial structures.
  • Memory Hook. DNA or RNA, never both; lysis kills, budding spares.
  • Connections. Structure → tropism → replication → disease → antiviral target.

Source: Virology 2.pptx — SLIDE 5.

Helminth parasitology: flukes, tapeworms and roundworms

Biological geography. [S] Helminths are parasitic worms: trematodes (flukes), cestodes (tapeworms) and nematodes (roundworms) (Helminths Parasitology SLIDE 3, 11, 18). Zoom: human → organ (intestine, bladder, liver) → tissue → worm → tegument → muscle → enzyme [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle for schistosomes: adult worms live in venules, eggs lodge in tissue, miracidia hatch in water, cercariae penetrate skin, and worms mature in portal vessels [S, SLIDE 6, 7]. [S] Praziquantel is effective against all species (SLIDE 10).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is transmission across environments: a parasite must survive outside the host, find an intermediate host, and re-enter a human. [S] The schistosome life cycle uses a snail intermediate host and skin penetration by cercariae (SLIDE 6, 7). [K] Physically, a cercaria swims and responds to light and chemical cues; skin penetration is aided by lytic enzymes and mechanical movement [K]. [I] Each transition is a bottleneck, which is why interrupting any one of them controls disease.

Just-in-time foundations. Trematode = fluke [S]; cestode = tapeworm [S]; nematode = roundworm [S]; definitive host = hosts the adult worm; intermediate host = hosts the larval stage [S]; miracidium = hatching larva [S]; cercaria = infective larva from snail [S]; scolex = attaching head of tapeworm; strobila = chain of proglottids [S]; proglottid = segment containing reproductive organs [S]; oncosphere = larval embryo [S]; haematuria = blood in urine [S]; cercarial dermatitis = swimmer's itch [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Five medically important Schistosoma species: S. mansoni, S. japonicum, S. intercalatum, S. mekongi cause intestinal disease and S. haematobium urinary disease (SLIDE 4). [S] Adult worms live in pairs, female in the male's gynecophoral canal; eggs pass into venules, rupture tissues with lytic enzymes and leave in stool or urine (SLIDE 6). [S] Miracidia infect specific snails — Bulinus for S. haematobium, Biomphalaria for S. mansoni, Oncomelania for S. japonicum (SLIDE 5 note), and cercariae penetrate human skin (SLIDE 7). [S] S. haematobium causes terminal haematuria, painful micturition, cystitis and bladder squamous cell carcinoma; S. mansoni causes swimmer's itch, dysentery and spleen/liver enlargement (SLIDE 8). [S] Diagnosis: eggs with lateral spine in stool for S. mansoni, terminal spine in urine sediment for S. haematobium (SLIDE 9). [S] Tapeworms are hermaphroditic, have scolex with suckers and strobila with proglottids, lack a digestive system and absorb food (SLIDE 11). [S] Taenia saginata uses cattle as intermediate host; humans get it from undercooked meat (SLIDE 12, 14). [S] Nematodes are unsegmented cylindrical worms with a complete gut; Ascaris larvae migrate through heart and lungs before maturing in the small intestine (SLIDE 18, 19).

Historical/research layer. [S] The deck is a modern parasitology teaching set (SLIDE 1). [K] Schistosomiasis was described by Bilharz in 1851; praziquantel was introduced in the 1970s [K] — names not on slides. [K] Unresolved: drug resistance monitoring, vaccine development, and the effect of climate change on snail ranges [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: stool for intestinal species, urine (after sedimentation) for S. haematobium (SLIDE 9). [K] Method: microscopy for eggs; concentration techniques, serology and PCR where available [K]. [S] Reference range: no parasite eggs in stool or urine [I]. [S] Error sources: intermittent egg shedding, wrong species differentiation by spine position, and inadequate sample (SLIDE 9) [I]. [K] QC: positive control slides and trained microscopists [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No sensitivity data are given.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Chronic infection causes organ damage. [K] S. haematobium is a recognised cause of bladder cancer in endemic areas; chronic S. mansoni causes portal hypertension and splenomegaly [S + K]. [K] Taenia solium can cause cysticercosis if eggs are ingested — a distinct disease from taeniasis [K]. [S] Prevention: health education, safe water, latrines, snail control by physical, biological and chemical methods (SLIDE 10).

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [S] Praziquantel is effective against all schistosome species; oxamniquine for S. mansoni, metrifonate for S. haematobium (SLIDE 10). [S] Niclosamide 2 g single dose and albendazole 400 mg daily for 3 days treat T. saginata; niclosamide 4 tablets chewed daily for 5 days treats H. nana (SLIDE 15, 17). [K] Praziquantel increases membrane permeability to calcium, causing tegument damage and paralysis; albendazole binds β-tubulin and blocks microtubule formation [K] — mechanisms are model knowledge.

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: praziquantel resistance surveillance, a schistosomiasis vaccine, and snail-control strategies under climate change [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No frontier material on slides.

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Calcium accounting in praziquantel action: Ca²⁺ enters the worm down its electrochemical gradient after drug-induced permeability change; the gradient stores the energy, not the drug [K]. Albendazole binding to β-tubulin prevents microtubule polymerisation; GTP-tubulin energy is not converted to structure, so the worm cannot maintain its tegument [K]. [I] No redox is claimed by the slides.

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Ascaris female lays up to 240,000 eggs per day (SLIDE 21). Over 30 days: 240,000 × 30 = 7,200,000 eggs ✓. [S] Development to adult takes 2–3 months (SLIDE 19, 21): at 30 days/month, 2 months = 60 days, 3 months = 90 days ✓. [S] Treatment doses from SLIDE 15: albendazole 400 mg × 3 days = 1,200 mg total ✓; niclosamide 2 g single dose = 2,000 mg ✓. [S] Schistosome species count: 5 total, 4 intestinal + 1 urinary = 5 ✓ (SLIDE 4).

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Complex life cycles create multiple control points and diagnostic stages.
  • Mechanistic Story. Egg → miracidium → snail → cercaria → skin → adult → egg.
  • Clinical Story. Organ-specific disease follows where eggs lodge.
  • MLS Story. Stool and urine microscopy with spine identification.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Praziquantel, oxamniquine, metrifonate, niclosamide, albendazole.
  • Research Story. Resistance, vaccines and snail ecology are open.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Recombinant vaccines and CRISPR gene drives target snails [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Anthelmintics require WHO prequalification for mass drug administration [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Geographic models map snail and infection risk [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Intermediate hosts expand transmission opportunities [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Mass drug administration programmes are public-health delivery innovations [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Tapeworms have a gut" — they absorb food; "all schistosomes cause urinary disease" — only S. haematobium.
  • Memory Hook. Lateral spine = mansoni; terminal spine = haematobium.
  • Connections. Life cycle → diagnostic stage → drug → prevention.

Source: Helminths Parasitology.pptx — SLIDE 6.

Pharmacognosy and materia medica

Biological geography. [S] Pharmacognosy is the study of materia medica — healing substances from natural sources, mostly plants but also minerals and animals (General Introduction to Pharmacognosy SLIDE 4). Zoom: natural product → plant → organ (bark, root) → tissue → cell → metabolite → target [I]. [K] Molecule lifecycle: a secondary metabolite is made in a plant cell, stored in a vacuole or glandular trichome, extracted during preparation, absorbed by the patient, metabolised by CYPs and excreted [K]. [S] The slides contrast oral folklore with written papyrus, clay tablets, parchment and herbals (SLIDE 6).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is preserving knowledge without printing. [S] Before printing, medical information travelled orally and through papyrus, clay, parchment and manuscripts (SLIDE 6). [I] Each medium has a different durability — clay survives millennia, papyrus survives only in dry climates — which is why the historical record is biased toward Egypt and Mesopotamia [S, SLIDE 5].

Just-in-time foundations. Materia medica = collected knowledge of therapeutic properties of healing substances [S]; pharmacognosy = study of natural medicinal materials [S]; folklore = orally transmitted knowledge [S]; cuneiform = wedge-shaped writing on clay [S]; papyrus = writing material from Cyperus aquaticus [S]; herbal = manuscript describing plants [S]; de Materia Medica = Dioscorides' work [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] The history of pharmacy is identical to the use of pharmacognosy or materia medica (SLIDE 4). [S] Records exist from Greek, Japanese, Indian and Chinese traditions but not from Australia, most of Africa and South America because knowledge was unwritten or documents were destroyed (SLIDE 5). [S] Information was transmitted orally, on the Ebers papyrus (c. 1500 BC), on about 660 cuneiform tablets from Nineveh (c. 650 BC), and on parchment and herbals (SLIDE 6, 7). [S] Ebers papyrus herbs include senna, honey, thyme, juniper and cumin for digestion, and pomegranate root and henbane for worms (SLIDE 8). [S] Dioscorides is the Father of Pharmacognosy and described over 600 medicinal plants (SLIDE 9). [S] Shen Nung (about 2700 BC) is credited with the first Pen T-Sao recording 365 drugs such as podophyllum, rhubarb, ginseng and ephedra (SLIDE 10).

Historical/research layer. [S] The slides explicitly note that African and South American knowledge was not written down or was destroyed by invaders (SLIDE 5) — an important honesty point about historical bias [S]. [K] The term pharmacognosy was coined in the 19th century; the discipline later narrowed from whole crude drugs to natural products chemistry [K]. [K] Unresolved: reconstructing lost ethnopharmacological knowledge ethically [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] Specimen: crude plant material and historical texts [I]. [K] Modern pharmacognosy uses microscopy, chromatography and spectroscopy to authenticate materials [K]. [K] Reference range: not applicable historically; modern monographs supply limits [I]. [S] Error sources: destroyed or absent written records, translation errors, and misidentified plants (SLIDE 5) [S + I]. [K] QC: voucher specimens and authenticated reference samples [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No analytical protocol is given.

Clinical/medical layer. [I] The clinical thread is continuity: remedies recorded centuries ago are still in use. [K] Senna remains a laxative (anthraquinones stimulate bowel motility), honey is used in wound care, and ephedra yielded ephedrine, a bronchodilator [S + K]. [K] Historical texts are not evidence of efficacy; they are hypotheses to test [I].

Pharmaceutical layer — named agents at exact mechanism points. [K] Morphine from Papaver somniferum is a μ-opioid agonist; quinine from Cinchona inhibits haemozoin formation; digoxin from Digitalis inhibits Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase; aspirin from willow inhibits cyclooxygenase; ephedrine from Ephedra is a mixed adrenergic agonist [S + K]. [S] The slides mention ephedra, podophyllum, rhubarb and ginseng as still recognised drugs (SLIDE 10).

Research/frontier layer. [K] Open: recovering lost knowledge through oral-history partnerships; using metabolomics to rediscover historical preparations; and ensuring benefit-sharing [K]. [SLIDES SILENT] No frontier discussion in the deck.

Atomic/electron/energy accounting. [K] Anthraquinone laxatives are glycosides; gut bacteria cleave the sugar to release the active aglycone — a conserved hydrolysis, not energy production [K]. [K] Morphine's alkaloid skeleton derives from tyrosine; atoms are conserved through the biosynthetic pathway [K]. [I] These are model-knowledge additions to a historically focused slide set.

Worked example (arithmetic-verified). [S] Ebers papyrus contains 876 prescriptions and more than 500 different substances (Role of Science SLIDE 10). Average substances per prescription = 500/876 ≈ 0.57 if treated as distinct, which is impossible for a one-to-one listing — so the two figures must count different things (prescriptions vs distinct ingredients), a logical consistency check ✓. [S] Shen Nung's 365 drugs and Dioscorides' over 600 plants: 600 − 365 = 235 more plants in the Greek work ✓ (SLIDES 9, 10). [S] The nineveh tablets number about 660 (SLIDE 6) — illustrative counting, not slide arithmetic.

End-of-section synthesis.

  • Big Picture. Pharmacognosy is the bridge between natural materials and modern drugs.
  • Mechanistic Story. Plant metabolite → extraction → active compound → molecular target.
  • Clinical Story. Old remedies persist but need modern validation.
  • MLS Story. Authentication and standardisation are the lab contribution.
  • Pharmaceutical Story. Morphine, quinine, digoxin and aspirin are named outputs.
  • Research Story. Reconstructing lost knowledge is open and ethically sensitive.
  • Biotech Story. [K] Metabolic engineering produces natural products in microbes [K].
  • Manufacturing Story. [K] Botanical supply chains need sustainable sourcing [K].
  • Computational Story. [K] Natural-product databases and docking prioritise leads [K].
  • Evolutionary Story. [K] Plant secondary metabolites evolved as defences [K].
  • Innovation Story. [K] Bioprospecting with benefit-sharing is a translational model [K].
  • Common Misconceptions. "Historical use equals proof" — it is a clue; "pharmacognosy is only plant identification" — it includes chemistry and pharmacology.
  • Memory Hook. Materia medica; Dioscorides 600 plants; Shen Nung 365 drugs.
  • Connections. Historical record → plant → compound → drug → validation.

Source: General Introduction to Pharmacognosy.pdf — SLIDE 4.

Alkenes: structure, naming and reactions

Biological geography. [S] Alkenes are organic compounds with a carbon–carbon double bond (Pharm 152 Alkenes 2025 SLIDE 4). This is a chemistry topic, so the "geography" is molecular: functional group → carbon skeleton → π bond → electrophilic attack site. [I] In a drug molecule the alkene can be a rigid spacer that fixes geometry, or a reactive handle. [K] Molecule lifecycle of an alkene drug: synthesised by elimination, absorbed, sometimes epoxidised by CYP450, and excreted after conjugation [K]. [S] The slides state the principal reaction is addition across the π bond (SLIDE 19).

Engineering/physics-first framing. The problem is geometry and reactivity: a C=C double bond is shorter and stronger than C–C but its π electrons are exposed and polarisable. [K] σ bonds come from head-on orbital overlap; the π bond comes from sideways overlap above and below the plane, so it is weaker and its electrons are accessible to electrophiles [K]. [S] The slides say π electrons are susceptible to electrophiles (SLIDE 19). [I] This is why alkenes react where alkanes do not.

Just-in-time foundations. Alkene = hydrocarbon with C=C [S]; π bond = the sideways-overlap component of the double bond; σ bond = head-on overlap [K]; electrophile = electron-poor species seeking electrons [S]; substitution = number of carbon groups attached to the double-bond carbons [S]; Z (zusammen) = higher-priority groups on the same side; E (entgegen) = opposite sides [S]; Zaitsev's rule = the more substituted alkene is favoured in elimination [S]; addition = π and σ bonds replaced by two σ bonds [S].

Mechanistic layer. [S] Naming: change -ane to -ene for the longest chain containing the double bond, number from the end nearer the double bond, and locate substituents (SLIDE 4). [S] Alkenyl substituents include vinyl, allyl and isopropenyl (SLIDE 5). [S] Cis/trans (Z/E) describes configuration; priority is by atomic number, so Br outranks Cl (SLIDE 6, 7). [S] Cis/trans is unambiguous only for disubstituted alkenes; tri- and tetrasubstituted ones need E/Z (SLIDE 6). [S] Stability rises with degree of substitution; cis alkyl groups create van der Waals strain (SLIDE 8). [S] Synthesis by dehydrohalogenation of alkyl halides (E2, alkoxide base, polar solvent) and by dehydration of alcohols with strong acid, more easily for 3° than 2° than 1° alcohols (SLIDE 10–13). [S] Zaitsev's rule favours the more stable alkene (SLIDE 16). [S] Addition converts one π and one σ bond into two σ bonds (SLIDE 19); hydrogen halides add rapidly in solvents such as pentane, chloroform, acetic acid and benzene (SLIDE 20).

Historical/research layer. [K] The IUPAC naming system was formalised in the 20th century; Zaitsev's rule dates to 1875 [K] — the slides give the rules without attribution. [K] Unresolved: stereoselective alkene synthesis and alkene metathesis catalysts, which won the 2005 Nobel Prize in Chemistry [K].

MLS/diagnostic layer. [S] No diagnostic specimen or clinical method is given — [SLIDES SILENT]. [K] Laboratory relevance is analytical: gas chromatography or NMR identifies alkene-containing compounds and determines E/Z geometry [K]. [K] Reference range and QC are not applicable to a pure organic chemistry topic [I]. [S] Error sources stated in the slides are conceptual: misapplying cis/trans to tri-substituted alkenes and mis-numbering the chain (SLIDE 4, 6) [S].

Clinical/medical layer. [I] Alkene geometry changes drug shape and receptor fit. [K] Cis/trans fatty acids have different membrane effects; trans fats pack more tightly and raise LDL cholesterol [K]. [K] Tamoxifen's Z/E geometry affects oestrogen-receptor binding; retinoids contain conjugated double bonds essential for activity [K].
