Source: STA__Statistical_Methods_3_Med_Sch_1_pdf.txt (Owiredu, slides "Basic Concepts of Probability" p.2–7: experiment, sample space, outcome); STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_pdf.txt (Quiz 3, Question 4); STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_ANSWER_KEY_pdf.txt (section 3.4, p.11).

Conditional probability and independence

Locate it (biological geography)

From the diagnostic pathway down: a patient has a symptom, which restricts the population under consideration; a test is interpreted within that restricted group. Conditional probability is the mathematics of "given what we already know". Input: an event of interest and a conditioning event; output: a revised probability. Independence is the special case where knowing the second event changes nothing.

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

Conditioning shrinks the sample space. If we know $B$ occurred, only outcomes in $B$ remain possible; of those, the ones also in $A$ are $A\cap B$. Hence $$P(A\mid B)=\frac{P(A\cap B)}{P(B)},\qquad P(B)>0.$$ Rearranging gives the multiplication rule $P(A\cap B)=P(A\mid B)P(B)$ — the probability of both events is the chance of the second times the chance of the first given the second.

Independence means $P(A\mid B)=P(A)$, so $P(A\cap B)=P(A)P(B)$. This is a factorisation statement, not a claim about causality. The law of total probability then becomes $P(A)=P(A\mid B)P(B)+P(A\mid B^c)P(B^c)$, splitting the problem over two exhaustive cases. The complement shortcut $P(\text{at least one})=1-P(\text{none})$ follows from the same partition.

Zero-assumption ledger

$P(A\mid B)$ = probability of $A$ given $B$ ("A given B"). $A\cap B$ = both events. $P(B)>0$ required. $A^c$ = complement of $A$. Independent = factorisation $P(A\cap B)=P(A)P(B)$. $M,W$ = events in the worked example (man watches, woman watches).

Quantity and unit accounting

All quantities are pure probabilities. In the worked example $P(W)=0.5$ and $P(M\mid W)=0.7$; multiplying gives $P(M\cap W)=0.35$. Check: $P(M)=0.4$ is given, so $P(M\mid W)=0.7\ne P(M)=0.4$ — the events are not independent, and the multiplication rule must use the conditional probability. Using $0.4\times0.5=0.20$ would be an accounting error: that assumes independence the data deny.

Six-layer check (concepts: conditional probability; multiplication rule; independence)

  • Mechanistic. Sample-space restriction derivation given above; independence as factorisation; total probability as a partition identity.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Thomas Bayes's 1763 essay formalised inverse reasoning; the modern notation $P(A\mid B)$ and the axiomatic treatment came later. Unresolved: conditioning on events of probability zero requires careful limiting arguments (regular conditional probabilities).
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] Sensitivity $P(+\mid D)$ and specificity $P(-\mid D^c)$ are conditional probabilities with disease status as the conditioning event. Spectrum bias arises when the conditional probability differs across disease severity — a source of error the single number hides. Reference standards define $D$; an imperfect standard corrupts every conditional estimate.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] A positive predictive value $P(D\mid+)$ answers the patient-facing question, while sensitivity answers the test-performance question; swapping them is the classic diagnostic error. Symptoms act as conditioning events that change pre-test probability.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] CYP2C19 genotype changes the probability of clopidogrel activation; the probability of a poor outcome is conditional on genotype. This is a named pharmacogenomic example of conditioning altering clinical decision-making.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: estimating conditional probabilities in high-dimensional subgroups without overfitting, and avoiding collider bias when conditioning on variables affected by both exposure and outcome.

Transfer check

Three tests separate correct conditioning from guesswork. First, the denominator must be the probability of the conditioning event: $P(A\mid B)$ divides by $P(B)$, never by $P(A)$. Second, $P(A\mid B)$ and $P(B\mid A)$ answer different questions; in the worked data $P(M\mid W)=0.7$ but $P(W\mid M)=P(M\cap W)/P(M)=0.35/0.4=0.875$ — Python confirms 0.35/0.4=0.875. Third, independence is a testable equality, not an assumption to make for convenience: compare $P(A\mid B)$ with $P(A)$; here $0.7\ne0.4$, so independence is rejected. A transfer question: a test has sensitivity 0.9 and specificity 0.8 in a population with prevalence 0.2. What is $P(\text{disease}\mid+)$? First $P(+)=0.9(0.2)+0.2(0.8)=0.18+0.16=0.34$, then $P(D\mid+)=0.18/0.34\approx0.529$ (Python confirms). Notice that conditioning flips the direction: the same joint probability is divided by a different event. Collider bias is the frontier warning: conditioning on a variable caused by both exposure and outcome can create a spurious association, so "adjusting for everything" is not automatically safe.

Boundary note

Conditioning can reverse an association — Simpson's paradox — when subgroups have different sizes and baselines. That is why a single conditional probability must be reported with the group it conditions on, not as a universal constant. A second boundary: $P(A\mid B)$ is undefined when $P(B)=0$; conditioning on an event that never occurred has no sample space to restrict. A third: multiplication rules need the right conditional probability for the order chosen — $P(A\cap B)=P(A\mid B)P(B)=P(B\mid A)P(A)$, and the two expressions agree only when the multiplication rule is applied consistently. Python check on the worked data: $0.7(0.5)=0.35$ and $0.875(0.4)=0.35$, confirming both routes. If they disagree, one conditional probability was mislabelled.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: $P(M)=0.4$, $P(W)=0.5$, $P(M\mid W)=0.7$. Find $P(M\cap W)$.

Step 1 — identify the rule. We need both events; we have a conditional probability, so use the multiplication rule.

Step 2 — apply. $P(M\cap W)=P(M\mid W)P(W)=0.7\times0.5=0.35$.

Step 3 — independence check. Independence would require $P(M\cap W)=P(M)P(W)=0.4\times0.5=0.20\ne0.35$; therefore do not use the simpler product.

Step 4 — independent check. Python 0.70.5=0.35 and 0.40.5=0.2, confirming the distinction.

Answer: $P(M\cap W)=0.35$.

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Genotype-conditional penetrance is a conditional probability; it changes with the genetic background.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Conditional defect probabilities given a machine setting drive process adjustment.
  • Computational/AI. Bayesian networks encode conditional independencies graphically; P(A|B,C) is computed by factor products.
  • Evolutionary-Design. Conditioning survived because evidence arrives sequentially; the ability to revise with new information is more useful than static counts.
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Diagnostic-decision products sell conditional-probability updates at the point of care.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. $P(A\mid B)\ne P(B\mid A)$. Independence is a factorisation, not "unrelated in any sense". Connects directly to Bayes.

Source: STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_pdf.txt (Quiz 2, Question 4); STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_ANSWER_KEY_pdf.txt (section 2.4, p.7–8); STA__KNUST_Stats_Exam_100Q_pdf.txt (Q10, Q14, Q18, Q25).

Bayes' theorem and screening tests

Locate it (biological geography)

From the screening programme down: a population has a prevalence, a test has sensitivity and specificity, a patient receives a result, and the clinician needs the probability that the patient truly has the condition. Bayes's theorem carries the probability from the test's performance back to the patient. Input: prior probability and test characteristics; output: posterior probability. The chain is population → prior → likelihood of result → posterior → decision.

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

Derive Bayes from the multiplication rule applied twice to the same joint event: $$P(D\cap+)=P(+\mid D)P(D)=P(D\mid+)P(+).$$ Equating the two expressions and dividing by $P(+)$ gives $$P(D\mid+)=\frac{P(+\mid D)P(D)}{P(+)}.$$ Expand the denominator by total probability over $D$ and $D^c$: $$P(D\mid+)=\frac{P(+\mid D)P(D)}{P(+\mid D)P(D)+P(+\mid D^c)P(D^c)}.$$ This is not a new assumption — it is the multiplication rule plus a partition. It explains the base-rate effect algebraically: if $P(D)$ is small, the first term in the denominator is small even when $P(+\mid D)$ is near 1, while the false-positive term $P(+\mid D^c)P(D^c)$ can dominate.

In likelihood-ratio form: posterior odds = prior odds × LR, where $LR+=P(+\mid D)/P(+\mid D^c)$ and odds $=P/(1-P)$. This form makes sequential updating easy: each new independent result multiplies the current odds by another LR.

Zero-assumption ledger

$D$ = event "has disease"; $D^c$ = "does not". $+$ = positive test; $-$ = negative test. $P(D)$ = prevalence (prior). Sensitivity $=P(+\mid D)$; specificity $=P(-\mid D^c)$; false-positive rate $=P(+\mid D^c)=1-\text{specificity}$. PPV $=P(D\mid+)$; NPV $=P(D^c\mid-)$. LR+ = positive likelihood ratio. Odds $=P/(1-P)$.

Quantity and unit accounting

For the malaria example: $P(+)=0.93(0.12)+0.13(0.88)=0.1116+0.1144=0.2260$. Numerator $0.1116$; denominator $0.2260$; PPV $=0.4938$. Accounting check: true positives per 100 patients = 11.16; false positives = 11.44; total positives = 22.60; fraction truly diseased = 11.16/22.60 = 0.4938. Every positive is accounted for. The common error is quoting sensitivity (0.93) as the answer — that is $P(+\mid D)$, not $P(D\mid+)$.

Six-layer check (concepts: Bayes theorem; PPV/NPV; sequential updating)

  • Mechanistic. Derived from multiplication rule and total probability; base-rate effect shown algebraically; odds form derived.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Bayes's 1763 essay was largely neglected; Laplace developed inverse probability further; frequentist methods displaced it in the early 20th century, and Bayesian inference returned with modern computing and diagnostic medicine. Unresolved: the choice of prior remains the main controversy, and sensitivity analyses are required.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] Screening programmes must report PPV/NPV, not only sensitivity/specificity, because PPV depends on prevalence. Specimen quality and reference-standard errors alter sensitivity; spectrum bias alters it across severity. QC of a test kit verifies sensitivity/specificity under controlled conditions, but field PPV will differ if prevalence differs.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] A malaria rapid diagnostic test in a low-prevalence setting can have a PPV below 50 % despite high sensitivity and specificity — a direct clinical consequence of the arithmetic above. HIV testing uses sequential algorithms precisely because repeated positives multiply LRs. Negative predictive value matters more in high-prevalence rule-out settings.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] Pharmacogenomic testing (e.g. CYP2C19 before clopidogrel) is a Bayesian decision: genotype result updates the probability of reduced activation. Bayesian adaptive trial designs use the same updating to modify allocation probabilities mid-trial.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: prior elicitation from heterogeneous evidence, and updating when repeated tests are correlated rather than independent (the "twice positive" calculation assumes conditional independence and can overstate certainty).

Transfer check

The odds form makes the mechanism portable. Prior odds for the malaria example are $0.12/0.88\approx0.13636$; $LR+=0.93/0.13\approx7.1538$; posterior odds $=0.13636\times7.1538\approx0.9756$, giving posterior probability $0.9756/(1+0.9756)=0.4938$ — identical to the direct Bayes result (Python confirms both routes). This decomposition is why the same calculation works for a positive and a negative result: $LR-=P(-\mid D)/P(-\mid D^c)=0.07/0.87\approx0.0805$ multiplies the odds downward for a negative test. Boundary checks: if sensitivity equals the false-positive rate, $LR+=1$ and the test carries no information — the posterior equals the prior. If prevalence is 0 or 1, the posterior is fixed regardless of result, an algebraic consequence of the prior term vanishing. A transfer question: a test with sensitivity 0.99 and specificity 0.95 applied twice independently to a disease with prevalence 0.001 gives $P(D\mid++)\approx0.282$, but a third correlated test (same specimen, same platform) cannot be multiplied again — correlation breaks the independence assumption. Always ask whether repeated results are conditionally independent before multiplying LRs.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: Malaria test: sensitivity 0.93, specificity 0.87, prevalence 0.12. Patient tests positive. Find PPV.

Step 1 — list inputs. $P(D)=0.12$, $P(D^c)=0.88$, $P(+\mid D)=0.93$, $P(+\mid D^c)=1-0.87=0.13$.

Step 2 — total probability of positive. $P(+)=0.93\times0.12+0.13\times0.88=0.1116+0.1144=0.2260$.

Step 3 — Bayes. $\text{PPV}=P(D\mid+)=0.1116/0.2260=0.493805...$

Step 4 — independent check. Python (0.930.12)/((0.930.12)+(0.13*0.88)) returns 0.4938053097, and per-100 counting gives 11.16/22.60, the same value.

Answer: PPV = 0.4938 (about 49 %).

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Diagnostic assay development reports sensitivity/specificity; field performance requires Bayesian translation using local prevalence.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Lot screening uses the same logic: a positive screen's predictive value depends on the true defect rate.
  • Computational/AI. Naive Bayes classifiers multiply likelihood ratios across features; spam and clinical coding are classic uses.
  • Evolutionary-Design. Bayesian updating survived because organisms and clinicians must act on incomplete evidence and revise when new evidence arrives.
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Rapid-test and decision-support products live or die by PPV in the target population, not by sensitivity on a research dataset.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. Sensitivity is $P(+\mid D)$; PPV is $P(D\mid+)$. Low prevalence lowers PPV. Repeated tests must be conditionally independent for the simple multiply-LRs trick.

Source: STA__KNUST_Stats_Exam_100Q_pdf.txt (Q1 and worked solution p.14); STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_ANSWER_KEY_pdf.txt (sections 1.1–1.4, 2.1, 5.1–5.2, 7.3–7.4); STA__ACING_STATS_pdf.txt (Bayesian practice set).

Discrete random variables

Locate it (biological geography)

From the laboratory count downward: number of defective tablets in a blister, number of children affected in a family, number of defective items in a sampled lot. A discrete random variable $X$ assigns a number to each outcome of a chance process. Input: the random process and its probability rule; output: a distribution, an expected value and a variance. These are the tools that turn "how many?" into "how many on average, and how variable?"

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

A probability mass function (PMF) $P(X=x)$ gives the probability of each attainable value $x$. It must satisfy $P(X=x)\ge0$ and $\sum_x P(X=x)=1$ — the second condition is an accounting identity used to find unknown constants. The expected value $E(X)=\sum_x xP(X=x)$ is the probability-weighted average; it is the long-run mean of many repetitions. The variance is derived by expanding the squared deviation: $$\operatorname{Var}(X)=E[(X-E(X))^2]=E(X^2)-[E(X)]^2,$$ where $E(X^2)=\sum_x x^2P(X=x)$. The shortcut subtracts the square of the mean from the mean of the squares; both terms are computed from the same PMF.

Two named families: if $X\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,p)$ ( $n$ independent trials, success probability $p$ ), then $P(X=k)=\binom{n}{k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}$, $E(X)=np$, $\operatorname{Var}(X)=np(1-p)$ — derived by summing the Bernoulli indicator variables. If events occur at rate $\lambda$ and $X\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)$, then $P(X=k)=e^{-\lambda}\lambda^k/k!$ and both mean and variance equal $\lambda$ — derived as a limit of the binomial when $n$ is large and $p$ small. The geometric distribution gives $P(X=k)=(1-p)^{k-1}p$ for the first success on trial $k$.

Zero-assumption ledger

$X$ = discrete random variable. $x,k$ = attainable values (integers). $P(X=x)$ = PMF. $E(X)$ = expected value. $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ = variance. $E(X^2)$ = expected value of the squared variable. $n$ = number of Bernoulli trials; $p$ = success probability; $q=1-p$. $\lambda$ = Poisson rate (mean events per interval). $e\approx2.718$. $\binom{n}{k}=n!/[k!(n-k)!]$.

Quantity and unit accounting

If $X$ counts defective sweets in a packet, $X$ is dimensionless (a count), so $E(X)$ and $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ are dimensionless counts². If $X$ is a monetary payoff in cedis, $E(X)$ is in cedis and variance in cedis². In the worked example, $\sum k(k+1)^2=0+4+18+48+100=170$ and $\sum(k+1)^2=55$, so $E(X)=170/55=3.0909$ — every $k$ contributes once with its probability $k$-weight. Checking $\sum P(X=x)=55/55=1$ precedes all other calculations.

Six-layer check (concepts: PMF; expectation/variance; binomial and Poisson)

  • Mechanistic. PMF normalisation, variance expansion, binomial and Poisson derivations given above.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Jacob Bernoulli treated repeated trials in the 17th century; Siméon Poisson published the rare-event limit in 1837. The binomial replaced ad hoc enumeration. Unresolved: overdispersion — real count data often have variance greater than the Poisson mean, requiring negative-binomial or beta-binomial models.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] QC sampling plans count defective units; acceptance probability is a binomial tail. Counting errors (misclassification of a defect) alter the PMF itself, not just the estimate. A lot sampled without replacement is technically hypergeometric, not binomial — a named model boundary.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] The Aa × Aa cross gives a discrete distribution over genotypes (AA 1/4, Aa 1/2, aa 1/4); the probability that an unaffected child is a carrier is $(1/2)/(3/4)=2/3$. Counts of hospital admissions or complications per patient are discrete and often Poisson-like.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] Blister-pack defect counts and tablet-count audits are binomial-type; the expected number of defective units in $n$ inspected tablets is $np$. Specific acceptable-quality limits are pharmacopoeial standards, not derived here.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: zero-inflated counts (many zeros plus a Poisson-like tail), and overdispersion in multicentre data.

Transfer check

A distribution question is answered by checking the model against the data's shape, not by choosing a formula at random. For a binomial setting, verify: fixed $n$, two outcomes, constant $p$, independence. For a Poisson setting, verify: counts of independent events in a fixed interval, with mean roughly equal to variance. Python check on the KNUST Poisson example with $\lambda=3.2$: 1-scipy.stats.poisson.cdf(1,3.2) gives about 0.8288, matching the exam solution — a quick way to verify hand arithmetic. A transfer question: if a count series has variance much larger than its mean, the Poisson model is wrong; a negative-binomial model adds a variance parameter. Another: the binomial variance $np(1-p)$ is at most $n/4$ (when $p=0.5$) — Python check for $n=10$: $10(0.5)(0.5)=2.5$, the maximum. Finally, expectation is linear even without independence: $E(2X-3)=2E(X)-3$, while variance is not: $\operatorname{Var}(2X-3)=4\operatorname{Var}(X)$ — the constant shifts the mean but leaves spread unchanged. Misapplying linearity to variance is a frequent examination error.

Boundary note

A proposed PMF must pass two gates before any calculation: all probabilities non-negative, and their sum exactly 1. In the worked example the gate is $55/55=1$; in a table, a missing or duplicated value fails it. If a constant is solved incorrectly, the expectation that follows is meaningless regardless of arithmetic care. A useful independent check is the range rule: $E(X)$ must lie between the smallest and largest attainable values — here 3.09 lies between 0 and 4, so the answer is plausible; a result of 30 would flag an error immediately. Similarly, variance cannot be negative; obtaining a negative variance means either the wrong formula or a failed normalisation. Python check: summing the five worked PMF probabilities $1/55,4/55,9/55,16/55,25/55$ for $x=0\ldots4$ gives 1.0 exactly.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: $P(X=x)=k(x+1)^2$ for $x=0,1,2,3,4$. Find $k$, $E(X)$ and $\operatorname{Var}(X)$.

Step 1 — normalise. $\sum_{x=0}^{4}(x+1)^2=1+4+9+16+25=55$, so $55k=1$, $k=1/55$.

Step 2 — expectation. $E(X)=\frac{0(1)+1(4)+2(9)+3(16)+4(25)}{55}=\frac{170}{55}\approx3.0909$.

Step 3 — second moment. $E(X^2)=\frac{0(1)+1(4)+4(9)+9(16)+16(25)}{55}=\frac{584}{55}\approx10.6182$.

Step 4 — variance and check. $\operatorname{Var}=10.6182-(3.0909)^2\approx1.0645$. Python returns $k=0.0181818$, $E=3.090909$, $\operatorname{Var}=1.0644628$, matching.

Answer: $k=1/55$, $E(X)\approx3.091$, $\operatorname{Var}(X)\approx1.064$.

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Sequencing variant counts and colony counts are discrete; Poisson assumptions are checked against observed variance.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Acceptance sampling uses binomial tails to set lot-release risk.
  • Computational/AI. scipy.stats.binom and poisson implement PMFs; simulations approximate them by drawing random variates.
  • Evolutionary-Design. Counts survived as the natural description of indivisible events; the Poisson arises whenever independent rare events are pooled.
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Sampling-plan software and QC dashboards monetise these distributions.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. Variance is not $E(X)^2$. Binomial variance $np(1-p)$, not $np$. Connects to the binomial theorem and to Bayes (discrete event probabilities).

Source: STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_pdf.txt (Quiz 6, Question 1); STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_ANSWER_KEY_pdf.txt (section 6.1, p.17–18); STA__KNUST_Stats_Exam_100Q_pdf.txt (Q26–Q45 and solutions p.15–16).

Continuous random variables

Locate it (biological geography)

From the analyser down: a concentration, a waiting time, a blood pressure reading — values that can take any number in an interval. A continuous random variable $X$ is described by a density, not by a list of point probabilities. Input: a density or cumulative distribution; output: interval probabilities, means, variances and percentiles. The laboratory reports a single value, but the model assigns probability to ranges, never to exact points (which have probability zero).

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

A probability density function (PDF) $f(x)$ is a height function whose area over an interval is probability: $P(a<X<b)=\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$, requiring $f(x)\ge0$ and $\int f(x)\,dx=1$. The cumulative distribution function (CDF) $F(x)=P(X\le x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}f(t)\,dt$ accumulates area from the left; hence $P(a<X<b)=F(b)-F(a)$ and $f(x)=F'(x)$ where $F$ is differentiable. Expectation is the continuous weighted average $E(X)=\int x f(x)\,dx$, and $\operatorname{Var}(X)=E(X^2)-[E(X)]^2$ with $E(X^2)=\int x^2f(x)\,dx$ — exactly the discrete formulas with sums replaced by integrals.

A median $m$ solves $F(m)=0.5$; a percentile $x_p$ solves $F(x_p)=p/100$. The uniform distribution on $[a,b]$ has $f(x)=1/(b-a)$; the exponential with rate $\lambda$ has $f(x)=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}$ for $x>0$, mean $1/\lambda$, and the memoryless property.

Zero-assumption ledger

$X$ = continuous random variable. $f(x)$ = PDF (height, unit 1/unit of $x$). $F(x)$ = CDF. $a,b$ = interval endpoints. $E(X)$ = expected value. $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ = variance. $m$ = median. $x_p$ = $p$th percentile. $\lambda$ = exponential rate (per unit time). $\int$ = integral. $e$ = base of natural logs. $t$ = dummy integration variable.

Quantity and unit accounting

If $x$ is years, $f(x)$ has units per year, so $f(x)\,dx$ is dimensionless probability. For the worked CDF $F(x)=\ln(1+x)$ on $0<x<e-1$, the density is $f(x)=1/(1+x)$; integrating $xf(x)$ from 0 to $e-1$ gives $e-2\approx0.718$, with the same units as $x$. Median check: $F(m)=0.5\Rightarrow\ln(1+m)=0.5\Rightarrow m=\sqrt e-1\approx0.6487$, which lies inside the support $(0,1.718)$ — a support check that must never be skipped.

Six-layer check (concepts: PDF/CDF; expectation/variance; percentiles; uniform/exponential)

  • Mechanistic. Area-as-probability, CDF-as-accumulation, derivative inverse, integral expectation — derived above.
  • Historical/Research. [context] The normal density is associated with Gauss and Laplace; the CDF concept was formalised in the 19th–20th centuries. Unresolved: density estimation from finite samples and modelling censored observations below assay detection limits.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] Analyte concentrations are continuous ratio-scale measurements; reference intervals are percentiles of the healthy distribution. Results below the lower limit of quantification are censored, not zero — treating them as exact numbers invents information and biases the mean. QC relies on the distributional assumption when setting control limits.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] Biomarker distributions are often right-skewed, so the median may be reported instead of the mean. Time-to-event outcomes (time to relapse) are continuous and modelled with exponential or survival distributions.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] The exponential distribution models waiting times between independent events; first-order elimination gives exponentially declining concentrations. Time to reach a threshold concentration is derived from the exponential CDF — a named point of application in dosing intervals.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: semicontinuous data with a spike at zero, limit-of-detection imputation, and non-parametric density estimation for skewed biomarkers.

Transfer check

Convert between the four representations fluently. Given a PDF, integrate to get the CDF; given a CDF, differentiate to get the PDF. For the worked example, $f(x)=1/(1+x)$ and integrating from 0 to $e-1$ gives total area $\ln(e)=1$ — the normalisation check. Expectation from the CDF using the survival identity $E(X)=\int_0^{\infty}[1-F(x)]dx$ for non-negative $X$ provides an independent route; Python numerical integration of $1-\ln(1+x)$ from 0 to $e-1$ gives about 0.718, matching $e-2$. Boundary checks: a CDF must be non-decreasing and run from 0 to 1; a density must be non-negative and integrate to 1; a median must lie inside the support (0.6487 is inside $(0,1.718)$ ✓). A transfer question: a uniform distribution on $[2,8]$ has density $1/6$; $P(3<X<6)=(6-3)/6=0.5$ (Python: 3/6=0.5). A common error is dividing by the range endpoints instead of the width. Another: for an exponential with mean 4, the rate is $\lambda=1/4$, not 4 — confusing mean and rate changes every later probability. Census-style exact values at a point are zero for continuous variables: $P(X=3)=0$, while $P(2<X<4)>0$.

Boundary note

A CDF must satisfy three properties simultaneously: non-decreasing, $F(-\infty)=0$, $F(\infty)=1$. The worked piecewise CDF meets them — it rises from 0 through $\ln(1+x)$ to 1 at $x=e-1$, then stays flat. Differentiating a CDF that is flat in a region gives zero density there, which is correct: no probability mass lies outside the support. A common error is evaluating the CDF branch outside its interval; at $x=5$ the correct value is 1, not $\ln6$ (which would exceed 1 and immediately break the third property). Another is confusing the density with probability: $f(1)=1/2$ is a height, not a probability, and can exceed 1 for narrow densities. Python check: math.log(6)=1.7918, impossible as a probability — the support check catches it before any answer is written.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: CDF $F(x)=\ln(1+x)$ for $0<x<e-1$ (0 below, 1 above). Find the median and $P(0.5<X<1)$.

Step 1 — median. Solve $F(m)=0.5$: $\ln(1+m)=0.5$, so $1+m=e^{0.5}$, $m=\sqrt e-1\approx1.648721-1=0.648721$.

Step 2 — interval probability. $P(0.5<X<1)=F(1)-F(0.5)=\ln2-\ln1.5=\ln(4/3)$.

Step 3 — evaluate. $\ln(4/3)\approx0.693147-0.405465=0.287682$.

Step 4 — support check and Python. Both 0.5 and 1 lie inside $(0,e-1)\approx(0,1.718)$, so the CDF branch is valid. Python math.exp(0.5)-1=0.6487212 and math.log(2)-math.log(1.5)=0.2876821.

Answer: median $\approx0.649$; $P(0.5<X<1)\approx0.288$.

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Single-cell measurements are continuous and often zero-inflated; density choice matters.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Particle size and coating thickness are continuous variables with specification limits derived from percentiles.
  • Computational/AI. scipy.stats provides CDF/PDF; Monte Carlo sampling draws from a CDF by inverse transform.
  • Evolutionary-Design. Continuous models survived because measurement instruments return real-valued readings; discretising loses information.
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Assay validation software estimates distributions and reference intervals from continuous data.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. $P(X=x)=0$ for continuous $X$; use intervals. Density can exceed 1. CDF is non-decreasing from 0 to 1.

Source: STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_pdf.txt (Quiz 6, Question 2); STA__GLI_STAT_153_QUIZZES_ANSWER_KEY_pdf.txt (section 6.2, p.18–19); STA__Statistical_Methods_4_Med_Sch_2_pdf.txt (slides "Random Variable / Continuous Random Variable" p.2); STA__Statistical_Methods_5_Med_Sch_2_pdf.txt (Uniform and Exponential sections).

The normal distribution and z-scores

Locate it (biological geography)

From the population down: birth weights, blood pressures and cholesterol levels cluster around a centre with symmetric scatter. The normal distribution models that shape. Input: a mean and a standard deviation; output: the probability of a range of values, or a percentile. In the laboratory it underpins reference intervals and QC limits; in clinical practice it turns a single measurement into "how unusual is this?"

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

Write $X\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, where $\mu$ is the mean and $\sigma^2$ the variance, so $\sigma$ (the positive square root) is the standard deviation. The density is $f(x)=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{(x-\mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right]$; the factor $1/(\sigma\sqrt{2\pi})$ is the normalising constant that makes total area equal to 1, and the exponent penalises distance from $\mu$ quadratically — hence symmetry and a single peak.

Why does the z-score work? Define $Z=\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}$. Substituting $X=\mu+\sigma Z$ into the density produces the standard normal $N(0,1)$: the location and scale parameters cancel, leaving a distribution free of $\mu$ and $\sigma$. Therefore every normal question becomes a table lookup. The empirical 68–95–99.7 rule follows from integrating the standard density over $\pm1,\pm2,\pm3$. Table identities: $\Phi(-z)=1-\Phi(z)$, $P(Z>z)=1-\Phi(z)$, $P(-z<Z<z)=2\Phi(z)-1$, where $\Phi(z)=P(Z\le z)$.

The Central Limit Theorem explains why the normal appears so often: the mean of many independent contributions is approximately normal regardless of the original distribution — a physical statement about summed noise, not a biological coincidence.

Zero-assumption ledger

$X$ = normal random variable. $\mu$ = population mean. $\sigma^2$ = population variance; $\sigma$ = standard deviation. $Z$ = standard normal variable, $Z\sim N(0,1)$. $z=(x-\mu)/\sigma$ = z-score. $\Phi(z)$ = standard normal CDF. $\pi\approx3.14159$; $\exp$ = exponential function. $f(x)$ = density. $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$ notation: the second entry is the variance, not the SD.

Quantity and unit accounting

The z-score is dimensionless: $(x-\mu)$ has the units of $x$ (mmHg), $\sigma$ has the same units, so the ratio is pure. In the worked example $x=110$ mmHg, $\mu=128$ mmHg, $\sigma=12$ mmHg, giving $z=-18/12=-1.5$ — a pure number. Probabilities have no units. Misreading $N(128,12)$ as variance 12 (instead of SD 12) changes $z$ to $-18/\sqrt{12}\approx-5.20$ and the answer drastically — the single commonest notation error.

Six-layer check (concepts: normal density; z-standardisation; empirical rule; CLT)

  • Mechanistic. Normalising constant, quadratic exponent, z-transformation cancellation, empirical rule and CLT explained above.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Gauss and Laplace developed the normal law in the early 19th century; Karl Pearson later called it "normal". Unresolved: real biomedical data often have heavier tails than the normal, so normality should be checked rather than assumed.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] Reference intervals are commonly set as $\mu\pm2\sigma$ (about 95 % of healthy values), which assumes normality and a stable analytical CV. Levey–Jennings QC charts assume normal run-to-run variation; a run beyond 2SD triggers a rule, beyond 3SD a failure. Outliers may be analytical or biological.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] Growth charts express height as a z-score or centile relative to a reference population; blood pressure thresholds are percentiles of risk, not of a normal distribution. A z-score of 1.5 on a diagnostic test result corresponds to roughly the top 7 % of healthy values — the basis of "borderline abnormal" flags.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] Bioequivalence and assay validation use normal-theory intervals for ratio-scale PK parameters; the common 90 % confidence interval for relative bioavailability is a direct z/t-based construction. Sample-size planning for trials assumes a normal approximation for proportions when $np$ and $n(1-p)$ are large.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: robust alternatives for heavy-tailed data, and validating normality assumptions per analyte rather than by tradition.

Transfer check

Work backward as well as forward. Forward: $x=110$, $\mu=128$, $\sigma=12$ → $z=-1.5$ → probability 0.0668. Backward: if only 5 % of a population exceeds a value, solve $\Phi^{-1}(0.95)=1.6449$ and compute $x=\mu+z\sigma$ — for heights $N(170,64)$ cm, $\sigma=8$ and $x=170+1.6449(8)\approx183.16$ cm (Python: 170+1.64498=183.1592). Boundary checks: the second parameter is variance, so $N(170,64)$ has SD 8, not 64; the total area is 1, so $P(Z>z)=1-\Phi(z)$; symmetry gives $\Phi(-z)=1-\Phi(z)$. A transfer question: newborn weights $N(3.2,0.4^2)$ kg — what fraction lies between 2.8 and 3.6? These are $\mu\pm\sigma$, so by the empirical rule about 68 %; exact calculation gives $2\Phi(1)-1=2(0.8413)-1=0.6826$ (Python: scipy.stats.norm.cdf(1)-scipy.stats.norm.cdf(-1)=0.682689...). A clinical caution: a z-score tells you how unusual a value is relative to the reference distribution chosen*; using the wrong reference population (age, sex, ethnicity) produces a correct number with a wrong meaning.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: BP readings are $N(128,12^2)$ mmHg. Find $P(\text{BP}<110)$, given $\Phi(1.50)=0.9332$.

Step 1 — standardise. $z=\frac{110-128}{12}=\frac{-18}{12}=-1.5$.

Step 2 — use symmetry. $P(Z<-1.5)=\Phi(-1.5)=1-\Phi(1.5)=1-0.9332=0.0668$.

Step 3 — check the notation. $N(128,12^2)$ means variance 144 and SD 12; using SD 12 is correct.

Step 4 — independent check. Python scipy.stats.norm.cdf(-1.5) returns approximately 0.0668072, matching 0.0668.

Answer: $P(\text{BP}<110)=0.0668$ (about 6.7 %).

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Normal and log-normal models describe assay noise and biological variability; transformed data may be normal even when raw data are not.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Process capability and control limits use the normal model; non-normal processes need transformations or percentiles.
  • Computational/AI. scipy.stats.norm.cdf/ppf computes probabilities and quantiles; z-scores are a standard preprocessing step.
  • Evolutionary-Design. The normal survived because summing many small independent effects produces it — a mathematical attractor, not a biological choice.
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Growth-chart and reference-range products monetise percentile interpretation.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. Second parameter is variance. $z$ is dimensionless. Normality is an assumption to check. Connects to confidence intervals and hypothesis tests.

Source: STA__KNUST_Stats_Exam_100Q_pdf.txt (Q66–Q80 and solutions p.17); STA__Statistical_Methods_5_Med_Sch_2_pdf.txt (slides "The Normal Distribution" p.22–27, "Reference Ranges" p.25, "Clinical Examples" p.28–31); STA__geo_ma_hg_basic_statistics_self_test_pdf.txt (Q20–Q25).

Standard error and confidence intervals

Locate it (biological geography)

From the sample upward: a laboratory measures 36 patients, obtains a mean, and wants to state a range for the true population mean. The standard error measures how much the sample mean would wobble across repeated samples; the confidence interval turns that wobble into a stated range. Input: sample mean, population SD and sample size; output: an interval with a stated confidence level. The care step is interpretation — the interval is about the procedure, not a probability attached to a fixed constant.

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

For independent observations $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ each with variance $\sigma^2$, the variance of their mean is derived by expanding: $$\operatorname{Var}\!\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i\right)=\frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Var}(X_i)=\frac{n\sigma^2}{n^2}=\frac{\sigma^2}{n},$$ because independence makes all cross-terms zero. Hence the standard error $SE=\sigma/\sqrt n$ — the standard deviation of the sampling distribution of $\bar{x}$. By the Central Limit Theorem, $(\bar{x}-\mu)/SE$ is approximately standard normal for large $n$, so $P(-1.96<Z<1.96)\approx0.95$ gives $$\bar{x}\pm1.96\,SE$$ as an approximate 95 % confidence interval for $\mu$. The multiplier 1.96 is the 97.5th percentile of the standard normal, leaving 2.5 % in each tail.

Interpretation: if we repeated sampling and built intervals this way 100 times, about 95 would contain the fixed true $\mu$. For a particular computed interval, $\mu$ is either inside or outside — it is not random. When $\sigma$ is unknown and $n$ small, replace 1.96 with a $t$-critical value having $n-1$ degrees of freedom.

Zero-assumption ledger

$X_i$ = $i$th independent observation. $\bar{x}$ = sample mean. $\mu$ = population mean. $\sigma$ = population SD. $n$ = sample size. $SE=\sigma/\sqrt n$ = standard error. $Z$ = standard normal variable. 1.96 = two-sided 95 % normal critical value. $t$ = Student's t variable; $df=n-1$ = degrees of freedom. $\operatorname{Var}$ = variance.

Quantity and unit accounting

If $\sigma=12$ mmHg and $n=36$, then $SE=12/\sqrt{36}=12/6=2$ mmHg — the SD shrinks by the square root of the count, not by the count itself. The interval $128\pm1.96(2)=128\pm3.92$ mmHg has the same units as the mean. Accounting check: doubling $n$ to 144 gives $SE=1$ mmHg and interval width $\pm1.96$ mmHg — half as wide, not a quarter; this square-root law is the quantitative heart of sample-size planning.

Six-layer check (concepts: sampling distribution; standard error; confidence interval; t correction)

  • Mechanistic. Variance-of-mean derivation, CLT-based multiplier, and frequentist interpretation given above.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Jerzy Neyman formalised confidence intervals in 1937; Ronald Fisher's fiducial intervals were a different, competing concept. Unresolved: the persistent misinterpretation of a 95 % CI as "95 % probability the true value is in this interval"; bootstrap intervals offer a non-parametric alternative.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] Replicate measurements quantify precision; the mean of replicates has a smaller SE than a single result. QC estimates analytical SD from control data, then sets control limits at $\pm2SD$ or $\pm3SD$. Sources of error: non-independence between replicates (same calibration, same run) breaks the $1/\sqrt n$ reduction.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] A trial reporting a mean blood-pressure reduction should give a confidence interval, not just a $p$-value, so the reader sees the plausible range of effect sizes. Reference intervals for a healthy population are themselves estimated with uncertainty.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] Bioequivalence is assessed with 90 % confidence intervals for the ratio of geometric means of AUC and $C_{\max}$, commonly within 80–125 % for many jurisdictions — a direct confidence-interval application. Assay precision is expressed as %CV, related to the SE through replication.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: confidence intervals for non-normal, skewed or correlated data; Bayesian credible intervals as an alternative framing.

Transfer check

Three questions test real understanding. First: what happens when $n$ quadruples? $SE$ halves and the interval halves in width — Python: for $n=36$, width $=2(1.96)(2)=7.84$; for $n=144$, width $=2(1.96)(1)=3.92$. Second: what if the data are skewed? The normal-based interval may have poor coverage; a log transform or bootstrap is preferred, and the back-transform must be handled carefully. Third: what does "95 %" refer to? The repeated-sampling performance of the method, not a probability about this fixed interval — the self-test's answer "there is a 95 % chance the population mean falls within the limits" reflects the common loose wording, but the rigorous reading is about repeated intervals. Boundary checks: $SE$ divides by $\sqrt n$, never by $n$; if $\sigma$ is unknown, the $t$ multiplier replaces 1.96 and is larger for small $n$; increasing confidence from 95 % to 99 % widens the interval because the critical value rises from 1.96 to about 2.576. A transfer question: duplicate measurements from the same patient are not independent, so treating them as extra $n$ shrinks $SE$ falsely — a design error that no formula repairs.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: A sample of $n=36$ patients has mean BP $\bar{x}=128$ mmHg; population SD $\sigma=12$ mmHg. Compute the approximate 95 % confidence interval for $\mu$.

Step 1 — standard error. $SE=12/\sqrt{36}=12/6=2$ mmHg.

Step 2 — margin. $1.96\times2=3.92$ mmHg.

Step 3 — interval. $128\pm3.92=(124.08,\ 131.92)$ mmHg.

Step 4 — independent check. Python: 12/36**0.5=2.0; 128-1.962=124.08; 128+1.962=131.92. The width 7.84 equals $2\times1.96\times2$.

Answer: $SE=2$ mmHg; approximate 95 % CI $=(124.08,\ 131.92)$ mmHg.

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Replicate assays report means with uncertainty; SE quantifies how much replication improves precision.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Sampling plans and capability studies use SE to set decision thresholds.
  • Computational/AI. Bootstrap resampling estimates SE without a formula; scipy.stats.t.interval handles unknown $\sigma$.
  • Evolutionary-Design. The square-root shrinkage survived because it reflects a real physical fact: independent errors partially cancel, but slowly.
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Trial-reporting and assay-validation products standardise interval reporting.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. SE is the SD of the mean, not of individuals. $1/\sqrt n$, not $1/n$. Connects to CLT and hypothesis tests.

Source: STA__geo_ma_hg_basic_statistics_self_test_pdf.txt (Q27 on standard error, Q32 on the t distribution, Q35–Q36 on confidence intervals); STA__KNUST_Stats_Exam_100Q_pdf.txt (Q69 for the BP parameters); STA__Statistical_Methods_5_Med_Sch_2_pdf.txt (slide "Sample means approach normality", p.22).

Hypothesis testing

Locate it (biological geography)

From the trial downward: a sample shows a mean difference; the question is whether it reflects a real effect or sampling noise. A hypothesis test formalises that decision. Input: a null claim, a test statistic and a significance level; output: reject or fail to reject, with a quantified error rate. The clinical stakes are explicit: approving an ineffective drug (Type I) or missing an effective one (Type II).

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

Set $H_0$ (null: no effect, mean equals $\mu_0$) and $H_1$ (alternative). Assume $H_0$ true; then the statistic $$z=\frac{\bar{x}-\mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt n}$$ follows (approximately) the standard normal, because it is the standardised distance between the observed mean and the claimed mean. The critical region is the set of $z$ values too extreme to be plausible under $H_0$; choose significance level $\alpha$ (commonly 0.05), giving two-sided critical values $\pm1.96$. Equivalently, the p-value $P(\text{data at least this extreme}\mid H_0)$ is computed; reject when $p<\alpha$.

The logic is proof by contradiction under a probability model: if $H_0$ were true, data this extreme would be rare; therefore either a rare event occurred or $H_0$ is wrong. Rejecting $H_0$ when true is a Type I error with probability $\alpha$. Failing to reject when $H_1$ is true is a Type II error with probability $\beta$; power $=1-\beta$. For a mean with unknown $\sigma$, use $t=\frac{\bar{x}-\mu_0}{s/\sqrt n}$ with $df=n-1$.

Zero-assumption ledger

$H_0$ = null hypothesis; $H_1$ = alternative. $\mu_0$ = mean claimed under $H_0$. $\bar{x}$ = observed sample mean. $\sigma$ = population SD (known case); $s$ = sample SD (unknown case). $n$ = sample size. $z,t$ = test statistics. $\alpha$ = significance level. $p$ = p-value. $\beta$ = Type II error probability; $1-\beta$ = power. $df=n-1$ = degrees of freedom.

Quantity and unit accounting

The numerator $(\bar{x}-\mu_0)$ is in mmHg; the denominator $\sigma/\sqrt n$ is in mmHg; $z$ is dimensionless — a unit check confirming the statistic is comparable to table values. In the worked example, $\sigma=15$ mmHg, $n=36$, so $SE=2.5$ mmHg and $z=8/2.5=3.2$. If the denominator were wrongly taken as $\sigma=15$ (no $\sqrt n$), $z=8/15\approx0.53$ and the conclusion would flip — a calculation error with clinical consequences.

Six-layer check (concepts: null/alternative; test statistic; p-value and errors; t test)

  • Mechanistic. Sampling distribution of the standardised mean, critical region, p-value and error rates derived above.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Ronald Fisher promoted p-values; Neyman and Pearson framed testing as a decision rule with prespecified error rates — two traditions often blended inconsistently. Unresolved: multiple testing inflates false positives, and $p$-value thresholds are contested; estimation intervals are often more informative.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] Method-comparison studies test whether a new assay's mean differs from the reference method; QC uses chi-square and t-based checks for bias between lots. Sources of error: non-independence of replicates and a reference method with its own uncertainty.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] A trial asks whether a treatment changes outcome; the RECOVERY trial's mortality result is a named example of a randomised test of a null hypothesis of no difference. A statistically significant result is not automatically clinically important — effect size matters.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] Regulatory trials prespecify $\alpha$, power and sample size; dexamethasone in RECOVERY is a named drug–outcome pair. Bioequivalence uses a different logic (two one-sided tests) rather than a simple difference test.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: multiplicity correction across many biomarkers, adaptive designs, and Bayesian alternatives that report posterior probabilities instead of p-values.

Transfer check

Connect the test to the interval: a two-sided 5 % test rejects $H_0:\mu=\mu_0$ exactly when $\mu_0$ lies outside the 95 % confidence interval. In the worked example the interval would be $128\pm1.96(2.5)=(123.1,132.9)$ mmHg; the claimed 120 lies outside, consistent with rejection (Python: 128-1.962.5=123.1, 128+1.962.5=132.9). That equivalence is the deepest check available — if a test and interval disagree, one calculation is wrong. Boundary checks: $\alpha$ is set before seeing data; $p$ is not $P(H_0\mid\text{data})$; a non-significant result is not proof of no effect (low power may be the cause). A transfer question: testing 20 biomarkers at $\alpha=0.05$ each gives roughly a $1-0.95^{20}\approx0.64$ chance of at least one false positive — Python: 1-0.95**20=0.6415. Hence multiplicity correction (e.g. Bonferroni, dividing $\alpha$ by the number of tests) is not optional. Another: a clinically trivial difference can be statistically significant with huge $n$ — always report the effect size and interval alongside the decision.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: Reference systolic BP is $N(120,15^2)$ mmHg. A sample of $n=36$ has mean $\bar{x}=128$. Test at 5 % whether the mean differs from 120.

Step 1 — hypotheses. $H_0:\mu=120$; $H_1:\mu\ne120$ (two-sided).

Step 2 — standard error. $SE=15/\sqrt{36}=15/6=2.5$ mmHg.

Step 3 — statistic. $z=(128-120)/2.5=8/2.5=3.2$.

Step 4 — decision. $3.2>1.96$, so the statistic is in the critical region; reject $H_0$. Python 2*(1-scipy.stats.norm.cdf(3.2)) ≈ 0.0014 < 0.05, confirming rejection.

Answer: $z=3.2$; reject $H_0$ — the mean is significantly above 120 mmHg at the 5 % level.

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Differential-expression testing applies the same logic across thousands of genes; multiplicity correction is essential.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Lot-release tests compare measured means to specifications with prespecified risk.
  • Computational/AI. scipy.stats.ttest_ind and permutation tests implement the comparison; permutation tests avoid normality assumptions.
  • Evolutionary-Design. Hypothesis testing survived because decisions require an explicit, auditable error rate — a formalisation of "how sure is sure enough".
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Trial platforms and A/B testing products monetise controlled decisions; misapplied testing is a liability.
  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. Fail to reject ≠ accept $H_0$. $p$ is not $P(H_0\mid\text{data})$. Connects to confidence intervals and chi-square.

Source: STA__geo_ma_hg_basic_statistics_self_test_pdf.txt (Q28, Q29, Q31, Q34); STA__Statistical_Methods_5_Med_Sch_2_pdf.txt (slide "Adult systolic BP: X ~ N(120, 15²)", p.23).

Chi-square tests and association

Locate it (biological geography)

From the clinical question down: is treatment type related to improvement? Both variables are categorical, so means and variances are unavailable. The chi-square test compares observed cell counts with counts expected if the variables were independent. Input: a contingency table of counts; output: a statistic and a decision about association. Its diagnostic analogue is a 2×2 table of test result versus disease.

The problem it solves, derived from first principles (engineering/physics-first)

Under independence, the probability of a cell in row $i$, column $j$ is $P_i P_j$, so the expected count is $$E_{ij}=\frac{(\text{row }i\text{ total})(\text{column }j\text{ total})}{\text{grand total}}.$$ This is derived from the multiplication rule for independent events, with probabilities estimated by observed proportions. The statistic $$\chi^2=\sum_{i,j}\frac{(O_{ij}-E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}$$ sums standardised squared discrepancies over all cells; dividing by $E_{ij}$ accounts for the fact that larger cells have larger natural variability (Poisson-like variance equals the mean). Under $H_0$ of independence and adequate sample size, this sum follows a chi-square distribution with $df=(r-1)(c-1)$ for $r$ rows and $c$ columns — because once row and column totals are fixed, only $(r-1)(c-1)$ cell counts are free. A large $\chi^2$ means observed counts are too far from independence to attribute to chance.

Zero-assumption ledger

$O_{ij}$ = observed count in cell $(i,j)$. $E_{ij}$ = expected count under independence. $r$ = number of rows; $c$ = number of columns. $df=(r-1)(c-1)$ = degrees of freedom. $\chi^2$ = chi-square statistic. $H_0$ = "the two variables are independent". $n$ = grand total. Cramér's $V$ = effect-size measure; $r_{\text{corr}}$ = Pearson correlation for numeric variables; $R^2$ = coefficient of determination.

Quantity and unit accounting

Expected counts must use the row and column totals from the same table: for a 2×2 table with row totals 60/40 and column totals 60/40 out of 100, the four expected counts are $60\!\times\!60/100=36$, $60\!\times\!40/100=24$, $40\!\times\!60/100=24$, $40\!\times\!40/100=16$, summing to $100=n$ — an accounting check that must pass. The statistic is dimensionless because numerator and denominator are counts² and counts. A rule of thumb: expected counts below about 5 make the approximation unreliable; use Fisher's exact test instead.

Six-layer check (concepts: expected counts; chi-square statistic; degrees of freedom; effect size)

  • Mechanistic. Expected count from independence multiplication, variance normalisation by $E$, and df from free cells — derived above.
  • Historical/Research. [context] Karl Pearson introduced the chi-square test in 1900. Unresolved: the test detects association but not strength or causal direction; Cramér's $V$ supplies strength, and study design is needed for direction.
  • MLS/Diagnostic. [inference] A 2×2 table of test result versus disease gives sensitivity/specificity; a chi-square test asks whether test and disease are independent (it should not be if the test works). Misclassification and spectrum bias alter cell counts; small expected counts violate the approximation.
  • Clinical/Medical. [context] Case–control studies cross-tabulate exposure and outcome; interpreting the association requires attention to confounding, which the chi-square test does not remove. Blood group versus disease status is a nominal–nominal question suited to this test.
  • Pharmaceutical. [context] Adverse-event tables (drug vs placebo × event vs no event) are commonly summarised with a 2×2 table; the chi-square test is a first check, with Fisher's exact test for small counts and logistic regression for adjustment.
  • Research/Frontier. Open questions: stratified tables (Mantel–Haenszel methods), sparse categorical data, and distinguishing association from causation.

Worked example — Python-verified

Question: 100 patients: 60 received a drug, 40 placebo; 60 improved, 40 did not. Observed: drug/improved 40, drug/not 20, placebo/improved 20, placebo/not 20. Test independence at 5 % ($df=1$ critical value 3.841).

Step 1 — expected counts. Drug/improved $60\times60/100=36$; drug/not $60\times40/100=24$; placebo/improved $40\times60/100=24$; placebo/not $40\times40/100=16$. Sum $=100$.

Step 2 — contributions. $(40-36)^2/36=16/36\approx0.444$; $(20-24)^2/24\approx0.667$; $(20-24)^2/24\approx0.667$; $(20-16)^2/16=1.000$.

Step 3 — total. $\chi^2\approx0.444+0.667+0.667+1.000=2.778$.

Step 4 — decision and check. $2.778<3.841$, so fail to reject independence. Python scipy.stats.chi2_contingency([[40,20],[20,20]], correction=False) returns $\chi^2\approx2.7778$, $p\approx0.0957>0.05$.

Answer: $\chi^2\approx2.78$; no significant association at the 5 % level.

End-of-section synthesis

  • Biotechnology. Genome-wide association studies test categorical and allele-count associations; multiple-testing correction dominates.
  • Manufacturing/Industrial. Defect type versus machine is a contingency table; chi-square screens for association before process adjustment.
  • Computational/AI. scipy.stats.chi2_contingency computes the test; Yates correction is applied by default for 2×2 tables (omitted above to match the hand calculation).
  • Evolutionary-Design. The chi-square survived because comparing expected with observed is a universal scientific operation: "did the model predict the counts?"
  • Innovation/Entrepreneurship. Clinical data platforms automate contingency analysis; correct use requires guarding against confounding and multiple testing.

Transfer check

Connect the chi-square result back to conditional probabilities. In the worked table, $P(\text{improved}\mid\text{drug})=40/60\approx0.667$ and $P(\text{improved}\mid\text{placebo})=20/40=0.5$; the observed difference is 0.167, yet the chi-square test does not reach significance because the sample is small. Python check: scipy.stats.chi2_contingency([[40,20],[20,20]], correction=False)[1]≈0.0957, so the difference is compatible with chance. Boundary checks: expected counts must sum to the grand total (36+24+24+16=100 ✓); each expected count should be at least about 5; df for 2×2 is $(2-1)(2-1)=1$. A transfer question: if the observed table were [[45,15],[15,25]], expected counts stay the same but $\chi^2$ becomes 14.0625 (Python: chi2_contingency([[45,15],[15,25]], correction=False)[0]=14.0625, $p\approx0.00018$), exceeding 3.841 — the association becomes significant. The lesson is that the same totals with different cell allocation changes the conclusion, which is exactly what the statistic measures. Association is still not causation: randomisation or adjustment is needed for that claim.

  • Misconceptions / memory hook / connections. Association is not causation. Expected counts come from row × column / total. Small cells → Fisher's exact test. Connects to probability, conditional probability and hypothesis testing.

The 2×2 table is a constructed example using the method from the self-test; no counts were taken from a real study.
